#10357. What to get right before you ship webhooks

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

OpenAI, Anthropic, Gemini, and Cursor are all sending webhooks now, and all four built them on Standard Webhooks, the open spec written by Svix. Downloads of the spec's npm package went from 1.7 million a month in September 2025 to 220 million a month currently.

This may be boring to hear for the nth time, but the reason is agents. An individual person checks on a running job a few times. An agent told to watch for changes checks every resource, every minute, for as long as it runs, and almost every one of those requests comes back with nothing new. An agent that starts a job, polls until it finishes, and then starts the next one spends most of its time waiting: six steps with 15 seconds of polling each add 90 seconds, even when every operation is fast. Polling more often adds load, and polling less often adds delay, so there's not really a sweet spot.

An event-driven API gets rid of that trade-off. It tells consumers when something changed or finished, so they stop asking. That matters most for work that runs for minutes or hours, like batch inference, fine-tuning, and video generation, and many APIs still don't offer it. Of the 57 AI APIs with long-running jobs that Svix reviewed for its State of Webhooks 2027 report, 19 make you poll for the result.

Webhooks are how most APIs push those events, and you can get a v1 out pretty fast. Making it dependable is an infrastructure problem, which is a class of problem that requires more time by default. Before you ship webhooks, you need an append-only event log behind every delivery, a fixed event format, signed payloads, retries with backoff, event IDs receivers can deduplicate on, per-endpoint isolation, and delivery logs your customers can read. If you skip one of these things, a customer is just going to bring it up later.

Each section below covers one of them with adaptable code, and Standard Webhooks works with existing verification libraries in most languages.

If the request handler that changes the data also sends the webhook, a crash between the commit and the HTTP call loses the event, and nothing records that it should have gone out. A customer whose endpoint was down for longer than your retry window has no way to find out what they missed.

Both problems go away if you write each event to an append-only table, scoped per customer, in the same transaction as the change. Webhooks are delivered from that table, and the same table backs an HTTP endpoint customers can read from a cursor. Svix calls this design POKE (Publish Once, Kickoff Everywhere). Build the log first: a consumer who misses a webhook needs somewhere to resume from.

Every code path that changes state calls one helper inside its own transaction:

Pass the helper the transaction handle. An insert through the connection pool commits separately, so you can end up with the event and no change, or the change and no event.

The advisory lock prevents a subtler bug. Postgres assigns sequence values at insert time, but rows only become visible at commit. With two concurrent writers, a reader can see ID 102, store it as its cursor, and then never see ID 101 when the slower transaction commits. Holding a per-customer lock until commit makes each customer's events visible in ID order.

Reading the log is one indexed range query. The endpoint returns a cursor even when there are no new events, so a client can always store it and ask again later:

authenticate stands in for the middleware that already protects your API and sets req.customerId. That scoping stops one customer from reading another's events. A customer who was offline for a week asks for events after their last cursor and gets everything they missed, in order. Publish your retention period, since it limits how far back that works. The same log can also serve synthetic created events for existing records when a new integration needs to load its initial state.

Customers write handlers against your payloads, so you'll be supporting every field you ship.

Give each event an id, type, timestamp, and data. Use the log row's ID as the event ID so webhooks and the pull endpoint agree. Name types as a resource plus a past-tense action, like invoice.paid, and publish a catalog with an example payload for each type.

Emit an event for each state change a consumer would otherwise poll for. Deletions are the ones teams forget most. Long-running jobs need completion and failure events, such as export.completed and export.failed.

Adding a field is safe. Removing or renaming one breaks handlers, so ship a new type such as invoice.paid.v2 and keep sending the old one until customers have moved over.

Anyone who finds a customer's webhook URL can POST a fake invoice.paid to it. A signature lets the receiver confirm the request came from you, wasn't modified in transit, and isn't an old request being replayed.

Standard Webhooks signs the string id.timestamp.body with HMAC-SHA256, using a separate secret for each endpoint. Including the timestamp is what lets receivers reject old requests. Generate secrets with "whsec_" + crypto.randomBytes(24).toString("base64").

Send the result in three headers. The ID comes from the log and stays the same on every retry. The timestamp is the time of the current attempt, so each retry gets a new signature.

Receivers need the matching check, so put it in your docs:

The function takes the raw body because frameworks that parse JSON and serialize it again change the bytes, and the signature stops matching. It compares with crypto.timingSafeEqual, since === returns faster on an early mismatch and leaks timing information. It rejects requests more than five minutes old to block replays. The signature header is a space-separated list so that during a secret rotation you can send one signature per secret and the receiver accepts either.

That code shows how the scheme works. In production, use a Standard Webhooks library on both ends. It implements the same algorithm:

Receiving endpoints go down during deploys and outages, and they return errors when they're rate limited. If you only try once, each of those is a lost webhook.

Count any non-2xx response as a failure, redirects included. Following a redirect to an arbitrary host is a security problem in its own right. Count slow responses as failures too. The code below gives up after 15 seconds. Publish whatever limit you pick, because customer handlers have to respond within it.

These delays match the schedule Svix runs in production: eight attempts over a little more than 27 hours. The early retries cover restarts. The later ones give someone time to notice an outage and fix it. After each failure, pass the number of attempts so far to nextAttemptAt, and when it returns null, mark the delivery as failed. The event is still in the log, so once the customer fixes their server they can replay it or read it from the pull endpoint.

Store attempt state in a table next to the log. Timers in process memory disappear when the process restarts, and you don't need a message broker to avoid that.

Decide what happens to endpoints that never come back. Svix disables an endpoint after five days of continuous failure and emits an event so the sender can tell the customer. Pick your own threshold, notify the customer when you disable their endpoint, and let them turn it back on.

Retries mean some events arrive twice. Usually the receiver processed the event but timed out before you saw its response, so you sent it again. Senders accept this at-least-once behavior because the alternative is dropping events, and receivers handle the duplicates.

Receivers deduplicate on webhook-id, which doesn't change between retries. The insert that stores the event can also be the duplicate check:

webhook_inbox needs a text primary key id and a jsonb column payload. The handler verifies the signature, stores the event, and returns 204. A background worker processes the inbox, so the handler responds well within the sender's timeout. express.raw gives the route the unparsed body that verification needs.

Put this pattern in your docs with a note about ordering. Events in the log have a fixed order, but webhook deliveries don't always arrive in it. A retried invoice.created can land after invoice.paid. Receivers should keep whichever event has the newer updated_at, or read the pull endpoint when they need strict ordering.

With a shared worker pool, one customer's hanging endpoint can tie up every worker for the full timeout while other customers' events wait. Limit concurrent deliveries per endpoint, and slow down delivery to endpoints that keep failing.

Customer-supplied URLs are also an attack surface. Someone can register http://169.254.169.254/ or an internal hostname and get your servers to call your cloud metadata service or private network. This is server-side request forgery (SSRF). Resolve the hostname before connecting, reject private and loopback addresses, and don't follow redirects. The delivery code sets redirect: "manual" for this reason.

Without a self-serve view, every "did you send it?" question goes to an engineer who has to search logs.

Give customers a page to add and remove endpoints, choose event types, and rotate secrets. For each message, show every attempt with its timestamp, status code, and response body. The response body usually contains the customer's own error message. Add buttons to replay a failed message and to send a test event.

Expose the same operations in your API. Creating an endpoint and choosing its event types should take one authenticated request, because scripts and AI agents now set up a lot of integrations and can't click through a dashboard.

This is the biggest item here because it needs a UI, and it's the one teams most often leave out of the first release.

You can build all of this yourself, and many teams have. It comes down to the fact that it's cheap to manage delivery for 99% of your webhooks, but it's nontrivial to guarantee it for 100% of them. This is also ignoring the UX side such as offering the customer portal for your customers to manage their webhooks in.

Svix, which sponsored this article, handles webhook delivery. You keep the event log and decide what goes in it. A small relay reads new rows and passes each one to Svix:

Svix also hosts the debugging portal. This call returns a single-use link where the customer can manage endpoints, read delivery logs, and replay messages:

The free tier includes 50,000 messages a month and doesn't require a credit card.

Before the first customer registers an endpoint, you should be able to answer yes to each of these:

#10356. On-Device Speech Recognition and Text-to-Speech in Google AI Edge Gallery

Today, we are introducing on-device Automatic Speech Recognition (ASR) and Text-to-Speech (TTS) capabilities in Google AI Edge Gallery. Voice is often the most natural way to interact with AI on phones and wearables. Recent advances in compact speech architectures now make it possible to run accurate, multilingual ASR and expressive TTS models entirely on device, all within a small memory footprint, with low latency, and completely offline. By pairing these lightweight speech models with on-device small language models (SLMs) like Gemini Nano and Gemma 4, developers can build apps that stream live transcriptions as the user speaks and synthesize natural spoken replies locally.

This release brings together major advancements across both Google’s system speech models and the open-weight ecosystem:

Below, we’ll explore how this local voice pipeline powers the new XR (Extended Reality) Language Teacher showcase, upgrades AI Chat in Google AI Edge Gallery, and how you can build with these APIs and models today.

Learning a language while traveling happens in the real world, often in underground stations, busy markets, or abroad where Wi-Fi and roaming data may not be available. To show how vision, language, and speech models can work together 100% locally on your phone with no network connection needed, we built the XR Language Teacher showcase in Google AI Edge Gallery. Simply point your camera at an unfamiliar object or menu, ask "What is that, and how do I ask for one?", and immediately hear an explanation in your language alongside authentic pronunciation in the local language.

Link to Youtube Video (visible only when JS is disabled)

With inference running locally on a paired phone, the same experience works standalone on a phone and extends naturally to hands-free smart glasses. To see how we adapted Language Teacher for intelligent glasses check out our recent talk at Android Dev Summit: Building Audio-First Experiences for Intelligent Eyewear with Gemini Nano.

Beyond the XR Language Teacher showcase, we also built ASR and TTS directly into Google AI Edge Gallery’s main AI Chat feature, giving on-device SLMs like Gemini Nano and Gemma 4 a natural, offline voice interface while on the go.

Link to Youtube Video (visible only when JS is disabled)

While multimodal models like Gemma 4 can process audio inputs directly, we provide a modular solution for cascading language models with separate, lightweight voice models for latency optimization and flexibility. Depending on your target devices and model requirements, our open-source code on GitHub shows you the optimized and recommended solution for Android using ML Kit, as well as MediaPipe Tasks and LiteRT for cross-platform coverage and open model support.

For developers building for Android devices, ML Kit’s GenAI Speech Recognition and Text-to-Speech APIs provide a standardized, platform-integrated path out of the box. With the underlying speech models managed through AICore and Google’s on-device speech services, developers get production-grade quality without having to manage model downloads or worry about APK size bloat. Your app also benefits automatically from hardware acceleration across NPU and TPU backends (when available) and seamless background model updates.

Integrating ASR and TTS into cross-platform applications usually requires handling low-level audio framing, Voice Activity Detection (VAD), and complex decoding pipelines. MediaPipe Task simplifies this by providing unified, turnkey ASR and TTS APIs across iOS, macOS, Windows, Linux, and Web that abstract away manual pre-processing, audio stream routing, and platform-specific audio management into simple, high-level interfaces:

Under the hood, ML Kit and MediaPipe Tasks are powered by LiteRT, Google’s high-performance on-device AI inference infrastructure. Cross-platform applications requiring even more fine-grained control over speech processing or custom model architectures can also build on it directly.

Speech models often rely on multiple subgraphs and auxiliary assets, such as encoder/decoder subgraphs and tokenizers for ASR, or phonemizer dictionaries, voice embeddings, and vocoders for TTS. All required components for each model are packaged into a single .litertlm file for easy deployment. The app ships with ready-to-use .litertlm models, including Parakeet-TDT-0.6B-v3 (multilingual ASR), PocketTTS 100M (multilingual TTS), Phonon 31M (ultra-lightweight English TTS), and Kokoro-82M (multilingual TTS). Beyond these, the vibrant Hugging Face LiteRT Community with over 10K+ followers offers a growing catalog of open-weight models, like Whisper, Qwen3-ASR and Moonshine for ASR, or Qwen3-TTS and Matcha for TTS. For developers seeking custom implementations or support for additional open-weight voice models, LiteRT provides a rich set of multimodal model examples to help you get started, with more on the way.

Adding a local voice pipeline takes on-device SLMs beyond typing-based chat interfaces, enabling natural, voice-first interactions that work anywhere, completely offline. All the building blocks to try these experiences or integrate them into your own apps are available today:

We'd like to extend a special thanks to our significant contributors for their work on this project:

#10355. Government may apply TV license fee to streaming services to fund the BBC

Controversial move could save the BBC by bringing in more than a billion in extra funding each year

A study suggests extending the TV license to cover streaming services such as YouTube and Netflix could solve the BBC’s funding problems, netting it an extra £1.6 billion ($2 billion) per year by 2032.

The British government is due to publish a white paper by the end of this year outlining its plans for the BBC's license fee and Royal Charter, which expires at the end of 2027.

One of the options is to extend the license so that it is needed to access streaming platforms like Netflix, Disney+, or Amazon Prime Video.

This is unlikely to prove popular, but if the fee was held at the current level (£180, $236 per year) it would generate up to £1.6 billion extra a year for the corporation and would better reflect how audiences watch television, according to entertainment industry research biz Ampere Analysis.

As any Brit knows, the BBC is funded primarily through the TV license, which is charged on a per household basis. A license is required to watch or record live TV on any broadcast channel, or access the BBC iPlayer for on-demand content. A license is not currently needed for on-demand streaming platforms.

The government is understood to believe the current license scheme is “outdated and no longer fit for purpose,” and is weighing several options to reform it. Scrapping it entirely looks unlikely for now: a committee found that other European countries that did so ended up with less stable funding for their public service broadcasters, and weaker independence from political interference.

TV licensing is controversial, with some people resenting having to pay the BBC just for the right to watch television. In response, the government has tried to limit fee increases, and made the license free for some elderly people. Together, these moved have reduced the BBC's funding, leading to service cuts and further. dissatisfaction.

Responding to a report by the National Audit Office (NAO) that found the BBC will remain under financial pressure despite widespread cuts, the National Union of Journalists (NUJ) urged the government to secure the broadcaster's future in the next charter renewal.

"The BBC shouldn't be in this position. These cuts are the result of a decade of underfunding, with NAO identifying a 26 percent real-terms reduction in license fee income in the last nine years alone," said NUJ general secretary Laura Davison.

Many Brits no longer pay the license fee, preferring to watch only streamed content, or claiming that they do. Ampere says more than 90 percent of UK households watch live television, but only 84 percent of eligible households pay the fee, meaning a sizable number are freeloading.  Extending the fee to cover on-demand and streaming services would close the loophole, it says.

Another option could see the license fee reduced to £150 ($196) in 2028, assuming perfect enforcement, analysts at Ampere say. They neglect to detail what this "perfect enforcement" mechanism consists of.

"Widening the remit of the license fee at current prices would significantly increase the BBC’s funding, strengthening its long-term financial position and ability to compete with local and global rivals," said Ampere Senior Analyst Sam Khoury.

"Alternatively, in the context of ongoing cost of living pressures in the UK, the government could opt to reduce the price of the fee payable by the extended pool of consumers. Coupled with a reformed enforcement mechanism that effectively reduces evasion, this would still improve the BBC's financial outlook relative to the status quo."

Earlier this year, the government revealed that it was planning for a switch-off of the broadcast digital terrestrial television (DTT) service in the future.

This is again because of the rapidly changing nature of the TV landscape, with viewers shifting to online streaming, creating a hybrid landscape between IPTV, traditional DTT, satellite broadcasting, and cable.

Current licenses for DTT services expire at the end of December, 2034, but the government may choose to extend them to 2044. ®

#10350. Storage is expensive right now, but these deals are worth it if you’re desperate for space

We can blame AI for many things, least of all the steep increase in the cost of integral storage and memory we need for PCs, Macs, phones, and consoles.

I don’t know if you’re aware – I really hope you are – but prices for SSDs, USB storage, SD cards, and yet more important storage components are exponentially higher than ever before. If, like me, you need these to keep a camera running, or maybe store important files, you’ll know the pain of buying any type of storage right now.

Thanks to Anthropic, OpenAI, Google, and more, we’re going to be stuck with shockingly bad prices for a while to come.

I’ve tried tracking some of the best prices, and while some are still eye-watering, brands like Lexar, PNY, SanDisk, and even Samsung have discounted some of the most popular storage and memory options. I’ve been tracking a few for a while, and sadly, we’re not going to get insane savings, but some are up to 50% off in very rare cases.

Another thing I would advise is to avoid going for huge drives at this stage unless you desperately need it. A 4TB NVMe SSD is going to be steep, and the price per GB starts to get ridiculous. The best deals start to sour once you get past the 1TB range. I wouldn’t recommend an HDD given the speed discrepancies, but maybe for long-term storage you could pick up a larger, slower drive and back up the important content in your life.

My personal pick for quick, easily accessible storage is the 128GB SanDisk dual USB thumb drive. I’ve used one for several years to transfer files from my phones to PCs and Macs. Fits in your pocket, and although it’s not the fastest, it’s just useful to have a sizable storage device on hand wherever you are. I used to move installation files between my devices, but I’ve even used it as a boot drive when setting up Linux distros on my PCs. It works with Android phones and iPhones too because of the USB-C port. I can’t recommend that one highly enough.

#10348. Google says it has fixed Gemini’s RAM usage error in Chrome

Google has fixed an issue that caused excessive RAM use by Gemini for Chrome users.

AI tools are infamous for RAM use, but that’s generally not on your local machine. A bug with Gemini, though, left some Chrome users noticing that the tool was eating up several gigabytes of memory unexpectedly.

Google, in a comment, says this issue has been fixed:

This has now been resolved, so please let us know if you are still experiencing any issues with high memory usage in Chrome.

Typically speaking, Gemini’s web app shouldn’t be using more than a few hundred megabytes of memory at any given time, maybe around 1GB if you’re on a particularly long chat session. But some users were reporting upwards of 5GB or more in what was clearly an error.

#10347. How to Add AI Facial Expression Editing to Your Video Pipeline (2026)

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Product teams keep asking engineers for the same feature: “Can users fix the reaction in this clip without reshooting it?” It lands on the backlog as “add expression editing,” and it hides a lot of decisions.

This guide compares the realistic integration options in 2026, sketches a pipeline architecture, models costs, includes a small runnable job helper, and covers the consent and audit logging you need before changing a real person’s face. Vendor details change quickly, so check each vendor’s documentation before you build against it.

Information about features, pricing, APIs and policies in this article was checked on November 10, 2026 and may change. Check each vendor’s documentation before you build against it.

Performance transfer is great for animation, but someone has to record a new take for every change. If your promise is “fix the reaction in the footage you already have,” direct editing fits better.

DRAMA is an AI facial changer from SyncMonster. In the web app, users upload a video or image (MP4, MOV, JPG, PNG), pick a face, choose an emotion, and set intensity on a slider from subtle to strong. The app offers four core emotions (Happy, Sad, Angry, Excited) plus presets such as Surprised, Serious, Evil and Intimidating, with the extra presets on paid plans. It supports emotion blending and editing one face in a group shot without touching the others. SyncMonster states that edits keep the original resolution, lighting and details such as glasses and beards, which is a vendor claim you should test on your own footage.

For developers, SyncMonster publishes public API documentation for DRAMA. Here is what those docs state, as checked on November 10, 2026:

A few things to note honestly. The same generate endpoint also serves other SyncMonster features behind their own flags, so read the parameter table carefully. In the version we checked, the parameter table marks an audio input as required on generate, and the drama-only example includes one, so plan for that in your request builder. The emotion values exposed in the API are the four core emotions, and the extra app presets did not appear in the API docs. The docs describe polling only, with no webhooks mentioned, and they do not publish rate limits, an SDK, or a numeric maximum video duration (an error code exists for exceeding a duration limit).

Runway exposes Act-Two through its developer API as the character_performance endpoint. You send a character (image or video) and a driving performance video, and Act-Two transfers facial expression, and optionally body and hand motion, onto the character. Output is 24fps.

Runway’s API pricing guide lists Act-Two at 5 credits per second of output, with credits priced at $0.01 each, so $0.05 per second. Earlier documentation mentioned a 15-credit minimum per generation, and the current pricing page does not list one, so check for minimums before you budget short clips. The documentation also exposes an expression intensity setting to control how strongly the performance is applied.

The catch is the input requirement: every edit needs someone to act the new reaction. That suits character animation, but it is a heavy ask for a user who wants a slightly warmer smile in an interview clip. It also drives one character per generation.

LivePortrait is an open-source portrait animation framework from Kuaishou’s KwaiVGI group. It animates a source image or video using a driving video or a saved motion template, supports video-to-video portrait editing, and offers retargeting controls for eyes and lips.

The README sets up a Python 3.10 conda environment and expects Git and FFmpeg, with an NVIDIA GPU recommended. It also notes that Apple Silicon runs roughly 20x slower than an RTX 4090, and that Humans mode needs PYTORCH_ENABLE_MPS_FALLBACK=1 on a Mac. That matters if you were hoping to prototype on a MacBook and ship the same setup. A basic run adapted from the README looks like this:

Here -s is the source portrait (an image or video) and -d is the driving clip. Retargeting is exposed through additional options and the Gradio interface, so check the README for current names because they change between releases. Also read the repository’s license file before you plan any commercial use.

The Hugging Face Spaces demo is handy for experiments, but production means owning GPU provisioning, queueing, model updates and quality review. You still steer with a driving input rather than a “happier by 30%” control.

Rule of thumb: if expression editing is a feature rather than your product, buy. If you need on-prem processing or model-level changes, build on LivePortrait and budget for ops.

Whatever the backend, the pipeline has five stages.

Here is the shape of the worker loop. This is illustrative pseudocode only, not any vendor’s SDK or real endpoints:

The idempotency and backoff tips below are easy to get wrong, so here is a small vendor-neutral module. The key function hashes the input file together with the parameters, so a retry with the same input maps to the same job. The poller backs off exponentially up to a ceiling and gives up after a maximum wait. It uses only the Python standard library.

A short test confirms the behavior: the same input gives the same key regardless of parameter order, different parameters give a different key, and the poller returns once the state becomes done.

Credits are not a shared currency across vendors, so compare units first and money second.

For prototyping, the DRAMA web app has a free plan (60 credits, 1080p, up to 2 minutes of processing, 2 projects, watermarked output), which is enough to judge quality on your own sample footage before writing integration code. The docs we checked list API billing in credits but do not state a dollar price per API credit, so confirm that with SyncMonster before you finalize a pricing model. Do not assume web-app plan credits convert at the same rate as API credits.

Plan details are as listed by the vendor on November 10, 2026 and can change.

Changing a real person’s expression can change what viewers believe they felt, so treat this as a product requirement.

For a product feature that edits reactions in existing footage, the DRAMA API is the most direct hosted option among those compared here: face detection with thumbnails for selection, per-face edits, and emotion values you set in code, with no driving performance. Go in knowing its current limits: four emotion parameters in the API, polling instead of webhooks, and no published rate limits or SDK. Starting with the free plan on your own clips is a low-risk way to judge quality. Product details and API access for this AI expression changer are on SyncMonster’s site.

Choose the Runway API if your users want to animate characters from an acted performance. Choose LivePortrait if you need on-prem processing or model-level control and have the GPU and ops capacity. Leave Flawless DeepEditor to studio editorial teams, and Photoshop to still-image workflows.

Yes. DRAMA by SyncMonster has a hosted REST API for editing expressions in existing video using emotion values, and Runway offers Act-Two through its API for performance transfer. LivePortrait is open source and can be wrapped in your own service.

Yes, with a direct editing tool. The DRAMA API takes emotion values from 0 to 1 for happy, sad, excited and angry on a selected face. Runway Act-Two and LivePortrait both expect a driving performance or motion template.

Run face detection first, let the user pick a face, then send that face’s ID with the edit request. The DRAMA API returns a face ID and a thumbnail for each detected face for this purpose.

Per vendor docs as of November 10, 2026: the DRAMA API bills 0.5 credit per second for face detection and 1 credit per second for drama generation, while Runway’s Act-Two API bills 5 credits per second at $0.01 per credit. LivePortrait costs whatever your GPU time costs.

Yes, but its README notes Apple Silicon can be about 20x slower than an RTX 4090. An NVIDIA GPU is recommended for anything beyond experiments.

Not a public one. DeepEditor is an Avid Media Composer extension with access by application, aimed at film and TV editorial teams.

Log the requester, consent record, selected face, emotion and intensity, backend used, input and output hashes, credits used, and a timestamp, and keep the original file.

#10345. WhatsApp is adding travel eSIMs

WhatsApp is adding support for eSIMs in its app, designed for keeping you connected while traveling, though details are not fully clear.

In a brief blog post, WhatsApp says it will be adding data-only eSIM services to its app soon. Users will be able to buy service and install the eSIM directly from WhatsApp, keeping the messaging app connected in another country without incurring pricey carrier roaming fees.

WhatsApp eSIMs will use providers such as Verizon and Jio, but exact details are unclear. There’s no word on pricing yet, and the functionality is only arriving “soon,” with no specific launch date announced yet.

To make this possible, we’re working with e-SIM providers and telco partners including Jio, Smart Axiata, Smart Communications, Verizon and others, and we’ll begin rolling this out in select countries soon. You’ll see it right in the app when it’s available for you.

#10343. The world's fastest computers have a problem: Keeping the work running

I node what you did last summer: you went down when an HPC project was relying on you

Frontier, Oak Ridge National Laboratory's exascale supercomputer, has thousands of processors working together on scientific problems. It also has a surprisingly ordinary way of ruining a researcher's day: one node going down.

An uncorrectable memory error can do it, or a maintenance mishap. Scott Atchley, chief technology officer at the Oak Ridge Leadership Computing Facility, describes technicians accidentally rebooting the wrong node or risking a neighboring node's power supply while working in tightly packed racks.

The hardware fault can affect just one node, but a simulation running across thousands of processors can grind to a halt. Then comes a series of questions. How recently did the application save its progress? How much computation needs repeating? And how long before the scheduler lets the job run again?

For operators, the challenge isn't just keeping the supercomputer running. It's keeping the science moving.

On Frontier, a memory error can have an outsized audience. Atchley says uncorrectable double-bit errors in high-bandwidth memory occurred about 1.5 times a day during the supercomputer's early bring-up.

Its predecessor, Summit, saw roughly one every two weeks. This sounds like a backward step, until you count the memory. Frontier has more than 16 times as much, giving bad bits considerably more places to hide.

Memory isn't the only problem. Problematic GPU firmware can crash a node, and so can a technician rebooting the wrong one. Interconnect faults add another way for a large job to come unstuck.

In a tightly coupled simulation using the Message Passing Interface (MPI) simulation, thousands of processes, or ranks, exchange data and participate in collective operations. If one rank disappears, the others cannot carry on as though nothing happened.

Clockwork.io CEO Suresh Vasudevan describes a similar dependency in synchronized GPU training.

One failed GPU can bring a 1,024-GPU job to a pause. An underperforming GPU can be almost as troublesome: if one participant runs at 80 percent speed, others might have to spend time waiting for it.

The bigger the machine, the more ways there are for one small problem to become everybody's problem.

Since stopping every component from failing isn't an option, researchers need a way to preserve their work. That's where checkpointing comes in. The application periodically saves its state so that, when something goes wrong, the simulation can resume from an earlier point instead of starting from scratch.

The catch is that saving progress costs computing time, and doing it too often can become almost as wasteful as losing the work itself.

Vasudevan explains this with an example. If writing a checkpoint takes 30 seconds, saving every five minutes consumes roughly 10 percent of computing time. Stretch that interval to 50 minutes, and the overhead drops to about 1 percent. But a failure could wipe out considerably more work.

Atchley says the calculation depends on the job's size and expected failure rate. He uses an example of ten-hour mean time between failures for a full machine job on Frontier. Using half the machine would roughly double that interval. The Young-Daly checkpointing model helps find the balance between saving too frequently and risking too much work.

Researchers also have the scheduler to contend with. Frontier's standard batch queue limits larger jobs to 12 hours, with shorter limits for smaller jobs. Its extended queue permits jobs of up to 64 nodes to run for 24 hours.

Then there's storage. Checkpoints can be written to the Lustre-based Orion filesystem or node-local SSDs. The faster the data can be saved, the less time checkpointing steals from the actual science.

Before recovery can begin, someone has to notice the job has stalled. Vasudevan says frameworks such as PyTorch can spend several minutes waiting before a hung process is recognized. Operators then have to sift through application logs and hardware telemetry to try to identify the offending GPU.

This can mean another encounter with the scheduler, which decides when replacement resources become available. Once allocated, the application still needs to load its libraries, initialize the runtime, and rebuild communication connections between processors.

And that's before the lost work enters the picture.

Restarting from a checkpoint means returning to the last saved state, potentially hours earlier, and repeating every calculation that has been completed since. The hardware may be back in action, but the simulation hasn't caught up.

Atchley says Frontier can launch a scheduled job in roughly 30 seconds. Restoring the application's data and getting back to useful computation takes longer.

Worse, researchers who lose their allocation may have to rejoin the queue. Depending on demand, Atchley says, "it might take me a day or so before my job runs again."

Keeping a supercomputer available is one thing. Making sure its processors are doing useful work is another, and the difference is measurable.

A 2026 study by researchers Carlo Graziani, Bethany Lusch, and Bronson Messer examined computational efficiency using records of 331,640 jobs run on Frontier during 2024. Their model estimated that, even with optimally timed checkpoints, only 95.7 percent of allocated computing resources would go toward useful calculations.

The remaining 4.3 percent? Around 1.2 percent goes to writing checkpoints, 2.8 percent disappears into failures, and another 0.3 percent is spent restarting applications.

Skipping checkpoints altogether would be worse. The model puts useful computation at just 91.4 percent without proactive checkpointing, demonstrating that saving work has a cost, but losing it can cost considerably more.

These efficiency figures are modeled estimates, not measurements of every source of lost productivity. They don't capture every delay from rejoining queues, stalled applications, or poorly performing components. The actual productivity bill can be harder to pin down.

This makes Frontier's reported 98.49 percent overall availability in 2024 an interesting contrast. The Oak Ridge Leadership Computing Facility's operational assessment measures how often the system was available to users.

Scale makes these losses expensive. An hour of wasted computation across 2,000 nodes represents 2,000 node hours gone, compared with just 100 node hours for a job using 100 nodes.

Saving checkpoints to local SSDs can speed things up, but there's a catch. If the node fails, the checkpoint stored on it can disappear too.

Atchley points to techniques such as Lawrence Livermore National Laboratory's Scalable Checkpoint/Restart (SCR), which can copy checkpoint data to neighboring nodes. This gives applications another recovery point if the original node goes down.

Keeping spare nodes available can also shorten recovery time. Atchley says researchers who reserve extra nodes will consume their computing allocation even if nothing breaks, which leaves fewer hours for useful computation.

Software developers are also trying to avoid the restart process altogether. Vasudevan describes techniques that attempt to transfer distributed application state to replacement hardware to allow synchronized workloads to continue without returning to an earlier checkpoint.

Some approaches can handle certain GPU and network failures through failover. These techniques depend on detecting faults quickly, preserving consistent application state, and having replacement resources available.

None of this makes failures disappear. Atchley notes that hardware improvements, including stronger memory protection, can address problems encountered on earlier machines. But as systems grow, new failure modes emerge.

The next generation of supercomputers may fix the faults that plagued their predecessors, but that doesn't mean they'll never break. Atchley explains that problems solved on one machine tend to give way to entirely new ones on the next.

Faster processors and more memory are only useful if scientists can keep their calculations moving. This makes fault detection and recovery every bit as important as another headline-grabbing performance record.

Resilience research, Atchley admits, "is not sexy at all." But when you're dealing with machines of this size, "scale just amplifies everything." ®

#10340. Android app developers will have to publicly post address, phone number in 2027

Following new regulations, Google Play will require Android app developers to publicly post a business address starting in 2027 and, while there are workarounds, it’s not going to be the easiest thing.

New Digital Services Act regulations in the EU and UK will require developers to share a phone number and address publicly. While this is only required in those regions, this information will be visible worldwide. Google does not allow PO boxes to be used for this requirement, meaning that many smaller developers will be left with limited options beyond posting their own personal address.

Exceptions to the rule include apps that are not published in the UK or Europe, or developers that qualify as “non-traders” which is essentially those making no money off of their apps.

Here’s how Google defines a “trader” in the context of Android apps:

The Verge notes that a “registered agent service” can be used as a business address for those who don’t have a physical office, and that Apple is allowing iOS developers to use a PO box if they prove the box is linked to a physical address.

Google will require Android app developers to confirm their trader status and their address by February 1, 2027. Account-wide status can be found in the Google Play Console via Manage account > Account details > About you, while status on a per-app basis can be handled via Monitor and improve > Policy and programs > App content.

#10337. Google Docs can now do more with Microsoft Word documents without converting the file

Google Docs is getting an update in how it handles Microsoft Word documents, adding the ability to compare revisions without converting the file.

You can already upload a Microsoft Word document (.docx) to Google Drive and use it within Docs without converting the file, but there are some limits. One of those was comparing documents, which Google is now fixing with an update.

Rolling out now, Google Docs can now directly compare two documents, even if one is in a Microsoft Word format. To do this, you’ll need to upload the Word document to Drive, then select it in the “compare documents” tool, with Docs then showing any differences between the two. There are plenty of scenarios where this is useful, including when comparing versions of a contract.

This new feature is enabled for all users, and the company is apparently looking at similar functionality for PowerPoint files too.

#10335. Google Pixel prepares new ‘Priority Notifications’ feature – here’s what it will do

Google has a new “Priority Notifications” feature in the works for Pixel phones with the ability to pull your most important information to the top of the screen and even highlight it with a bit of color.

Google Pixel phones already have tools to manage your notifications, but an upcoming update seems to be building on that functionality. The folks at Android Authority flipped the switch on some work-in-progress changes that reveal “Priority Notifications.”

This is a new feature that Google describes as “highlighting urgrent messages” and “time-critical updates” by adding color to the notification and placing it at the top of the stock. Android’s notifications list already tries to place messages at the top, but it sounds like this AI-powered feature will further emphasize that. It’s also mentioned that this is powered by on-device AI.

Highlight urgent messages, deliveries, and time-critical updates with hints of color at the top of your notifications.

Priority notifications use Al on your device, which may sometimes make mistakes. Your notifications are never sent to Google.

The feature would also integrate with HiLight on the Pixel 11 Pro series and the feature allows users to exclude certain apps. Google also seems to be shuffling the branding, turning “Notification intelligence” into “Notification organizer,” and the existing organizer section into “Notification bundles.”

Exactly when this might land on Pixel phones is unknown. It’s not mentioned what build this feature was found hiding in, but it seems likely it was in the ongoing QPR3 beta. QPR3’s stable release will arrive around March 2027, but there’s no guarentee it’ll bring this new feature with it.

#10334. Gatwick drop-off customers' personal data exposed in supplier breach

AutoPay login and vehicle records affected, alongside limited payment information and customer service emails

London Gatwick Airport has warned customers that a breach of its drop-off payment service exposed personal information and, in limited cases, payment records.

A notification email seen by The Register identifies the affected system as AutoPay, operated by ParkPark and used by Gatwick's drop-off zone manager, Times24 UK Limited.

AutoPay lets drivers register their vehicle and payment details in advance so drop-off charges are collected automatically.

Gatwick told us it was unable to provide details about how many customers were affected by the breach, or how far back the affected records go.

The email said most affected customers would have only some of the listed data exposed. The could include names, home addresses, cities, postcodes, contact details, and AutoPay login credentials such as email addresses and bcrypt-hashed passwords were affected.

Other records implicated in the breach include details of vehicles linked to AutoPay accounts, those vehicles' entry and exit times at Times24-managed parking locations, and vehicle registrations.

The email also states that payment records, including amounts, card types, card expiry dates, payment references, transaction statuses, transaction IDs, and drop-off locations and times were affected, but a Gatwick spokesperson said this applied only to "very limited cases."

Again, in a minority of cases, "some limited emails between the customer service team and customers" were also exposed, the email stated.

Gatwick said Times24 "immediately suspended" AutoPay after detecting the intrusion. The service remains disabled while the investigation continues.

Airport officials are overseeing the response with outside cybersecurity specialists. Gatwick warned of possible disruption to its website but said flights were unaffected.

Customers can still use Gatwick's drop-off zones, although they'll have to use alternative payment methods, and all other airport services are still operating as usual.

The notification email urged customers to exercise caution when receiving communications from Gatwick, Times24, AutoPay, or ParkPark, especially those that ask for payment or bank details.

Times24, which operates across more than 200 car parks in the UK, did not immediately respond to our request for more information.

Dray Agha, senior manager at Huntress's tactical response division, warned that criminals could use the data for convincing scams long after news coverage of the breach has faded.

"The real damage often comes after the breach," he said. "Criminals rarely use stolen data straight away; they'll wait until the headlines fade, then send a text about a 'parking fine' or an email offering a 'refund' that references a real booking. Even partial card details add a layer of credibility that makes people far more likely to hand over the rest.

"Gatwick customers should assume any unexpected message about their parking could be a scam, check directly with the airport, and keep a close eye on their bank statements over the coming months." ®

#10332. Your Pose Model Gives You Coordinates, Not Meaning

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Pose detection in the browser is a solved problem. You can load a model, point it at a webcam, and get a skeleton tracking a human body in real time, on a laptop, with no server involved.

And then you have a skeleton that follows someone around the screen, which is a toy.

The gap between that toy and a feature someone would pay for is smaller than most developers expect, and it isn't made of machine learning. It's made of trigonometry you already know. This article builds a working bicep-curl rep counter to show exactly where that line falls — and more importantly, which side of it your bugs live on.

I'm using MediaPipe Pose Landmarker from Google AI Edge, which is Apache-2.0 licensed and runs entirely in the tab. Here's the whole setup:

Point that at a

Thirty-three points. Each one is a normalised coordinate — x and y run 0 to 1 across the frame, z is a relative depth estimate — plus a visibility score saying how confident the model is that it can actually see that joint.

That's the entire output. Coordinates and confidences.

The model has no concept of exercise. It doesn't know what an arm is for. It has never heard of a bicep curl. It reports where it thinks your joints are, thirty-odd times a second, and stops.

Everything that turns that into a product is yours, and it's ordinary code.

A bicep curl is a change in the angle at the elbow. The elbow angle is defined by three of those 33 points: shoulder, elbow, wrist. In MediaPipe's pose topology those are indices 11, 13 and 15 on the left side, 12, 14 and 16 on the right.

Getting the angle is two vectors and a dot product:

This is the same maths you'd use to point a game character in a direction. Nothing about it is machine learning, and that matters for a reason I'll come back to.

One thing to notice: I'm ignoring z entirely. The depth estimate is relative, not metric — it's good enough for working out which parts of a body are nearer the camera, and not good enough to measure anything. For a 2D elbow angle, using it adds noise and buys nothing.

Here's where almost everyone's first attempt breaks.

The naive version looks at the angle and counts whenever the arm is up:

Run that at 30 frames a second and one slow curl gives you about twenty reps. You haven't counted repetitions. You've counted frames in which the arm happened to be bent.

A rep isn't a state. It's a transition between states:

Fifteen lines, and that's the feature. The counter increments exactly once per cycle, on the edge, because the guard on phase makes every other frame in that cycle a no-op.

If you used a single threshold — say, 100 degrees — you'd get a counter that fires repeatedly whenever someone holds their arm near 100 degrees and the estimate jitters by a degree either side. Every tiny fluctuation across the line is a transition, and every transition is a rep.

Two widely separated thresholds give you hysteresis. Once the arm is "up," it has to travel all the way past 150 degrees before the counter will consider another rep. Noise of a few degrees can't produce a count because no amount of jitter near 55 crosses 150.

This is the same trick a thermostat uses to avoid switching the heating on and off every few seconds, and it's the single most useful pattern in this whole category of work. Any time you turn a continuous signal into discrete events, you want two thresholds, not one.

visibility is the field people skip, and skipping it is how you get phantom reps.

When a joint goes out of frame or gets occluded, the model still returns a coordinate for it. It is obliged to return 33 points. What it does is return a guess, with a low visibility score attached — and a guessed wrist position can swing wildly between frames, which looks exactly like a very fast curl.

Returning null and having update bail out means an occluded frame holds the current state rather than corrupting it. The arm stays "up" until the model can actually see it come down.

If you want to be stricter, smooth the angle over the last three or four frames with a rolling median before it reaches the state machine. A median rejects single-frame outliers in a way a mean doesn't.

Once you've written one of these, you start recognising the shape everywhere:

None of those require a different model. They're the same 33 coordinates with different geometry sitting on top, and the geometry is a few dozen lines each.

Here's why I keep insisting on the distinction between coordinates and meaning.

When my rep counter miscounts, I don't retrain anything. I don't collect a dataset, I don't label examples, I don't spin up a training job. I look at two things: my thresholds, and my state machine. One of them is wrong.

That's a normal bug in normal code, and you already know how to find it. You can log the angle. You can set a breakpoint on the transition. You can write a unit test that feeds a synthetic sequence of angles through update and asserts the count — no camera, no model, no fixtures:

The hard part of this problem — turning pixels into joint positions — was solved by a research team and shipped to you as a file you can download. The part that's left is the part you're already good at.

You are not blocked on machine learning knowledge. You were blocked on nobody telling you this was available.

I set delegate: 'GPU' above without comment, and it deserves one. MediaPipe Tasks lets you choose between a CPU path and a GPU path, and availability of the GPU path varies by browser, platform and graphics driver.

The practical consequence is that you have to test the CPU path, because for some meaningful share of your users it isn't a fallback — it's the only path they will ever take. If you develop on a machine where the GPU delegate works and never exercise the CPU one, you have shipped something you've never run.

That, and the question of how often you should run the model at all, is a bigger subject than this article. It's the next one.

Jay Nirmal is a Senior Software Engineer at Meazure Learning with over eight years of experience in distributed systems, cloud infrastructure, WebRTC, and production reliability. He leads engineering initiatives supporting large-scale digital assessment platforms, focusing on high-availability systems, real-time media infrastructure, recording reliability, and assessment integrity. Jay holds a Master of Science in Information Systems from Northeastern University and a Bachelor of Engineering in Computer Engineering from the University of Mumbai.

#10330. Amazon’s newest Ring Battery Doorbell beats Nest with 2K, and it’s only $39 right now

A video doorbell for under $40? Kinda wild, but that’s how it goes with Ring Black Friday deals this year.

If you woke up this morning and were hoping to get a new video doorbell for dirt cheap, Ring has a Black Friday deal you’re going to love. The company’s latest Ring Battery Doorbell 2K model is down to $38.99 for Black Friday, which is the lowest price ever since its release in April this year.

Meanwhile, the cheapest you can find the Nest Battery Doorbell today is $154.99, and that model is a whopping five years old. That camera operates at 960 x 1280 resolution, while the $39 Ring Battery Doorbell 2K operates at a perfectly square 1920 x 1920 resolution. Square sensors have taken root as the better option for doorbell cameras because of the aspect ratio, and the significantly higher resolution of Ring’s camera offers 6x digital zoom as well.

Like Google’s Nest cameras, Ring cameras don’t offer local storage options built into the camera, but Ring plans are just $9.99 per month and cover all cameras on your account with up to 180 days of playback history, so it’s not exactly pricey to keep them all running. Nearly everything Ring makes is on sale for Black Friday, so be sure to check that out if you don’t see something specific here that you were looking forward to.

#10328. Amazon to flog motors in Britain, but Prime won't deliver

Hyundai leads 2027 rollout, with a showroom visit still required to finish the paperwork

Amazon is channeling its inner car salesman as it prepares to flog motors on the UK arm of its online tat bazaar.

Amazon Autos will launch in Britain in early 2027, its first expansion outside the US, where it debuted in December 2024. Customers can browse and reserve cars online before completing the purchase with a dealer.

Hoping it will be "a nice little earner," Amazon will let punters view real-time dealer inventory, prices, get an instant trade-in valuation, and connect with a local dealer to reserve a car, "all on Amazon."

In the US, the service now covers more than 150 cities and offers vehicles from 35 brands. Hyundai is the first announced UK carmaker partner, while dealer group Vertu Motors will list used vehicles. More brands and dealers are due to be confirmed before launch.

Hyundai's launch range will include electric, hybrid, and petrol models.

"Buying a car is one of the biggest purchases people make, and we think it should feel as simple and transparent as shopping for anything on Amazon," said Matt Nuffort, director of Amazon Autos International.

Of course, buying a car isn't quite like adding an air fryer or a memory stick to your basket. How does financing and checkout work for UK buyers? Customers will complete the purchase and paperwork at their chosen dealership.

Amazon is not offering financing itself, but buyers can explore financing options via the site, adjusting terms like deposit and duration to see how monthly payments change. Credit applications must be completed with the dealer.

In the US, buyers can submit a credit application through the platform, review terms and conditions, then sign electronic contracts online. They typically pay a deposit and visit the dealership to complete the remaining paperwork and pick up the vehicle.

Buyers shouldn't expect their new car to arrive in an enormous cardboard box with a smiley logo on it, or to be lowered onto their driveway by a giant Amazon delivery drone.

But given that one of the online retailer's flying delivery robots crashed into someone's garage after making a drop-off near Detroit last week, perhaps that's for the best.  ®

#10327. Like clockwork, the Pixel 11 is a way better buy after a few months [Video]

Like clockwork, the first quarter of the Pixel 11 brings discounts, and this shines a light on the base model that needs to be explored.

By this point, you don’t need me to tell you that this is a familiar design. It’s turning into a comfortable pair of slippers at this point in time. Nothing is wrong, and nothing particularly stands out. A safe but samey design that is inoffensive.

I hoped that the maximum brightness bug would have been resolved by now with the screen. It creates a shadow around the edges of the display after a minute or so when in direct sunlight. Somewhat like blooming on a cheap LCD screen. It does diminish what is otherwise a great display.

The thickness has started to feel like a relic of the 2023 design that feels a little dated now. I have been using the Pixel 11 without a case most of the time, which mitigates it, but throw a case on and it’s a chunky boi. The added thickness would probably have been acceptable had the extra space been filled with more battery, but it feels like that boat has set sail a long time ago. A slimmer profile probably would have helped differentiate without deviating too much from last year’s phone.

After months of using the Pixel 11, the move of the NFC module to the top edge has been huge for me because my car uses an NFC terminal to lock and unlock when you’re without a set of car keys. With the Pixel 10, it worked but not quite as smoothly. It’s one of those inconsequential changes that makes a real-world difference.

Gemini Intelligence has been something I’ve started to rely on quite heavily. I love that I get a prompt to add an appointment to my calendar, and all of the important details have been taken care of, like the location, extra contact details, the works.

I’m not sure what the hold-up is for adding wider functionality. It’s actively hurting things, in my opinion. Google Messages is great, but some of the automation functions locked in there mean I haven’t been saving any time with Gemini’s prowess at doing the legwork of keeping my life up to date.

I’ve tried using Pause Point, but found it’s just easier to leave my phone face down rather than use an app to try and stop using my phone…maybe if we all did that, so many of the world’s ills wouldn’t be so pronounced. Just a thought.

The November Pixel Drop has also made some notable changes to the Pixel 11 experience that I can’t believe were held back since launch. If you waited and bought in the last few weeks with the substantial discounts kicking around, you objectively get a better phone today than at launch.

The improvements don’t overhaul things, just enhance or heighten the fluidity and experience above what was available in the months post-launch. More cohesive theming, little tweaks to the UI; it’s not reinventing the Pixel 11, just augmenting it in ways that would have been nice back in August.

I don’t really have much to complain about with the Pixel 11 lifespan even after months of charging. Yes, charging is still slow, but you only need to charge once per day. If we were talking about a phone with bad battery life and slow charging, I’d have more to complain about. However, it’s still solid and dependable.

The camera continues to impress without really improving drastically. I can’t help but feel that every couple of generations, the Pro camera setup should be passed on to the base Pixel, then we get a newer, improved system on that specific Pro model. It’d allow for quicker improvements and maybe, dare I say it, make the camera system cheaper as parts get reused. If anything, it would help Google’s supposed environmental pledges by preventing camera components from hitting landfill.

A lot of people will tell you the best camera is the one you have with you, and this hasn’t ever been a problem for a recent Pixel. I can count on one hand the frustrations I have felt with the Pixel 11 camera since it landed on my desk on launch day. I am happier than a year ago with the base Pixel 10 than the 11, which is to say this is a bigger leap or a return to form.

Having the ability to tune Camera Looks was something I have loved, but you might just fall into using one tuned look that fits your own personal style. There is an element of this that might change the perception of the Pixel 11 camera consistency versus its predecessors. What I mean is that while it does still excel at point-and-shoot, the fact that Looks are baked into the JPEG could be a problem. There are times when I’ve thought that an image might have been better using default settings.

That said, I have stuck to “Shadows” with Contrast set to -5, Highlights to -5, Shadows to -5.5, warmth to 0.5, and Grain to “Strong.” That is a recipe I’ll probably stick with for a long time to come. It’s still weird that we can do all this at the camera level with a Pixel. Whether “normies” even use the options or just stick to the out-of-the-box default settings is another discussion for another day.

At $699, the Pixel 11 becomes a decent buy for a lot of people. For others, the distinct lack of power is something that can’t ever be overcome. I would buy this for a relative because it’ll do everything they could ever want and more. For a friend who cares about having the cutting edge in their pocket? Probably not.

A lot of the problems over the past few years on Pixel phones have come from the price tag.

Even with the discounts in and around Black Friday, the fact that you can get a device like the Galaxy S25 Ultra that’ll have better specs and lots of valuable features for around the same price. The last few generations of the Pixel have been harder to recommend because of the drawbacks versus the cost and the competition.

I said a few months ago that there is a good phone under the price hikes, and that remains unchanged. However, there are better products from the year prior that won’t compromise and are even cheaper. In a silo, you’re getting a 7/10 product at a 3/10 price. Just how that changes depends on forthcoming updates. One saving grace is that no Pixel is ever quite the same months after it rolls off the production line. A few Pixel Drops, even more discounts, and maybe a true seal of approval might be easier.

To put it into context, this was never a $900 phone and arguably never should have been. That’s somewhat a failing on Google’s part in more ways than one. At least it’s down to a palatable price that should be the Pixel baseline.

#10326. A2UI 1.0: Build delightful, fast, reliable, agentic experiences

User interactions with agents today are where computers were in the terminal era: capable, and answering almost everything in text. Users can ask an agent for anything by typing or talking, and what comes back is usually a wall of text, a poor way to compare three products or edit and approve a detailed plan.

Today, we're releasing A2UI 1.0, the first stable version of our open protocol for generative UI. It lets AI agents answer with interactive screens, such as a form to confirm or a chart. Developers can use it now with open-source renderer SDKs for the web, Android, and iOS and agent SDKs for Python, TypeScript, and Dart. Companies such as Atlassian, Alation, Bloomberg, and ByteDance are already building with it.

With A2UI, the agent describes what to show, and every app draws the UI with its own native components. The application receives A2UI messages and each app renders the content securely and at native speed. Users get the same low latency, high trust, and complete native fidelity whether they reach a company's agent in its own app or in a third-party AI assistant that hosts MCP apps, such as Gemini or Claude.

You wake up sick. Getting through the morning takes six apps: book a video visit, move your meetings, tell your team, turn on an out-of-office reply, ask your partner to do school pickup and order soup. Ask a text-only agent instead, and you get a numbered wall of text, then round after round of “which ones?”. A2UI won't handle your sick day, but it gives developers the framework to build an agent that can. That agent could answer with one view of all six apps. Changing your plan is a couple of taps instead of another round of messages, and nothing is sent until you confirm.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

The same agent output works on any screen. Ask an agent to “move dinner with Priya to Friday”, and it can answer with one view that lines up your calendar, open tables from a restaurant booking app and prices from a rideshare app, so the time that works for everyone is obvious. The developer builds that view once with A2UI, and it renders natively on your phone and on your laptop.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

In both examples, A2UI is the framework that simplifies building these experiences across platforms. Developers build the agent and choose the platforms it works with. A2UI gives them the protocol the agent uses to describe each view, a way to combine components from different apps in one view, and renderers that draw it natively on the web, Android, and iOS.

New to A2UI? Start with the A2UI Playground by CopilotKit or check out industry examples.

Atlassian is bringing A2UI to Jira, where teams can assign work items to AI agents. When an agent needs a follow-up from the team to finish a work item, such as a missing detail or a decision, it asks with an interactive card in the work item, and the team answers right there in Jira.

The Alation Intelligence Operating System (AIOS) builds its feeds using A2UI: metric cards, charts, and more. Each chart can show the SQL query and data behind it. Ask Claude for a feed about profitable products, and the feed renders in the conversation using A2UI to render metric cards, charts, and custom components. The same feed with the same information can be viewed directly inside Alation with a nearly identical layout.

ByteDance released an A2UI renderer for Lynx, its cross-platform framework, in June 2026. Lynx keeps streamed screens smooth and ships a default set of components built on its `lynx-ui` library. In the Lynx GenUI Playground, you can watch A2UI messages rendered natively, for example, as a two-day trip planner for Kyoto. Lynx supports A2UI 0.9 today and is adapting to 1.0 with the A2UI team.

Ask Gemini Notebook for a slide deck and it replies with a card that asks two things: presenter slides or a detailed deck, and which topics to focus on. A review card follows with the format, focus, and visual style, which you can edit before it generates the deck. The same cards guide Video Overviews on the web and on mobile.

Organizations can register their own agents that respond with A2UI. Gemini renders those responses using the components in its A2UI component gallery, and supports A2UI 0.9.1 today. The Google Cloud documentation covers registering an agent and hosting one on Cloud Run.

Several other Google teams, including some behind our largest products, are building features with A2UI.

Several other Google teams, including some behind our largest products, are building features with A2UI that haven’t launched yet. We’ll share more as they ship.

A2UI and MCP Apps work together. Alation already uses both to show its A2UI feeds in Claude. Our June post, A2UI + MCP Apps, covers three ways to combine them, and the A2UI in MCP Apps guide shows how to run A2UI screens inside an MCP App.

Jetpack Compose support for 1.0 is coming soon. Agents in Gemini Enterprise can already use A2UI, and we're working on upgrading Gemini Enterprise to A2UI 1.0. The A2UI roadmap covers the rest, from design-system primitives that render natively on every platform to official SDK support for decision models like Jev.

We’ve collaborated with partners who have built A2UI 1.0 demos across different industries to give you inspiration on how to adapt A2UI to your use cases. Explore what they’ve built:

From Files to Cited Answers in One API Call: Managed Ingestion and SearchDocuments in Agent Retrieval

Multi-Turn Agentic Reinforcement Learning with Tunix and Gemma 4 on Google TPUs

Securing Your Agents: A Hands-On Guide to Agent Anomaly Detection

Did the user ask for this? Securing AI agents against intent hijacking with Semantic governance policies

#10319. Microsoft 365 Backup stretches recovery window to ten years

Recovery windows of up to ten years are now available in Microsoft 365 Backup for OneDrive and SharePoint, up from a previous maximum of two.

The change took effect on October 31, though Exchange remains capped at two years.

Administrators can select recovery windows of three months, six months, or any whole number of years from one to ten. Microsoft's list price is $0.15 per GiB per month of protected content. Extended windows can increase costs by keeping deleted data billable for longer.

Switching to a longer recovery window will also not make backups for that period magically available. So no nostalgic trips back to 2016.

Wider recovery windows may appeal to organizations that need access to older data, but backups are not a substitute for compliance retention and archiving. Recovery after a breach or ransomware attack is also critical, particularly getting back to a healthy state before any malware seeped into files.

Microsoft says backups are physically redundant and geographically replicated while respecting the tenant's data residency requirements.

Full OneDrive account and SharePoint site restores offer recovery points every ten minutes for the most recent two weeks, then weekly points through the configured window, up to ten years.

Individual file and folder recovery is limited to recovery points from the most recent 14 days, regardless of the configured window.

Restore remains free, which is handy since regular testing is a vital part of any disaster recovery plan.

The arrival of a ten-year recovery window is good for enterprises that need it and have deep enough pockets. However, it is important to keep in mind that being able to configure a longer recovery window doesn't constitute a disaster recovery plan in itself and may be overkill in many instances.

Microsoft notes: "The existing one-year default recovery window remains unchanged. Longer recovery windows must be configured on the applicable backup policies."

The company also recommends that users "review your organization's SharePoint and OneDrive backup requirements to determine whether longer recovery windows are needed."

A decade of recovery history could be valuable. It also means a decade of data to account for when setting budgets, access controls, and retention policies. ®

The X12 uses an 11-stage reverse osmosis filtration system that helps reduce TDS, chlorine, heavy metals, PFOA, PFOS, and other specified contaminants. It also adds minerals such as calcium and magnesium back into the water through alkaline remineralization, delivering a smoother, more balanced taste for everyday use. With that 1,200 GPD capacity, it can keep up with heavier day-to-day use as well as busier holiday hosting without slowing things down.

You’re also getting a tankless design with a 3:1 pure-to-drain ratio, along with a smart faucet that comes in silver, black, bronze, and gold finishes to better match different kitchen setups. The X12 is certified to NSF/ANSI 42, 58, and 372 standards, and it has picked up some notable recognition too, including being named “Best Smart Reverse Osmosis Water System” by Forbes Vetted in 2026 and appearing in The New York Times Wirecutter’s roundup of the best reverse osmosis systems.

The X12 is easily one of the standout options in Waterdrop Filter’s Black Friday lineup, and it’s worth considering if you’re looking for a long-term under sink reverse osmosis upgrade and want strong flow, advanced filtration, and plenty of everyday convenience.

You don’t necessarily need a huge kitchen or a large family to make the jump to a proper RO setup, and that’s where the latest Waterdrop G6A6 Reverse Osmosis System comes in. This compact model is down to $189.99 shipped from its $300 usual price for Black Friday, and you use promo code 9TO5GBF26 to score an extra 5% off, making it one of the more affordable options in Waterdrop Filter’s under-sink lineup this year. The biggest advantage of the G6A6 system is its compact design, which Waterdrop says can save up to 81.8% of under-sink space. That makes it a particularly good fit for apartments, smaller kitchens, or any setup where you don’t want a bulky filtration system taking up most of the cabinet.

Despite the smaller footprint, though, this model can delivers up to 600 gallons per day, which should be more than enough for the everyday drinking, cooking, and beverage needs of a smaller household. Its eight-stage filtration system is also paired with alkaline remineralization to add minerals such as calcium and magnesium back for a better-tasting water.

It’s safe to say the Waterdrop G6A6 is probably the more sensible pick over stepping up to something as powerful as the X12 if you have a smaller family of three or four, or you’re simply working with limited kitchen space.

If you’d rather skip an under-sink installation altogether, the Waterdrop M6HG Mineral Instant Hot Reverse Osmosis System is another solid option in the sale. This particular model gives you filtered water plus instant heating in one compact setup without any plumbing required.

It can dispense filtered water anywhere from room temperature up to 95°C, or 203°F, in seconds, which means you can jump straight into making coffee, tea, breakfast, or other hot drinks without waiting around for a kettle.

It also uses an eight-stage RO filtration system to help reduce TDS, chlorine, fluoride, PFAS, heavy metals, unpleasant odors, and other specified substances. Waterdrop then adds minerals such as calcium and magnesium back into the water through remineralization, similar to the X12 and G6A6 models highlighted above.

There’s no permanent installation required either, so you can simply place the unit on the counter and get going. That makes it a particularly convenient option for renters, apartments, home offices, RVs, or anyone who wants a countertop reverse osmosis system without having to make any changes to the plumbing.

The CT01 can dispense water anywhere from 59°F to 203°F, covering everything from a cold glass of water to oatmeal, coffee, tea, and other hot drinks without the need for a separate kettle. It’s geared more toward everyday convenience than advanced RO filtration, so it works particularly well for apartments, offices, students, couples, or anyone who wants a simple all-in-one countertop setup.

It offers a six-stage filtration system to help reduce chlorine, rust, sediment, and other common impurities while retaining beneficial minerals in the water. Its 1.32-gallon capacity also helps cut down on frequent refills throughout the day, and there’s no wastewater or installation to deal with either. That keeps the whole setup fairly simple if you just want filtered hot and cold water without adding a more involved system to your kitchen.
