#10273. Catch Go bugs that fuzzing misses with Gemini and a constraint solver

I help lead Google's agent skills program, currently with 175 agent skills that supplement community collections like go-skills. To keep that many skill files consistent, I wrote skill-lint, a standalone Go CLI that checks SKILL.md files and applies LLM-generated line edits before they ship. Even with skill-lint's own unit and fuzz test suite, a small slice or line-number mistake inside its Markdown parsers or edit applier can sit dormant until an unusual file or edit range triggers a runtime panic in the linter.

In Go, every slice or index operation that can panic at runtime compiles down to an explicit bounds-check trap in machine code, after the compiler's optimizer strips out the checks it can already rule out. To hunt for crashes, I compiled key functions from my code to see which panic traps survived optimization, and Gemini 4 Argon translated the path to each trap into rules for the Z3 constraint solver. An LLM-written solver model isn't a sound proof system, but it gives the agent an independent way to reason about control flow and synthesize concrete Go reproducers, and every crash it finds is real because go test runs the offending bytes directly. That workflow caught all three bugs I planted in test parsers and turned up three latent bugs in skill-lint.

In this post, I'll walk through how the Gemini and Z3 pipeline works, why go test -fuzz missed two of those crashes (and the one-line change that fixes it), and where this approach fits in a day-to-day Go workflow.

A recent post from Anthropic showed how formal verification catches latent bugs in C and C++ by having frontier AI models write machine-checked Rocq proofs across OSS-Fuzz libraries. Instead of sampling random inputs like a fuzzer, a formal tool works backward from each failure condition to solve for the exact input that triggers it. Full theorem proving is still too heavy for most day-to-day Go projects, so I wanted to see how much of that bug-hunting power an agent can get from an LLM and a constraint solver in about a minute per function.

In C and C++, reading or writing past the end of a buffer corrupts memory. Go catches out-of-bounds access at runtime instead, compiling every slice or index operation it can't prove safe into an explicit branch to runtime.panicBounds that panics, crashing the process unless recovered.

Before generating machine code, Go's SSA prove pass already eliminates most of those checks by tracking slice lengths and loop variables. That optimization pass leaves behind only the runtime.panicBounds traps the compiler couldn't rule out on its own, giving the solver a short list of candidate crash sites to target.

I compiled each target with GOOS=linux GOARCH=riscv64 go build -gcflags="-S -d=ssa/check_bce/debug=1" (-S prints the assembly listing and ssa/check_bce/debug=1 logs every surviving bounds check) and passed both the Go source and the disassembly to the model. Looking at Go source alone, you can't see which bounds checks the compiler already removed or where a register gets truncated to 8 bits; in the disassembly, both show up. I targeted RISC-V because it's the instruction set Anthropic's post targets for its Rocq proofs, and Go cross-compiles to it out of the box.

From there, Argon read the Go source and disassembly and returned a Z3 script modeling the path to each surviving trap, along with a patched function whenever it flagged a bug. Z3 then checked whether those constraints could be satisfied, and whenever it found a crashing input, go test replayed those bytes against the real function. Using an LLM saved me from translating assembly into solver constraints by hand. Argon's translation dominated the runtime, at about a minute per function, while Z3 itself finished in milliseconds.

Anthropic's approach relies on interactive theorem proving, where experts model machine semantics and Rocq checks LLM-generated proofs against those rules. In contrast, my workflow uses a constraint solver based on satisfiability modulo theories (SMT), relying on Argon to translate assembly directly into Z3 constraints. While a constraint solver doesn't provide a complete correctness proof without a semantic model, it is highly effective for finding bugs. Z3's check-sat command returns sat when input constraints can be satisfied and unsat when they cannot.

A sat answer ("definitely buggy") comes with concrete bytes aimed at the trap, and go test runs them against the real function. Once go test panics, you have a concrete bug reproducer regardless of whether Argon's model was sound. Like a Bloom filter for bugs, an unsat answer only means "maybe correct." Trading away a correctness proof is what makes the approach so practical: you get most of the bug-finding value of formal methods for a tiny fraction of the effort.

Anthropic's setup proved no-crash properties on whole binaries under a 64 MB budget; I checked individual functions on fresh 64-byte slices where cap(buf) == len(buf), so any slice past len(buf) panics. (If you want the full-binary Rocq setup for Go, theorem-labs/oss-verify includes a Go 1.27 runtime overlay that makes the runtime single-threaded and deterministic.)

For the fuzzing baseline, I ran unseeded go test -fuzz on Go 1.27.3 against each planted bug: one 3-second run and three 60-second runs, each with a fresh fuzz cache so a short time budget wouldn't explain a miss.

I started with three safe parsers to make sure the pipeline didn't flag clean code. The compiler's prove pass removed every bounds check in a TOML key parser on its own, and Z3 returned unsat on the remaining traps in an HTTP header scanner and a varint decoder.

Then I wrote three parsers with subtle panics. Here's the first one, ParseTwoChunkFrame:

In the RISC-V disassembly, the bug is much easier to see:

total := hdrLen + bodyLen adds two uint8 values, so MOVBU wraps the sum at 256 before the length guard checks it, while endBody adds the full 64-bit ints. Argon turned those instructions into a Z3 query asking whether a fresh slice of up to 64 bytes can pass the guard and still fail the endBody check at 0x0092:

Z3 returned a 59-byte counterexample with hdrLen = 48 and bodyLen = 236: their uint8 sum wraps from 284 down to 28 (so 28 + 4 ≤ 59 passes the guard), and buf[endHdr:endBody] then slices to index 288 and panics.

At 59 bytes, 19.4% of all possible (hdrLen, bodyLen) pairs trigger this crash. Yet a 3-second fuzz run missed it, and so did three 60-second runs.

Go checks a slice expression like buf[endHdr:endBody] against the slice's capacity, not its length, and Go's fuzzer rarely passes a slice without spare capacity. In a 10-second probe of 386,741 fuzz calls, 89% of inputs were views into a shared ~100 MB buffer, and only 0.07% had cap == len. With that much spare capacity, endBody (at most 514) never exceeds cap(buf), so the buggy parser returns bytes past the end of buf instead of panicking.

The second target, ExtractQuotedToken, hit the same blind spot when a trailing backslash pushed the index one byte past len(buf). Z3 caught it immediately with the 2-byte input []byte{'"', '\\'}, while the fuzzer absorbed the overrun in spare capacity.

Clipping capacity to length with a full slice expression turns any slice past len into a panic:

After that change, the fuzzer crashed both ParseTwoChunkFrame and ExtractQuotedToken in all three runs (median 0.28 s and 0.17 s). The third target, ParseMagicPacket, was a control because its signed int8 length bug panics on a negative index regardless of capacity, and the fuzzer found it in every run either way. Fuzzing still can't give you an unsat signal across the remaining paths, which is how I checked the three safe parsers and Argon's patches.

Next, I pointed the pipeline at skill-lint itself. A whole Go CLI is too large to hand to a solver directly. Even a 10-line parser links 1,559 symbols from the Go runtime, and the 28 MB skill-lint binary has 32,321 symbols in total. Following Functional Core, Imperative Shell, I left CLI flags, network calls, and file I/O to go test -fuzz and go test -race, and ran the solver only on pure functions and slice helpers extracted from file-reading code.

Of the five functions I tested, two scanners came back unsat (including a CRLF line splitter with six surviving bounds checks), and the other three had bugs. The sharpest was in ApplyReplacementChunks, which validates LLM-generated {StartLine, EndLine} edits against the initial line count and sorts them bottom-to-top so earlier edits don't shift later line numbers:

Z3 immediately returned sat because sorting by StartLine doesn't prevent two chunks from overlapping. In the extracted helper, which checks bounds once up front, replacing lines 3 and 4 of a 4-line file with one line shrinks lines to 3 elements, so a second chunk spanning lines 2 through 4 evaluates lines[4:] and panics with slice bounds out of range [4:3]. In the shipped ApplyChunks, a per-iteration bounds check turns that case into a misleading range error, while overlapping chunks that stay within len(lines) silently overwrite the earlier edit. Rejecting overlapping chunks after the sort fixes both.

The other two bugs were similar accounting errors. In the local fixer, target line numbers were checked once per violation before the inner chunk loop applied deletions that shrank the slice (a latent panic if a fixer ever returns a second chunk after a deletion, though DeleteLineFix only returns one today). In the prompt packer, a truncation check reserved a flat 100 bytes for a ~70-byte wrapper plus file path, overshooting the 2 MB context budget in the shipped code when a path exceeds about 30 bytes and panicking in the extracted helper.

When you check a function modularly like this, the target has to include its own entry-point guards (like the bounds check and sort in ApplyReplacementChunks). If an internal helper assumes preconditions that are only enforced by its caller or stated in a doc comment, Z3 will happily invent inputs that violate them. Because each extracted helper included its entry guards, false positives weren't an issue: across all six buggy functions in my tests, the first sat assignment Z3 returned triggered a real panic in go test without needing to filter out non-crashing candidates or loop back into the solver.

Once I pulled these helpers away from file I/O, a 3-second fuzz run caught all three bugs too. To make sure Argon wasn't leaning on source comments in my test files, I also reran the skill-lint targets with all comments stripped; Argon and Z3 still caught all three bugs and returned unsat on the two scanners and all three patches.

Staying entirely inside the target language makes a whole class of bugs hard for an agent to see. When an LLM reviews Go source directly, a line like if int(total)+4 > len(buf) looks like a textbook bounds check, and when it runs go test -fuzz, target-language runtime details like spare slice capacity or multi-step precondition checks can hide the crash indefinitely. Asking the agent to translate Go and assembly into a constraint solver forces a change of representation that strips away surface syntax and makes every bit width and slice bound explicit, while go test turns any solver hit back into a concrete Go reproducer.

For most Go code, fuzzing pure helpers with slice capacity clipped to length will catch bounds bugs in seconds. A solver is worth the extra minute on the traps that survive both the compiler's range prover and a quick fuzz run, especially in parsers and editors that compute offsets from multi-field headers or line ranges.

Single-threaded solver models don't see data races or deadlocks, so concurrent code still belongs with the race detector, deterministic concurrency testing, and Go 1.27's goroutine leak profiles.

If you're experimenting with solvers or coding agents on Go, let's compare notes on X or LinkedIn.

#10269. NASA's nuclear-powered Mars delivery service takes shape

SR-1 Freedom gets a shorter boom and larger antennas, with roughly two years to finish design, construction, and testing

NASA has settled key design choices for its planned nuclear-fission-powered spacecraft, Space Reactor-1 (SR-1) Freedom, following a mission review in October.

Targeting launch in late 2028, SR-1 would be the first fission-powered spacecraft to travel beyond Earth orbit. It is designed to demonstrate nuclear electric propulsion and deliver a capsule carrying three helicopters to Mars.

There have been some changes since NASA Administrator Jared Isaacman showed off the concept [PDF] at the agency's Ignition event in March. Improved shielding and spacecraft design will allow a shorter boom between the reactor and the spacecraft's main body, protecting its electronics and the SkyFall payload. The shorter boom will, according to NASA, "reduce weight, simplify assembly, and help streamline the spacecraft’s design."

The revised design also includes larger X-band antenna dishes to transmit data to Earth.

Decisions are still pending on how many radiator panels the vehicle needs, the output power levels of the nuclear power module, and how long the reactor will operate.

Currently, the mission calls for the spacecraft to start its reactor once it has left Earth orbit and gradually ramp up power. After a period during which performance data will be sent back to Earth, the reactor will be shut down until after the SkyFall capsule, containing a trio of Mars Ingenuity-class helicopters, is delivered to Martian orbit.

Solar arrays will power the spacecraft during its cruise to Mars. The fission reactor will then be reactivated to let engineers collect more data on its performance in deep space.

NASA also awarded a $39.3 million contract to Johns Hopkins University's Applied Physics Laboratory to develop the heat rejection system for the nuclear power module.

"Further maturation of the spacecraft plans will focus on reducing risk, simplifying the design, and staying on schedule," the agency said.

The latter part regarding the schedule is certainly a challenge. Meeting the late-2028 launch target leaves roughly two years to finish designing, building, and testing the spacecraft.

ESA is also targeting late 2028 for its long-delayed Rosalind Franklin rover. Missing the intended Mars departure opportunity could push SR-1 into a later launch window, although NASA has not specified a fallback schedule. ®

#10268. What an AI Marketing Agent Actually Does, and How to Build a Guarded One

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

An AI marketing agent is a system that pairs a language model with access to real tools, such as a CRM, an ad platform API or a CMS, and lets it work toward a goal instead of following a fixed script. That is the core idea. Much of what gets labeled agentic marketing is either a wrapper around that loop or a scheduled job with a new name.

This article breaks down the mechanics in code terms, walks through a minimal guarded agent loop with tests, and gives a framework for deciding what to build, what to buy and what to keep away from any autonomous system. Product capabilities change quickly, so check each vendor's current documentation.

● An agent is a goal, a tool set and a loop. The model chooses the next action at runtime instead of following branches you wrote in advance.

● Guardrails belong in code. Spend caps, allow-lists and approval thresholds should never depend on the prompt.

● The prototype is the easy part. Validation, retries, logging and evaluation are where the weeks go.

● Match autonomy to reversibility. Let agents do reversible, measurable work and keep irreversible actions supervised.

● Treat vendor claims as claims. Ask for logs and test on your own data.

Many marketing features have been renamed in the last couple of years. Email sequencing, lead scoring and campaign tools are now often described as agents. The honest question is whether a given product is a real architecture shift or marketing automation with a chat box added. Both exist: some systems run a perceive-reason-act loop with dynamic tool selection, while others are if/else branches with a model writing the subject line.

Strip away the marketing language and an agent has three parts:

● A goal, expressed in natural language or as a measurable target.

● A tool set: functions the model is allowed to call, each described by a schema.

● A loop that runs until the goal is met, a limit is hit or a human steps in.

The reasoning step is a model call. You pass it the current state, the goal and the available tools, and it responds with text or a structured request to call a tool. Your code runs the call, feeds the result back and asks again. OpenAI's function calling guide describes this as a conversation between your application and the model, which is the primitive underneath most agent frameworks.

Compare that with a cron job. A cron job runs a rule such as pausing ads below a click-through threshold every morning. It cannot decide that cost per acquisition matters more than click-through this week. An agent can, because the branching is chosen at runtime by a model rather than at write time by you. A useful test: if you can draw the full decision tree before the system runs, it is automation. If the path depends on what the model decides after seeing the data, it is an agent.

● Perceive. Read current state from an ad platform's reporting endpoint, a CRM webhook or a database. Ordinary code, no model involved.

● Reason. One model call containing the goal, the state, a summary of past actions and the schemas of every tool the agent may use.

● Act. The model returns a structured tool call. Your code validates the arguments against the schema and your guardrails, then executes.

● Observe. Capture the result, including failures. A rate-limit error or validation error is information for the next step.

● Update memory. Record the action and outcome so the agent does not repeat itself.

● Repeat or stop. Loop until the goal is met, an iteration cap is reached or a guardrail escalates to a human.

Steps two and three rely on function calling, where tools are declared with typed parameters and the model returns matching arguments. The Hugging Face function calling guide shows the same pattern. For connecting tools across systems, the Model Context Protocol is an open protocol for integrating LLM applications with external data sources and tools. Instead of writing an adapter for every CRM and ad account, you point an MCP client at a server that exposes those tools with discoverable schemas.

Lead scoring and routing. The agent reads CRM state, enriches it with lookups, scores the lead and writes back an owner and next action. It is agentic when it chooses which enrichment tools to call based on what is missing, and not agentic when the score is a fixed model output piped into a rule.

Ad creative generation and testing. The agent produces variations from brand inputs, launches a structured test, reads performance data and shifts budget toward what works. This is among the clearest closed feedback loops because the outcome signal is numeric and fast.

IBM's overview of AI agents in marketing frames a similar progression, from scripted chatbots at one end to agents that decide, design workflows and use function calling at the other. Most products sit somewhere in between.

Vendors sometimes blur this line. Four criteria help even from the outside:

Practical tests: give it a goal it has no preset workflow for and see whether it composes a path or returns an apology. Change the data mid-run and check whether the next step differs. Ask for the tool call log, because a real agent produces one. Ask what happens when a tool call fails; a script throws, while an agent should retry or re-plan.

Three questions decide this, and none are about how clever the model is: can errors be detected quickly, can the action be reversed, and what does one bad decision cost?

● Reasonable to hand over: drafting copy variations, summarizing campaign performance, flagging data mismatches, writing internal briefs, pausing clearly underperforming ads.

● Keep supervised: budget reallocation across accounts, legal, medical or financial claims, pricing and discount changes, messages to named customers and final brand-voice sign-off.

A human approval step costs minutes. An unsupervised mistake in those areas can cost far more.

For many teams the practical answer is to build the orchestration that touches proprietary data and internal systems, and to evaluate off-the-shelf tools for narrow, high-volume tasks that other teams have already solved. What people underestimate when building is not the prototype, which can take an afternoon, but everything after: schema validation, retries, idempotency, rate-limit handling, prompt versioning, cost controls, an audit log and an evaluation harness.

Ad creative is a common candidate for the existing-tool route because volume is high and the loop is similar across businesses. Vendors in this space describe their products differently, so compare scope, integrations and pricing. As one example, Larven describes an AI marketing agent that runs Meta ads, pulls products and copy from a store such as Shopify, and connects to your own ad account. Treat any such description as the vendor's own, and verify it with a trial on your data before committing.

The goal: keep spend inside a daily cap while letting a model propose budget changes. The model is injected as a function, so you can wire in any provider's function-calling API or a stub for tests. The important part is that every limit is enforced in code. The model proposes, and your code decides.

"""A minimal guarded agent loop. The model is injected as a function, so you can swap in any provider's function-calling API, or a stub for tests.""" from dataclasses import dataclass, field @dataclass class Guardrails:     allowed_campaigns: set     daily_cap: float            # max total daily budget across campaigns     max_change_pct: float = 0.2 # larger single changes need a human     max_actions: int = 5        # per run @dataclass class State:     budgets: dict               # campaign_id -> daily budget     log: list = field(default_factory=list)     pending: list = field(default_factory=list) def check(call, state, g):     """Return 'run', 'approve' or ('deny', reason). Enforced in code, never by the prompt."""     if call["name"] != "adjust_budget":         return ("deny", "unknown tool")     cid, new = call["args"]["campaign_id"], call["args"]["new_daily_budget"]     if cid not in g.allowed_campaigns or cid not in state.budgets:         return ("deny", "campaign not allowed")     if new < 0:         return ("deny", "negative budget")     others = sum(v for k, v in state.budgets.items() if k != cid)     if others + new > g.daily_cap:         return ("deny", "daily cap exceeded")     old = state.budgets[cid]     if old and abs(new - old) / old > g.max_change_pct:         return "approve"     return "run" def run(model, state, g):     """model(state) -> a tool call dict, or None when it has nothing to do."""     for in range(g.maxactions):         call = model(state)         if call is None:             break         verdict = check(call, state, g)         state.log.append({"call": call, "verdict": verdict})         if verdict == "run":             state.budgets[call["args"]["campaign_id"]] = call["args"]["new_daily_budget"]         elif verdict == "approve":             state.pending.append(call)     return state

The tests use a stub in place of a model. They confirm that small changes run, large changes wait for approval, the daily cap and allow-list are enforced, an unknown tool is denied, and a model that never stops is halted by the action limit with everything logged:

from agent_loop import Guardrails, State, check, run G = Guardrails(allowed_campaigns={"a", "b"}, daily_cap=200) def call(cid, new): return {"name": "adjust_budget", "args": {"campaign_id": cid, "new_daily_budget": new}} def test_small_change_runs():     assert check(call("a", 105), State({"a": 100, "b": 50}), G) == "run" def test_large_change_needs_approval():     assert check(call("a", 150), State({"a": 100, "b": 50}), G) == "approve" def test_cap_and_allow_list_are_enforced():     s = State({"a": 100, "b": 90, "c": 10})     assert check(call("a", 115), s, G) == ("deny", "daily cap exceeded")     assert check(call("c", 11), s, G) == ("deny", "campaign not allowed")     assert check({"name": "delete_account", "args": {}}, s, G) == ("deny", "unknown tool") def test_loop_stops_at_action_limit_and_logs_everything():     s = State({"a": 100, "b": 50})     g = Guardrails({"a", "b"}, 500, max_actions=3)     run(lambda st: call("a", st.budgets["a"] + 1), s, g)   # a model that never stops     assert len(s.log) == 3 and s.budgets["a"] == 103 def test_denied_and_pending_actions_do_not_change_budgets():     s = State({"a": 100, "b": 50})     g = Guardrails({"a", "b"}, 500)     seq = iter([call("a", 150), call("a", 600), None])   # needs approval, then breaks the cap     run(lambda st: next(seq), s, g)     assert s.budgets["a"] == 100 and len(s.pending) == 1

Run them with pytest. In a real deployment, the model function would call your provider with the state, the goal and the tool schemas, then return the parsed tool call.

● It handles one tool. A real agent needs several, such as reporting, pausing ads and requesting approval.

● It keeps state in memory. Production systems need a durable store and idempotent actions so a retry cannot apply a change twice.

● The thresholds are placeholders. Set your own caps and percentages from your spend and risk tolerance.

● It validates the call but not the data. If upstream numbers are wrong, the agent will act on them.

Runaway spend. Budget APIs act instantly and changes compound. Without a hard ceiling in your own code, a reasoning error becomes a billing event.

Dirty upstream data. Stale CRM records, a broken product feed or a misattributed conversion. The agent can act confidently on wrong inputs and leave a tidy audit trail of well-reasoned bad decisions.

Missing audit trails. If you cannot reconstruct why an agent acted, you cannot fix or defend it. Deloitte's State of AI in the Enterprise reports that close to three-quarters of companies plan to deploy agentic AI within two years, and that only 21% of those companies report having a mature model for agent governance.

● Least-privilege credentials. A separate API key per agent, scoped to exactly what it needs, never a shared admin token.

● Hard caps in code. Daily spend ceilings and a maximum percentage change per adjustment, enforced outside the model.

● Human-in-the-loop thresholds. Define which actions always need approval.

● Log every tool call. Inputs, outputs, errors, model and prompt version, with timestamps you can query.

● Start read-only. Run in suggest mode and compare recommendations with what your team would have done.

A system that pairs a language model with real tools and lets it pursue a goal. It reads state, decides on an action, executes it, observes the result and decides again.

Automation starts from a trigger and follows branches defined in advance. An agent starts from a goal and selects tools at runtime.

Not on current evidence. They handle high-volume, measurable, reversible work well. Strategy, positioning and final brand approval still sit with people.

Function calling, where tools are declared by schema and the model returns matching arguments, and increasingly the Model Context Protocol for connecting tools.

It depends on how quickly errors are detected, whether actions are reversible and what a bad decision costs. Automate the cheap and reversible; supervise the rest.

Wrong tool calls, runaway spend, confident action on dirty data and brand drift at scale. Missing audit trails make all of them harder to diagnose.

An AI marketing agent is less magic than it sounds: a goal, a set of tools and a loop with limits. The model's intelligence matters, but the system around it decides whether it is safe to run. Define the tools narrowly, enforce limits in code, log everything, start read-only and widen autonomy only as the evidence supports it.

#10266. PENN’s Interactive revenue rise rests on partner taxes as gaming revenue slips 2.1%

PENN Entertainment's online segment grew revenue in the third quarter, but the gain came from passed-through partner gaming taxes, not from players. The segment now needs a positive fourth quarter to reach a full-year target that has already been lowered.

#10262. Your Site Loads, but Google Can't Crawl It: Catching robots.txt Mistakes After a Migration

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A website migration can look finished the moment the new site loads in your browser. Pages render, links work and the design looks right. Then, a few weeks later, rankings start slipping and new pages stop appearing in search results.

That's what happened when the Islamic EdTech platform Usmani Academy moved from WordPress to a custom HTML build. Everything worked for human visitors, but the production robots.txt file, generated through an API during the rebuild, was restricting search engine crawlers. The fix itself took minutes. Finding the cause took longer, because nobody thought to look at a file that's easy to forget once a site is live.

This article walks through why this kind of problem happens, how to spot it, and how to add a few automated checks so it can't slip through on your next launch.

During development, it's standard practice to keep a staging site out of search results. Teams often do this with a blanket rule in robots.txt:

Those two lines tell every compliant crawler not to request any URL on the site. That's exactly what you want on a staging server and exactly what you don't want in production.

The risk grows when robots.txt isn't a hand-edited file anymore. In a custom build, it might be generated by a framework, a build step, a CMS setting or, as in Usmani Academy's case, an API. If the generator's defaults or environment variables carry the staging configuration into production, the restrictive rules go live silently. Nothing looks broken, because browsers ignore robots.txt completely.

It's worth being precise here, because the two are often confused:

The distinction matters when you fix things. If a page is blocked in robots.txt, Google can't fetch it, which also means it can't see a noindex tag on it. So if your goal is to keep a page out of search results, use noindex and leave the page crawlable. If your goal is to get pages crawled again after a bad launch, check robots.txt first.

A crawler block rarely announces itself. The signs tend to build gradually:

When Usmani Academy's rankings dropped, the first instinct was to look at content and keywords. The breakthrough came from asking a more basic question: can Google actually crawl the migrated site?

Start with the file itself. Fetch the live version from the command line, not from your source code, since the deployed file is the one that matters:

Look for any Disallow: / under User-agent: *, and for rules that block important folders such as /blog/ or /courses/.

Next, rule out an indexing block in the HTTP headers and HTML:

Finally, confirm what Google sees. In Search Console, open the robots.txt report under Settings to see the version Google last fetched, then run a live test in the URL Inspection tool on a few key pages.

Once you've found a blocking rule, correct it at the source. If the file is generated by an API, build step or CMS setting, fixing the deployed file by hand won't last: the next deployment will overwrite it.

A permissive production robots.txt can be very short:

An empty Disallow: line allows crawling of everything. Add specific Disallow rules only for areas that genuinely shouldn't be crawled, such as internal search results or admin paths.

Then be patient. Google has to recrawl and reprocess the site before rankings can recover. In Usmani Academy's case, indexing began recovering after the fix, and rankings improved gradually over the following weeks rather than bouncing back immediately.

The most reliable fix is one that doesn't depend on anyone remembering. A small script can fail a deployment, or at least raise an alert, when production is about to block all crawlers:

Run it against production right after each deployment, or against a preview URL that uses production settings before you go live. It won't catch every possible problem, but it will catch the single most damaging one.

Before and immediately after launching a migrated site, check:

A migration isn't finished when the site loads in your browser. It's finished when search engines can crawl and index it as well. Usmani Academy's experience shows how one generated file can quietly undo the visibility a site has built up. Treat robots.txt as part of your production configuration, test what's actually deployed, and automate the check so it happens on every release.

For more examples of how technical issues like this affect search visibility, see these SEO case studies from the Usmani Academy project and others.

#10257. From AI-Generated HTML to a WordPress Child Theme: A Practical Workflow

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

AI code editors like Cursor make it quick to produce a polished static page. Describe a hero section, an about block and a contact form, and you'll have working HTML, CSS and JavaScript in an afternoon. The trouble starts when that page needs to live inside WordPress. WordPress doesn't serve a folder of HTML files; it builds pages from PHP templates, a theme hierarchy and its own content system.

This tutorial shows a lightweight way to bridge that gap: wrap your generated front end in a child theme of an existing parent theme, then deploy it with nothing more than a ZIP upload. It's the approach SEO specialist Shumaila Asghar used to build her portfolio website, and it works well for small sites where you want full control of the design without building a theme from scratch.

You could paste your HTML into a page builder block or edit the parent theme's files directly. Both approaches cause problems later. Page builders often strip or rewrite markup, and any edits made to a parent theme are overwritten the next time it updates.

A child theme inherits everything from its parent, including templates, styles and features, and lets you override only what you need. For this workflow, a lightweight, well-maintained parent such as Kadence works well: it handles the essentials like menus, accessibility features and responsive defaults, while your child theme supplies the custom design.

Before touching WordPress, finish the front end as a static page. Plan each section up front (for a portfolio, that might be hero, about, skills, services, experience, projects and contact), then generate and refine the code in your editor.

Test it locally on desktop and mobile. Check navigation, spacing, button states and readability. It's much easier to fix layout bugs now, when you're dealing with plain HTML and CSS, than after the code is split across PHP templates.

One habit worth forming: read the code the AI produces rather than accepting it wholesale. You'll need to understand its structure in the next step.

A minimal child theme needs just two files. Create a folder, for example portfolio-child, with this structure:

The style.css file starts with a header comment that tells WordPress about the theme. The Template line must match the parent theme's folder name exactly:

#10245. US Treasury financial crimes unit becomes latest fed breach victim

FinCEN takes systems offline and extends filing deadlines while investigating 'unauthorized access'

The US Treasury's Financial Crimes Enforcement Network (FinCEN) has taken some systems offline following unauthorized access, disrupting regulatory filings for financial institutions.

An alert has gone up on FinCEN's Bank Secrecy Act E-Filing website, as shown in this archived copy, informing those who need to submit reports that they might not be able to while the agency deals with the situation. FinCEN not only enforces compliance with the Bank Secrecy Act and associated regulations but also collects and analyzes financial intelligence for sharing with other US agencies and foreign government partners.

In other words, it has some juicy info in its databases.

“FinCEN is investigating a cybersecurity incident that resulted in unauthorized access to a limited subset of its systems,” the alert states. “As a proactive measure, FinCEN has taken some systems offline.”

FinCEN hasn't said which systems intruders accessed, but clues in the deadline extension notice the agency posted alongside the warning suggest the disruption affects its reporting services for banks and other financial institutions required to submit reports detailing certain financial transactions and suspicious activity that may indicate money laundering or other illicit activity.

FinCEN's Secure Data Transfer Mode, one method of submitting those reports, appears to be affected, as do services companies use to submit beneficial ownership information reports and individuals use to update their FinCEN identifiers. Eligible organizations and individuals with filings or updates due from November 18 through December 29 now have until December 30 to submit them.

The Register has learned that there’s no evidence of any unauthorized access to Bank Secrecy Act data or beneficial ownership databases. That said, the investigation is ongoing - so that could change.

“Treasury is working with the Federal Bureau of Investigation, the Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency, and third-party forensic examiners to determine the scope and impact of the incident,” a FinCEN official told us in an email.

Several US government agencies have experienced cybersecurity incidents in recent months, including the ATF and the FBI, and reports emerged this week that intruders had also compromised an unclassified CIA system. While US authorities have linked previous cyberespionage campaigns to Chinese or other government-backed groups, financially motivated criminal groups have also targeted federal agencies.

As the FinCEN breach is still under investigation, the agency hasn’t stated who may be involved and for what purpose.  ®

#10242. A Preflight Checklist for Shipping 3D Models on the Web

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A 3D model that looks smooth in a desktop modeling application can become a very different experience inside a web page. The browser must download the asset, decode its geometry and textures, upload resources to the GPU, compile materials and render the scene while the rest of the page stays responsive.

Consider an ecommerce team adding a rotatable chair model to a product page. The asset works well on a developer's laptop, but the first mobile test shows a long blank interval, a sharp increase in memory use and uneven rotation. The problem is not necessarily the JavaScript viewer. The model itself may contain more data than the page can comfortably deliver.

A preflight process catches these issues before the asset is connected to production code. This checklist walks through it, and includes a small, tested script that audits a GLB file against a budget. Tool details change, so check each project's documentation for current behavior.

For the chair, rotation may help shoppers inspect the rear frame, arm shape and the relationship between the seat and legs. That is a clear use. Adding the same model as a moving background behind the product description would add download and rendering costs without equivalent value.

Define the purpose before setting a performance budget. A featured product viewer may justify more detail than a small decorative object. The decision also affects whether the model should load immediately, wait until the user scrolls near it or appear only after someone selects "View in 3D."

Editable authoring files often contain information a browser does not need, so web delivery should use an asset prepared for runtime rather than the original scene from a modeling application.

The Khronos glTF specification describes glTF as a format designed for efficient transmission and loading of 3D scenes and models. A .gltf asset can reference separate buffers and images, while a binary .glb file can package the scene and its resources into one file.

GLB is convenient when a team wants a single deployable asset, but a single container does not guarantee a small payload. Large textures, dense geometry, unused animations and unnecessary scene objects can still make the file expensive. Document the format decision along with supported materials, texture limits, coordinate conventions and animation requirements.

Before debugging React components, shaders or loading logic, confirm that the file itself behaves as expected. Several free viewers can open a model in the browser for a quick visual check:

Whichever you use, rotate the chair, zoom in and inspect it from above, below and behind. This check can reveal obvious problems:

A viewer is useful for visual inspection, but it is not a performance profiler or automatic repair tool. A model can display correctly while still being too heavy for the intended page. Viewers also differ in lighting and material handling, so confirm the final look in your own renderer.

Open the model in an appropriate inspection or editing tool and review what it contains. A product model should not carry unused lights, cameras, hidden prototypes, duplicate meshes or animation clips left over from production. These elements can increase file size, complicate traversal or create confusing behavior after import.

Check the object hierarchy as well. Hundreds of separately transformed parts can increase CPU work and draw-call overhead even if the total polygon count looks reasonable. At the same time, combining every surface into one mesh can make material changes or interaction difficult. If users can change the chair's upholstery and frame independently, those areas need useful separation. Decorative components that never change may not.

Triangle count matters, but it is only one part of the cost. A model with moderate geometry and several large uncompressed textures may use more memory than a denser mesh with efficient textures. Transparent materials, multiple lights, shadows and many separate materials can also affect rendering performance.

Establish budgets by device class and use case rather than copying one universal limit. A hero asset viewed full-screen has different requirements from twelve models displayed in a product grid.

Several of those numbers can be read straight from the file. The Node.js script below reads a GLB's JSON chunk, counts triangles, meshes, primitives, materials, textures, images, animations and cameras, and compares them with a budget. It has no dependencies and runs on current Node.js versions.

The test builds a small GLB in memory, confirms the triangle count (a line primitive is ignored), and checks that a too-low triangle budget, a stray camera and an unwanted animation are all flagged:

Run the test with node test_glb_budget.mjs, and audit a real file with node glb_budget.mjs chair.glb.

Textures often account for much of a 3D asset's download and memory footprint. Inspect every map and ask whether its resolution matches the size at which the surface will actually appear. A 4K texture may be wasteful if the chair occupies only a few hundred pixels on a phone. Multiple surfaces may be able to share a smaller atlas, and detail that is never visible can be removed.

Do not lower every texture to the same resolution automatically. The seat fabric may need more detail than the underside of the frame. Test reductions on the real page at normal viewing distances.

Material count also deserves attention. A separate material can produce another rendering operation even when it differs only slightly from another surface. Consolidate materials where doing so does not remove required customization.

A product page should not delay its primary text, price, photographs and purchase controls while downloading a below-the-fold 3D asset. Use an explicit interaction or an IntersectionObserver to begin loading when the viewer approaches the viewport. A simplified pattern looks like this:

The positive margin starts the request shortly before the viewer becomes visible. The appropriate value depends on page layout, model size and expected network speed. Display a meaningful poster image while loading, so the page stays useful if the asset fails, WebGL is unavailable or the user prefers not to spend bandwidth on the interactive view.

Using the device's full pixel ratio can make a canvas unnecessarily expensive on high-density screens. Cap the rendering resolution and evaluate the visual difference on actual devices. Shadows, environment maps, antialiasing and post-processing should also be treated as adjustable costs. A desktop may use the complete presentation, while a lower-powered phone receives simpler lighting and reduced texture resolution.

Avoid guessing from a user-agent string. Where possible, choose conservative defaults, observe runtime performance and let the experience degrade gracefully. Pause rendering when the viewer is outside the viewport or the browser tab is hidden, since a product model does not need to consume GPU time while the customer reads specifications farther down the page.

A model that performs well in isolation may struggle when combined with analytics scripts, image carousels, embedded video and the rest of the production page. Test on real phones, not only through desktop emulation, and include a mid-range device and a throttled network. Measure the time until the poster appears, the time until the model becomes interactive, frame consistency during rotation, and whether the page stays responsive during decoding.

Use browser performance and network tools to separate bottlenecks. A slow download suggests a payload problem. A long main-thread task may point to parsing or scene initialization. Low frame rates after loading may indicate excessive draw calls, shader cost, overdraw or high rendering resolution. Repeat the test after each significant optimization, because a smaller file is useful only if the model still communicates the product accurately.

Interactive content should enhance the product page rather than become its only usable representation. Provide well-composed images, descriptive product text and accessible controls alongside the viewer. Keyboard users should be able to reach and operate relevant controls, while motion should not begin unexpectedly or continue without purpose. If the 3D experience fails, the customer should still be able to understand the chair and complete the intended task.

GLB packages everything in one file, which is convenient to deploy. Separate .gltf files let you cache buffers and images independently. Neither guarantees a small payload.

No. A viewer confirms how a model looks, not how it performs. Measure size, textures, draw calls and load time in the real page.

There is no universal number. Set budgets by device class and by how prominent the model is on the page, then test on a mid-range phone.

Shipping 3D on the web is ultimately a delivery problem. The model, textures, loading strategy, renderer, device and surrounding page all contribute to the result. A disciplined preflight process prevents teams from treating a successful desktop preview as proof of production readiness, and gives users the benefits of interactive 3D without making the rest of the page pay an unreasonable price.

#10239. HP sees premium-priced AI PCs as its meal ticket in a shrinking market

PC shipments set to fall further thanks to the memory shortage, but high-margin models for AI at the edge keep profits up

HP Inc expects the PC market to shrink by "high single to low double digits" in calendar 2027, squeezed by the memory chip crisis, but execs reckon the corporation can still gain share by focusing on premium categories and AI PCs.

Interim CEO Bruce Broussard said HP would do this "by capitalizing on the growing shift of AI workloads to the edge and leveraging our industry-leading portfolio designed to meet the needs of users with more demanding workloads."

The pitch echoes the previous quarter's earnings call. "Our focus is on helping customers implement and manage AI across their environments. We believe that the future of AI is hybrid with workloads running both in the cloud and closer to where people work and data is created," he added.

Broussard cited IDC estimates that half of enterprise AI inference workloads will run on endpoints or edge nodes by 2030, and claimed that with half a billion devices shipped, HP has "deep expertise in how devices perform, how they're secured and how they're managed at scale."

HP fourth quarter revenue - for the three months ended October 31 - was $16 billion, up 9.3 percent year-on-year. Full-year fiscal 2026 revenue was up 9.5 percent to $60 billion. Personal Systems drove the growth, as its printer business slipped slightly.

Personal Systems revenue grew 14 percent on commercial and advanced compute sales, and AI PCs represented nearly 60 percent of HP’s shipments in the quarter.

"That said, higher component costs and challenging pricing environment, particularly in consumer, pressured profitability," said Broussard.

Like its rivals, HP is leaning on higher-margin premium models and signing long-term deals with the memory chip makers to secure supplies. Nikkei Asia named HP among the firms buying a limited amount of memory from China’s ChangXin Memory Technologies (CXMT).

Asked whether it planned to procure more Chinese chippery, HP’s CFO Karen Parkhill sidestepped the question: "We don't expect any material changes in our suppliers right now. We're confident in the suppliers that we have, and we are confident in the supply that we have what we need to fulfil the demand this year."

She conceded that "We are using suppliers from all over the world."

Parkhill said HP expects memory and storage costs to continue rising, but at a slower rate than during this last fiscal year. "We also expect CPU costs to increase, but not as much as what we've seen with memory and storage. And all of that is baked into our outlook."

Broussard expects Q4 to prove the low point for Personal Systems margins, with improvement coming from further optimization of its product design and component configurations.

In Ferbruary, HP said memory and storage costs had grown from 15 to 18 percent of the PC bill of materials at the start of the year to a figure closer to 40 percent. Parkhill declined to update the figure, saying the moderating cost curves and fast-changing production mix make the BOM percentage "less useful."

HP’s newly hired CEO, former GSK boss Emma Walmsley, will start work at the company on January 4, leading it through what continues to be uncertain times for the PC industry. ®

#10227. Unlocking Stable On-Policy RL on TPU with Bitwise-Zero Train–Inference Mismatch

Reinforcement learning (RL) for large language models is notoriously sensitive to numerical instability: even tiny discrepancies between rollout generation and gradient updates can destabilize training or stall convergence. In LLM RL, the model serves as the policy, π(action | previous tokens), and true on-policy learning requires every action in an update to be generated and evaluated by that exact same policy. While a synchronous loop ensures each rollout batch is scored using the same weights that generated it, a critical numerical requirement remains: the sampler and trainer must evaluate that policy identically.

In practice, production RL stacks run sampling and training through separate, specialized execution paths. The sampler chooses each action token during incremental decoding and records its behavior logprob. The trainer evaluates the same token in batched forward-backward passes to compute its current logprob and gradients. Under the same weights, these differing execution paths can still assign different logprobs to the same token and context. The training schedule is on-policy, but the numerical execution has introduced a hidden off-policy gap that compounds over multi-turn trajectories.

Our zero train–inference mismatch (Zero-TIM) implementation, developed in Tunix and verified at multi-host scale across 64 TPU v5p chips (DP8×TP8), closes this numerical gap. At the same weights, the sampler and trainer assign bitwise-identical logprobs to the same action and token history—bringing state-of-the-art, true on-policy stability to TPU RL training.

A reduction is a tree of floating-point additions. Floating-point addition is not associative, so changing the tree changes where rounding occurs. The effect is easier to expose in bfloat16, which retains less precision than float32.

The following bfloat16 example isolates the effect of reduction order:

The same issue appears in model reductions: matrix products accumulate partial sums, normalization computes statistics, and tensor-parallel shards combine partial outputs. In our original stack, sampler decode and trainer forward used different token-array shapes and forward implementations. JAX specializes computations to their input shapes, and the training forward also runs inside a differentiated program.

Across these paths, reduction order and intermediate precision were not consistently aligned. Floating-point rounding turned those arithmetic differences into different logprob bits. The weights and model equations were identical; the arithmetic was not.

The contract is simple: across sampler decode, serving prefill, and trainer forward, all three paths must produce bitwise-identical logprobs for every sampled action—comparing raw floating-point bytes under the same rollout weights, token history, and sampling settings, with zero rounding or tolerance.

Why three paths instead of two? In a decoupled RL stack, a direct sampler–trainer disagreement shows that a numerical gap exists, but not where it enters. Introducing serving prefill as a middle checkpoint turns alignment into a systematic two-step diagnostic that any RL stack can adopt:

Sampler decode vs. prefill verifies that the serving engine agrees with itself between incremental token generation and full-sequence re-scoring, while prefill vs. trainer verifies that the serving and training engines agree across compilation and differentiation boundaries.

Achieving Zero-TIM does not require merging the sampler and trainer into a single monolithic engine. Instead, we use a generalizable four-part recipe that bridges separate serving and training pipelines across multi-host TPU meshes: locking within-shard kernel accumulation, ordering cross-shard reductions, standardizing the logprob scoring tail, and wrapping serving kernels with custom differentiation rules.

Inside each tensor-parallel rank, batch-invariant Pallas kernels control accumulation order and intermediate precision. Projection kernels traverse fixed contraction blocks in order, keeping the reduction blocking independent of token-row count. Partial products accumulate in float32, with explicit output casts.

The production kernel initializes its scratch accumulator before the tile traversal, then applies this core to each tile:

The next tile continues from the float32 accumulator, not the cast output. This construction covers the attention and MLP projections and the LM head. Dedicated kernels fix RMSNorm reductions and SwiGLU’s intermediate casts. The existing Pallas paged-attention forward is retained.

For row-parallel projections, each tensor-parallel rank produces a partial output. We separate communication from addition: ppermute transports the partials, then every rank adds them in the same source-rank order.

The reduction order is explicit in the program rather than selected by a collective implementation.

Alignment must extend beyond model logits to the probability of the sampled action. Decode, prefill, and trainer apply matching sampling transforms, use the same fixed Pallas log-softmax construction, and select the recorded action token.

Prefill and trainer score the action generated during rollout rather than choosing another action. We also preserve sampled token IDs across environment turns so that every path evaluates the same token history.

The trainer reuses the serving prefill computation. Custom vector–Jacobian products retain the serving kernels for forward evaluation and provide their backward rules. The matmul example shows this separation:

The differentiable replica mirrors the kernel’s accumulation and casts, but is used only to construct the pullback. Forward values still come from the serving Pallas kernel.

Training places that forward inside a differentiated JAX program. We pass --xla_allow_excess_precision=false through XLA_FLAGS to the compiler process, constraining excess intermediate precision in the operations surrounding the Pallas kernels.

Long trajectories are evaluated in fixed-size chunks, carrying the paged KV cache forward. All action logprobs feed the complete train-batch RL loss. The reverse pass propagates gradients through those cache connections and combines parameter-gradient contributions before the optimizer update.

Across the AI systems community, eliminating train–inference mismatch has rapidly become a state-of-the-art requirement for stabilizing large-scale RL convergence. Recent open-source efforts—including vLLM with TorchTitan and RadixArk/LMSys Miles—have demonstrated how batch-invariant kernels and bitwise numerical alignment prevent off-policy drift during policy optimization.

Our work delivers an open-source, modular JAX/XLA and Pallas implementation of this contract for TPU in Tunix. Verified across a multi-host 64 TPU v5p (DP8×TP8) mesh, these kernel and collective patterns provide a reusable blueprint that teams can adopt to achieve bit-exact on-policy RL across distributed JAX/TPU stacks.

To benchmark our TPU kernels, we run a 200-step numerical verification test on the synthetic FrozenLake environment using a public Qwen3-8B checkpoint as a baseline test case using 64 TPU v5p chips with a DP8×TP8 mesh. The recipe uses a GSPO-token loss with leave-one-out advantages. We compare Standard training with trainer-old logprobs and no additional sampler correction, token-level truncated importance sampling (TIS), and Zero-TIM.

The Zero-TIM configuration also uses token-in/token-out (TiTO) handling to preserve sampled token IDs across environment turns. The comparison runs retain their existing tokenization paths.

Zero-TIM records zero mean absolute sampler–trainer logprob difference at every logged step in this window, while its training solve rate increases. Standard and TIS retain nonzero differences: TIS adjusts their contribution to the update without removing the underlying mismatch. Zero-TIM reaches higher observed solve rates in these runs.

We developed Zero-TIM prototype as part of Tunix, Google's open-source, JAX-native library for LLM RL and post-training on Cloud TPU. The kernel patterns and three-way verification contract are modular by design—making them straightforward to adopt across existing multi-host JAX/TPU serving and training pipelines—and we are actively extending support to linear-attention and Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architectures.

#10225. How AI-Powered Cameras Are Changing Content Creation: What Developers Should Know

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Creating high-quality visual content once meant specialized cameras, technical knowledge and hours of editing. Today, software does a growing share of that work. Cameras recognize subjects, adjust exposure, stabilize footage and hand clips to editing tools that transcribe, reframe and tag them.

Digital photography made images easy to capture, review and share. Smartphones then folded capture, editing and publishing into one device. The next shift was computational photography: instead of relying only on the lens and sensor, the device combines several captures and processes them in software.

High dynamic range (HDR) imaging is the clearest example. A camera captures frames at different exposures and merges them so both bright skies and dark foregrounds keep detail. Research indexed on PubMed, published in IEEE Transactions on Pattern Analysis and Machine Intelligence, examines how deep learning has advanced HDR imaging, particularly in reconstructing visual information across different exposure levels.

Machine learning extends this approach from merging pixels to understanding them, which is what lets a camera decide what is the subject and what is the background.

Most AI camera features fall into a few groups.

Because of these failure modes, a pipeline should check what the camera produced before running expensive downstream steps.

A cheap pre-check can reject a frame that is too soft or badly exposed before you spend time on transcription, upscaling or model inference. The script below uses numpy and Pillow. It measures sharpness as the variance of a Laplacian, a standard blur indicator, and measures exposure as the share of crushed and clipped pixels.

The test builds a detailed random frame and a blurred copy, then checks that the sharp frame scores far higher, that a mostly black frame is flagged as underexposed and that a mostly white frame is flagged as overexposed:

Run the tests with python3 test_frame_quality.py. In our run, the detailed test frame scored about 33,000 for sharpness and its blurred copy scored under 2.

Traditional capture means holding a camera, propping up a phone or setting up equipment. Wearable cameras let creators record from their own point of view while their hands stay free, which suits travel video, instructional content, outdoor activities and behind-the-scenes footage.

Camera-equipped smart glasses are one form of this. Several makers sell them, and consumer listings such as ai glasses with camera show how the category is presented to buyers. Depending on the device, users start recording with physical controls or voice commands. Specifications differ widely, so compare field of view, storage, battery life and software support before choosing one.

For developers, wearables change the input. Footage is shaky, framed by head movement and often recorded outdoors, which is exactly where a quality check and stabilization matter. Battery capacity, camera position, storage and recording duration also limit extended productions. Wearables are an additional option for perspectives that are hard to reach otherwise, not a replacement for traditional cameras.

Editing involves reviewing clips, correcting color, adjusting audio and preparing videos for each platform. AI tools automate several repetitive parts of that work:

Creative judgment stays human. Choosing meaningful moments, shaping narrative flow and keeping a consistent visual style are decisions software cannot reliably make. Real-time assistance during recording will depend on hardware, software and environmental conditions.

As cameras get smaller, responsible recording matters more. Wearable devices can capture footage without the obvious movements of a handheld camera, so people nearby may not know recording is happening. The European Data Protection Board has highlighted transparency, personal data protection and recording awareness as important considerations for camera-equipped smart glasses.

Authenticity is the second challenge. AI tools can enhance an image, but heavy alteration can also mislead. The National Institute of Standards and Technology has examined technical approaches to digital content transparency, including provenance tracking, watermarking and synthetic-content detection. On the provenance side, the C2PA coalition publishes an open standard called Content Credentials, which helps show where content came from and what edits it has undergone.

Cameras will likely get better at faster subject recognition, more efficient automated editing and stronger low-light performance as computer vision and on-device computing advance. Wearables may widen first-person storytelling, and editing tools may make multi-platform publishing simpler. Technical gains alone will not decide quality, though. Originality, storytelling and thoughtful creative decisions still do.

Not always. Toolkits such as ML Kit face detection and Apple's Vision framework run image analysis on the device, which reduces delay and keeps frames local.

It narrows the gap in many conditions, but optics, sensor size and control still matter for demanding work, and AI enhancement can introduce artifacts of its own.

Add an early quality gate that checks sharpness and exposure, as in the script above, and calibrate its thresholds on your own footage.

It depends on location and context. Regulators such as the European Data Protection Board have raised transparency and consent concerns, so check local rules and be open about recording.

Provenance standards such as C2PA Content Credentials aim to show a file's origin and edit history, though they only help when tools and platforms support them.

#10221. Microsoft's NTLM security changes trip up third-party logins

It's not our fault if you've implemented it wrong

Microsoft's November security updates can break authentication in some third-party applications and devices following changes to NTLM message signing.

Affected systems include authentication proxy servers, single sign-on (SSO) products, and application packaging tools, the company's support notice says.

The failures stem from products that claim to supply required information during NTLM authentication but return incomplete or empty data, Microsoft said: "An affected device might set the NTLMSSP_NEGOTIATE_TARGET_INFO flag in its CHALLENGE_MESSAGE but provide an empty or incomplete TargetInfo field."

Administrators can use the notice's network trace and event log examples to identify affected products. For confirmed cases, the temporary workaround is to set the ClientAllowUnsignedNtlm Registry value to 1 on affected clients. This stops the client offering signing, backing out the new default rather than fixing the incompatible product. The recommended long-term options are a vendor update or Kerberos authentication, where supported.

The value sits under HKEY_LOCAL_MACHINE\SYSTEM\CurrentControlSet\Control\Lsa\MSV1_0 and defaults to 0, enabling clients to offer signing on non-anonymous connections.

The NTLM changes arrived in the November 10 update as phase 1 in a 2-phase process. In phase 1, "Windows client devices begin offering NTLM signing on all connections by default. Servers accept both signed and unsigned clients. Auditing events identify clients not using signing."

Phase 2, scheduled for May 2027, will make Windows servers require NTLM signing by default. "Clients that do not offer signing will be rejected by secure servers," Microsoft stated.

Ominously, Microsoft warned that it "might remove the registry keys at a future date," so the company is not kidding about the temporary workaround thing.

The additional protections were added to address CVE-2026-118737, a vulnerability in which "improper verification of cryptographic signature in Windows Remote Procedure Call Runtime allows an unauthorized attacker to execute code over a network."

NTLM is on Microsoft's hit list, having joined the Deprecated Features list in 2024. It dates back to the era of Windows NT, and Microsoft has made clear that it wants customers to move to Kerberos, which became the default in 2000. Even so, NTLM lingers on.

Deprecation doesn't remove Microsoft's obligation to secure NTLM while it remains in supported Windows releases. For administrators, though, the immediate task is identifying incompatible products before temporary exceptions give way to stricter enforcement. ®

#10218. How to Build an Accessible Multi Step Form: A Practical Guide With a Vanilla JavaScript Example

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A long form can feel like a chore before anyone types a word. Splitting it into smaller steps lets people focus on a few related questions at a time.

The code looks simple: show one section, hide the rest and add a button. The difficult parts are validation, keyboard use, focus and what happens when JavaScript doesn't run. This guide builds an accessible multi step form with HTML and plain JavaScript. It also reproduces a bug that can leave visitors stuck on the first step.

Multiple steps make sense when questions fall into clear groups, such as project details, timing and contact information. A form with only an email address and a message usually doesn't need them.

Splitting a form doesn't make unnecessary questions useful. Remove those first. More steps also aren't a guarantee of more completed forms, so test the change with your own audience.

The W3C tutorial on multiple page forms advises dividing long forms into logical stages and telling users about their progress. Our example applies those ideas within a single page.

Without JavaScript, all three groups remain visible and Send works as an ordinary submit button. The Back button stays hidden because it has no job in that view. This approach is called progressive enhancement: start with a working form, then add the step behavior.

The action value is a placeholder. Point it to a real server endpoint in your project. If your stylesheet sets a display value on fieldsets, check that it doesn't override the hidden attribute.

Save the following code as form.js in the same folder:

The form has one submit button. Its label reads Continue until the last step. The submit handler decides whether to show an error, open the next step or let the browser send the data. Pressing Enter in a text field follows the same path.

Back is a regular button, so it never submits anything. The answers remain in their fields because the code only hides each step. It doesn't remove or rebuild it.

To test its purpose, I removed only this line, filled in the first field and pressed Enter in headless Chromium 158. The form stayed on step 1. The console reported messages like these:

Before firing the submit event, the browser checked the entire form. The empty required fields on later steps were invalid, but they were hidden. Submission was blocked, so our handler never had a chance to open step 2.

Setting noValidate stops that automatic check during submission. It doesn't disable checkValidity() or reportValidity(), so our code can validate only the visible step. Because the line lives in JavaScript, the browser's normal validation still applies if the script never loads.

For more detail on native validation rules and methods, see SitePoint's Complete Guide to HTML Forms and Constraint Validation.

The test intercepted the request, so it checked what the browser sent rather than a real server. It also doesn't show how other browsers behave.

Each legend includes the current step number, and the page title changes with it. After someone moves to another step, focus goes to the new legend. This gives keyboard users a clear starting point. The code doesn't move focus during the first page load.

The negative tabindex lets JavaScript focus a legend without adding it to the normal Tab order. Keep the focus outline visible, or replace it with an equally clear style. Screen reader behavior varies, so test with the browser and screen reader combinations your visitors use.

Native validation messages are useful for a small example. A production form may need persistent error text beside each field. If so, connect each message to its field with aria-describedby, set aria-invalid when needed and clear both after the error is fixed.

Also avoid asking for the same information twice. WCAG 2.2 Redundant Entry requires previously entered information needed again in the same process to be filled in or available to select. There are exceptions, including certain security needs and information that is no longer valid. This example alone doesn't make a whole website WCAG compliant.

Browser checks can be changed or bypassed. Your server should validate required fields, allowed choices, lengths and formats again. It should also handle spam, protect against cross site request forgery where relevant and escape stored values when displaying them.

Before release, try the complete form with only a keyboard. Check mobile layouts, zoom, browser autofill, Back and Forward navigation, slow connections and server errors. Make sure repeated clicks don't create duplicate records.

SitePoint's practical workflow for testing JavaScript forms is a helpful next step when you're preparing a broader test plan.

The example also leaves out conditional questions. If one answer can remove a later step, calculate the active steps before updating the progress label. Decide how to handle old answers from inactive branches, and enforce the same rules on the server.

Custom code gives you control over the markup and behavior, but your team must maintain validation, delivery, security and testing. A form builder can reduce the amount of interface code you manage. For example, LeadGen App lists multi step forms, conditional logic and website embedding among its features.

Whichever route you choose, test the finished form instead of relying on a feature list.

An accessible multi step form needs more than a few hidden sections. Start with semantic HTML, validate each visible step and keep progress and focus clear.

Pay special attention to required fields that appear later. A single hidden field can stop the first button from working, even when the visible answer is correct. Test that case, then check the whole journey with real users and a real server before launch.

#10214. Hera probe finds DART left a stadium-sized dent in asteroid Dimorphos

Next step is to move in for a closer look at the carnage

The European Space Agency's Hera probe has revealed a stadium-sized crater on Dimorphos, the asteroid NASA struck with its DART spacecraft in 2022.

The Double Asteroid Redirection Test was deliberately crashed into the asteroid on September 26, 2022. The original plan called for a European spacecraft – Asteroid Impact Mission (AIM) – to launch first and observe the before and after effects of DART. AIM was canceled, and the Hera mission launched in 2024.

DART was a success. Scientists were able to determine that the impact had indeed changed the orbit of Dimorphos. The question was what else had happened to the asteroid. Scientists spotted ejecta and debris following the DART impact, but the close-up observations by Hera have revealed the full extent of the collision.

Hera principal investigator Patrick Michel, Director of Research at CNRS / Observatoire de la Côte d'Azur, said: "Scientists debated whether Dimorphos had sustained an impact crater, changed its shape or stayed the same.

"Much to our surprise the answer turns out to be all three at once! The face struck by DART has been flattened into an impact crater the size of a sports stadium, which extends across the asteroid's entire width."

Scientists also noted that the asteroid is now tumbling in its orbit.

Hera is surveying the 151-meter-wide Dimorphos and its larger companion, the 730-meter-wide Didymos, from about 25 to 30 km away. It moves around the pair at roughly 12 cm/s, using thruster firings to trace rectangular paths rather than conventional circular orbits.

The plan is to approach closer, to about 13 km, to get higher-resolution snaps. A complicating factor is the discovery that hundreds of orbiting boulders surround the asteroids. Michael Kueppers, ESA's Hera mission scientist, said: "The team will have to take account of these objects in the coming weeks as the mission moves in closer."

Hera will deploy two CubeSats, Juventas and Milani, in early 2027. The Italian-led Milani will study surface composition and surrounding dust, while the Luxembourg-led Juventas will use radar to probe Dimorphos's interior before attempting a landing.

Scientists also want to measure Dimorphos's mass. Combined with the observed change in its orbit, that will help them assess how effectively DART transferred momentum to the asteroid and improve predictions for future deflection attempts.

Hera mission manager Ian Carnelli said: "NASA's DART mission demonstrated that we can change an asteroid's orbit. Hera is now revealing what happened to the asteroid itself. By combining these two missions, humanity is taking a decisive step towards making asteroid deflection a reliable tool for protecting our planet." ®
