#10213. Node.js Hosting Between Serverless and VPS: Predictable Costs Without Cold Starts

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

The first sign of trouble is rarely a crash. It is the invoice.

A small team ships a Node.js app on a serverless platform because it is quick to set up. Traffic grows, and the bill grows with it, in ways that are hard to forecast. Some visitors also wait a beat longer for their first response, because the function has to start up before it can answer.

The usual alternative is a virtual private server (VPS). The price is fixed, but the work is not. Someone has to patch the operating system (OS), configure the web server, renew SSL certificates and keep backups running.

Between the two sits a third option: managed hosting for Node.js. Here is how the three compare, and how to choose.

Platforms such as AWS Lambda charge for what runs. Lambda's event functions are priced on the number of requests and on duration, measured in GB-seconds, and the memory you choose sets the CPU and the cost per second.

That suits small or spiky workloads. A new app with little traffic can sit inside the free tier, which covers one million requests and 400,000 GB-seconds a month.

The surprises come with growth. Billed duration is multiplied by the number of requests, so even small inefficiencies in a handler can turn into significant costs at scale.

Then there is the cold start. When the platform has to create a new execution environment, the first request waits. AWS's own decision guide puts Lambda cold starts at roughly 100 milliseconds to over 1 second.

Since August 2025, AWS bills for that initialization (INIT) phase across all function configurations, so a cold start now shows up on the invoice as well as in the response time.

AWS's own analysis found they occur in under 1% of invocations, so steady traffic makes them far less. For a checkout page that does hit one, it is still a pause the customer feels.

There are fixes. Provisioned concurrency keeps functions initialized and ready, but it is billed for the time it is enabled. Newer options let one execution environment handle several requests at once, which means fewer cold starts.

Limits matter, too. A Lambda function can run for 15 minutes at most, and accounts start with a default of 1,000 concurrent executions. Long-running jobs need a different home.

A VPS fixes the pricing problem. A plan costs the same every month, whatever traffic does.

On an unmanaged VPS, the team owns everything above the hardware. That means operating system updates, a firewall, a web server such as NGINX in front of the app, a process manager such as PM2 to restart it if it crashes, SSL certificate renewals and backups that someone has to test.

None of it is exotic. All of it is a job. Left undone, it is how small teams end up with unpatched servers and no way to restore one.

For a team without a sysadmin, the real price of a VPS is the hours.

Managed Node.js hosting sits between the two. The app runs on a persistent infrastructure, so there is no scale-to-zero and no cold start between requests. The price is a flat monthly plan. The provider handles the work around it.

Cloudways Velocity is one example. It patches the OS, manages SSL and domain mapping, and runs backups automatically.

The stack is NGINX and PM2, pre-configured with caching, and PostgreSQL is provisioned on all plans. Deployment is by Git push with built-in CI/CD, and a WAF, malware protection, DDoS mitigation and Cloudflare Enterprise CDN are included.

It ships presets for 12 project types, including Next.js, Remix, Express, Fastify, Astro, Nuxt, Angular and n8n. Anything that builds to SSR or a static bundle and starts with a Node command can run under "Other". Runtime versions are selectable, with Node.js LTS 22.x and 24.x listed.

The trade-off is scaling. Velocity scales vertically: you pick a server size and move up when the app outgrows it. A traffic spike beyond that server's capacity is a capacity problem, not something the platform absorbs by adding instances.

Look at the traffic first. If it comes in bursts with long quiet gaps, serverless often wins. If it is steady, a server is usually simpler to reason about.

Take one handler and keep the assumptions the same throughout. Each request runs for half a second on 1 GB of memory. The rates are AWS's published x86 prices: $0.20 per million requests and $0.0000166667 per GB-second, after the free tier, as shown earlier.

Now compare Velocity's plans, which use a pay-as-you-go model billed on an hourly basis according to the selected plan, ranging from Starter at $20 a month (2 GB RAM, 2 vCPU, 5 apps) to Business at $400 (64 GB RAM, 16 vCPU, 150 apps). More importantly billing is strictly tied to the server plan rather than incoming traffic, a sudden spike in visits will not automatically raise your hourly compute rate.

The Lambda figures leave out API gateway charges, data transfer and logging. A single server has a ceiling, so load-test before assuming one plan carries 100 million requests.

And Velocity has metered extras: $0.033/GB for offsite backup storage, $0.02/GB for CDN bandwidth beyond the plan's allocation, and $1 per 10 GB of extra disk.

If you want to test managed hosting, you can try Cloudways Velocity during Black Friday with the promo code BFCM802 for 80% off for 2 months plus unlimited migrations, valid through December 4, 2026.

The point is the shape, not a winner. Serverless cost tracks usage. A server's cost tracks its size. Choose the one you can forecast.

The strongest case is also the most common one: steady traffic, a small team and a budget that has to be forecast.

When cloud hosting costs have to fit a budget, a flat monthly plan beats a meter. Velocity's plans give you Node.js hosting with predictable pricing, so finance can see the number in advance and it does not move with every spike.

Because the server stays on, there is no cold start between requests, and the first visitor after a quiet spell is not left waiting.

Cloudways says Velocity patches the operating system, manages SSL and domain mapping, and runs backups automatically. The people who built the product keep building it.

APIs, full-stack apps and automation workflows such as n8n need a process that stays up. A persistent server is built for exactly that.

When the app outgrows its server, you move up a size, and you can move back down if you don't need it.

For most small teams, that adds up to a simple choice. Velocity trades the theoretical ceiling of serverless for something many teams value more: costs they can forecast and an app that stays ready, without a DevOps hire.

Teams whose traffic is extreme, or who need code running at the edge, may still prefer a specialist platform, but that is the exception.

Serverless is strongest when work is irregular. A VPS is strongest when you want control and have the time. Managed Node.js hosting suits the large group in between: teams with steady traffic, a small headcount and no appetite for server upkeep.

Teams looking for managed hosting can explore Cloudways Velocity to deploy and scale Node.js applications efficiently.

#10208. Huawei and crew tout homegrown EDA tools to overcome China’s fab deficit

Chip design software leans heavily into 3D packaging and multi-node assemblies

Chinese telecom and IT giant Huawei boasted about its advancements in domestic electronic design automation (EDA) software this week, which it needs to accelerate the adoption of homegrown chip manufacturing.

EDA is an umbrella term for software, hardware, and services essential to the planning, design, and production of modern chips.

Today, Cadence Design Systems, Synopsys, and Siemens dominate the industry, but US export controls have restricted Huawei and other blacklisted Chinese businesses' access to their tools.

The US blacklisted Huawei back in 2019 over concerns the telecommunications equipment giant could be forced to inject backdoors into its gear.

Unable to access top EDA tools, Huawei’s chip division, HiSilicon, was forced to develop its own with the help of Phlexing Technology.

This week, Phlexing chairman Qing He detailed the company’s efforts to bring new EDA tools to support Huawei’s so-called Tau Scaling Law design philosophy.

“Tau EDA is the domestic EDA tool chain for Tau chips. It comes from a long, close partnership with Huawei,” he said.

We discussed the tech earlier this year, but in a nutshell, it involves using advanced packaging, specifically 3D hybrid bonding, to achieve higher transistor density for a given area.

The TSMC of China, Semiconductor Manufacturing International Corporation (SMIC), is still years behind Taiwan, South Korea, and the US on process technology. It currently has the ability to mass-produce chips on the equivalent of a 7 nm process.

For comparison, TSMC began producing 5 nm-class products in 2020 and commenced mass production of 2 nm-class chips in late 2025.

So, Huawei aims to use 3D packaging to close the gap, but for this to happen, it first needs tools capable of designing such chips, and that’s just what Phlexing claims to have done with its GloryBolt and GloryEX platforms.

“Traditional EDA has long handled only single processes. Tau EDA needs to integrate and decompose multiple processes,” He said. “Coupling multi-scale, multi-structure, and multi-physics issues called for new analysis tools.”

This, He says, culminated in the co-development of a process design kit (PDK) with Huawei beginning in 2024. These technologies have now gone live.

The development comes as Huawei looks to bring larger, more capable processors to market.

We previously discussed the company’s latest developments around AI accelerators, near packaged optics, networking, and rack systems. Many of these advancements will depend on multi-die chip packages, enabled by EDA tools, like those built by Phlexing. ®

#10207. How to Shut Down a Node.js API Without Dropping Requests

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Every deploy, autoscaling event and node drain ends the same way for a running Node.js process: the platform sends it SIGTERM and waits. What happens next decides whether your users notice the deploy.

If the process has no handler for that signal, it exits on the spot. Any request that was halfway through, say a report that takes three seconds of database work, never gets a response. Here is what that looks like with a plain Express app and no signal handling:

Exit code 143 is 128 plus 15, the number of SIGTERM: the process was killed by the signal rather than exiting on its own. The client gets an empty reply, and if that request was writing data, nobody knows whether the write finished.

A graceful shutdown fixes this by draining the process: it stops taking new work, finishes what it already started, closes its connections and then exits. This tutorial builds that sequence for an Express 5 API backed by PostgreSQL, tests it against a real database, and covers the container and Kubernetes details that decide whether the signal ever reaches your code.

The order of operations matters more than any single line of code. A process that receives SIGTERM should go through four steps:

The first step is the one most implementations skip. If the server closes immediately, requests that the load balancer routes in the next second or two get connection errors, which is exactly what graceful shutdown was supposed to prevent.

Create an ES module project and install Express 5 and node-postgres:

Two timings drive the whole sequence. DRAIN_DELAY_MS is how long the process keeps serving after it fails readiness. It should be longer than your load balancer's health-check interval multiplied by its failure threshold. SHUTDOWN_TIMEOUT_MS is the hard deadline for the whole shutdown, and it must be shorter than the time your platform waits before sending SIGKILL.

The sleep helper comes from Node's promise-based timers API, so the shutdown code can await a delay without wrapping setTimeout by hand.

The middleware handles a detail that is easy to miss. HTTP clients reuse connections with keep-alive, so a client that already has a socket open to this instance would keep sending requests down it. A Connection: close header on the response tells the client to close that socket and open a new one, which the load balancer will route to a healthy instance.

Liveness stays green during shutdown. The process isn't broken; it's leaving on purpose, and a failing liveness probe could get it restarted in the middle of draining.

Shutdown code rarely gets tested, because it only runs on deploys. You can test it locally with curl and kill. Start the server with a short drain delay, start the slow request, and send SIGTERM while it is running:

Here is the timeline from a run against PostgreSQL 16 on Node.js 22:

Everything the sequence promised shows up in order. Readiness failed within a third of a second, the instance kept answering during the drain delay, new connections were refused once the server closed, and the request that was already running finished with a 200 before the process exited cleanly. Compare that with the empty reply and exit code 143 at the start of this article.

All of this only works if SIGTERM actually arrives at the Node.js process. In containers, that is not a given.

The Dockerfile reference explains the difference between the shell and exec forms. With the shell form, CMD node server.js, Docker starts /bin/sh -c as the main process, and the shell does not pass SIGTERM on to Node.js. Your handler never runs, docker stop waits out its grace period (10 seconds by default), and then the container is killed. For the same reason, start node directly rather than through an npm script or a shell wrapper, so there is no extra process between the platform and your code.

The first process in a container runs as PID 1, which the Linux kernel treats specially: signals whose default action is to terminate are ignored unless the process installs a handler. The code above installs handlers, so Node.js shuts down correctly as PID 1. But any code path that relies on default signal behavior won't, and PID 1 also has to reap orphaned child processes. Running the container with docker run --init puts a tiny init process in front of Node.js that forwards signals and reaps zombies, and it costs nothing to add.

When Kubernetes deletes a pod, it removes the pod from Service endpoints and sends SIGTERM at roughly the same time, not one after the other. That race is the reason for the drain delay: for a few seconds after the signal, traffic can still arrive. After the grace period, 30 seconds by default, the kubelet sends SIGKILL. The pod termination documentation describes the full sequence, including preStop hooks, which run before SIGTERM and count toward the same grace period.

The timings need to nest inside each other:

With a 2-second readiness period and a failure threshold of 1, a 5-second drain delay leaves a comfortable margin for the pod to leave rotation. The 20-second shutdown deadline sits well inside the 30-second grace period, so the process exits on its own terms instead of being killed. If your slowest legitimate request takes longer than about 15 seconds, raise terminationGracePeriodSeconds along with the deadline rather than letting those requests be cut off.

Graceful shutdown is about forty lines of code, and it runs on every single deploy. Getting it right means a release is something your users never notice, which is exactly how a release should feel.

#10200. The security of US water systems is even leakier than we thought

And intruders don't always need to exploit bugs to break in

American water systems are even less secure than we thought, according to a study of 236 technology vendors that provide gear to US drinking water, wastewater, and industrial water systems.

The federal government has issued 4,014 security advisories for industrial control systems and cataloged 1,792 vulnerabilities known to have been exploited in cyberattacks. A new index, called Water Supply Chain Watch and published by exposure management firm Securin, however, found almost twice as many vulnerabilities as public records identified – including 20 exploited flaws that weren't even in the federal government's Known Exploited Vulnerabilities (KEV) catalog.

The security provider compared data on water-tech vendors with records from the US Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency (CISA), the Environmental Protection Agency (EPA), the FBI, and WaterISAC, drawing on CISA advisory data through November 20 and KEV catalog entries through November 23.

It found that federal water advisories hit their highest level this year since 2020.

However, the volume of security alerts isn't a reliable predictor of real-world attacks, the researchers noted.

Five vendors – Rockwell Automation (58), Advantech (48), Schneider Electric (35), Siemens (26), and ABB (24) – account for 191 of the 376 water-tagged security advisories. The No. 2 firm, Advantech, accounted for the highest number of vulnerabilities rated critical, but none of the CVEs in its advisories appear in CISA's KEV catalog. Unitronics, on the other hand, accounted for only two water-tagged security advisories, but at least 75 of its controllers were compromised between November 2023 and January 2024 by attackers taking advantage of default or missing passwords.

The report also highlights another disconnect between water-related CVEs and real-world cyberattacks, which don't always require attackers to exploit software bugs. The July intrusions, which security researchers suspect were the work of Iran-linked attackers, targeted more than 100 water systems, with incidents reported across at least 12 states, and involved poorly secured internet-connected controllers.

According to Securin, 791 Rockwell MicroLogix 1400 controllers across multiple industries in the US were exposed to the internet as of November 17.

Additionally, research by Forescout cited by the security shop found that most exposed Rockwell controllers in the US – seven in 10 – sit on mobile carrier networks, where poorly secured cellular modems can provide an entry point for attackers. A CISA advisory from July describes this technique:

The authoring agencies observed Iranian-affiliated APT actors using several foreign-based IP addresses to access internet-facing PLCs manufactured by Rockwell Automation/Allen-Bradley, Schneider Electric, Siemens, and potentially other manufactured PLCs [T0883]. The actors used leased, third-party hosted infrastructure and manufacturers’ PLC programming software to connect to misconfigured victim PLCs. Inbound malicious traffic has been observed targeting PLC devices on the following ports: 44818, 2222, 102, and 502, as well as targeting modems on port 22.

These cellular modems, often installed by third-party contractors, frequently aren’t documented.

Unsurprisingly, the top recommendation for water utilities is to find every cellular modem in the environment, including those installed by contractors, make sure they aren’t exposed to the open internet, and put a gateway with multifactor authentication (MFA) in front of them to stop would-be intruders from gaining access via the modem and controller combo.

“Water utilities are spending time and money fixing the vulnerabilities they're told to worry about,” Securin CEO Srinivas Mukkamala said in a statement. “Meanwhile, attackers are getting in through equipment nobody knew was connected or passwords nobody bothered to change.” ®

#10199. Beyond Smartphones: How AI Is Shaping the Next Generation of Personal Devices (Developer Guide)

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Smartphones have defined personal computing for nearly two decades. They changed how people communicate, work, navigate and find information. They also still depend on screens, app switching and a lot of manual effort.

Artificial intelligence is starting to change that relationship. Emerging personal devices can respond to natural language, use context and offer help when it is needed instead of waiting for constant input. For developers, that raises practical questions: where should a model run, what should an interface look like without a large screen, and how much data should leave the device? This article walks through those questions and includes a small, tested helper for one of them. Product details change quickly, so check each platform's current documentation.

The smartphone interaction model is screen-centered: open an app, navigate menus, enter a command. AI-powered devices combine machine learning, sensors and conversational interfaces to interpret a request with fewer manual steps.

Navigation is a simple example. On a phone, you check the map or listen to directions. A wearable could give timely audio instructions or a visual cue based on location and movement. That does not mean the phone goes away. Personal computing may instead spread across interconnected devices, with the phone continuing to supply connectivity and processing.

Contextual computing means a system uses relevant information about the environment, user activity or earlier interactions. A traditional app responds to explicit commands. An AI-assisted one can also interpret natural-language requests and combine several inputs. A personal assistant might use calendar and location data, with permission, to suggest when someone should leave for an appointment.

Multimodal AI, which McKinsey describes as AI that can handle several kinds of data at once, including text, images, audio and video, makes interactions feel more natural than touch controls alone.

Context only helps when the underlying information is reliable. Developers need to manage permissions, data accuracy and user expectations, or the feature will feel intrusive or wrong.

Smartwatches and fitness trackers already showed the value of getting information without pulling out a phone. AI extends this to other products, including smart eyewear, earbuds and connected accessories.

For interaction design, the point is to deliver help while the user keeps doing something else. That changes what an app should show, say and ask.

Many AI features rely on cloud infrastructure. The cloud offers large models and plenty of compute, but adds network delay and requires sending data off the device. On-device AI runs supported models on personal hardware. It can reduce latency, support some offline features and limit how much sensitive information is transmitted.

Local processing has costs. Small devices have limited memory, battery and thermal headroom, so teams balance model size, compute demand and response quality. Hybrid architectures split the work between local hardware and cloud services, and are a practical answer for many applications.

A hybrid app needs a rule for where each request runs. The Python module below is a deliberately simple example. It takes the task (model size, whether the data is sensitive, the latency the user will tolerate) and the device state (free memory, battery, connectivity, measured round-trip time) and returns device, cloud, ask_user or unavailable.

The tests cover the main cases: a live caption on glasses stays on the device when the network is too slow, a large model that does not fit goes to the cloud, sensitive data stays local or triggers a question to the user when the model will not fit, an offline device runs only what fits, and low battery pushes a heavy job to the cloud.

Ambient computing describes technology that blends into everyday environments instead of demanding continuous attention. Users may reach connected services through speakers, watches, glasses and other devices. Someone could start a task with a voice assistant, get a reminder on a watch and continue on a laptop without recreating the context.

That requires reliable synchronization, interoperable services and consistent identity across devices. AI can help interpret requests and coordinate interactions, but a seamless experience still depends on well-designed software architecture. Developers should decide how information moves between devices, what happens when connectivity fails and how users stay in control of automated actions.

Apps built around large screens and touch navigation may not translate well to devices that rely on speech, audio feedback or compact visual displays. Google's Android XR developer documentation describes building for several device types, including XR headsets, audio glasses and display glasses, and lists tools such as the Jetpack XR SDK, Unity, OpenXR and WebXR.

Whatever the platform, a few priorities recur:

Not every interaction benefits from AI. Predictable tasks may be better served by a conventional interface, so use intelligent features where they measurably improve usability.

Probably not soon. Phones are likely to remain a hub for connectivity and processing, while wearables and ambient devices handle some everyday interactions.

Running a machine learning model directly on personal hardware, so a task can run without sending each request to a remote server.

When the model is too large for the device, the data is not sensitive and the network is fast enough. Many apps use a hybrid, as in the routing helper above.

Interaction relies more on voice and audio, displays are small or absent, and messages must be short and timely because the user is busy doing something else.

#10197. TPU Model Design Considerations and Optimization Guide

Google designed TPUs from the ground up to be exceptionally efficient for machine learning workloads, delivering top-tier performance per total cost of ownership (TCO). Because TPUs trade general-purpose hardware scheduling for wide systolic arrays, dedicated SparseCore engines, and custom interconnect fabrics, adhering to specific architectural best practices is essential to achieve maximum performance. To help developers get the most out of TPUs, we are launching the open-source AI-Hypercomputer/tpu-codesign guide. This post highlights its key principles—spanning shape alignment, architectural choices like LatentMoE, and parallelism strategies—to accelerate LLMs, Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models, and other hardware-intensive workloads on TPUs.

Performance tuning starts with model definition. Architectural choices made before the first training step set permanent ceilings on MFU (Model FLOP Utilization), cost, and serving latency. This guide gives concrete, hardware-grounded design rules for teams building or adapting models for TPUs, organized into three groups:

How to use this guide. The shape rules apply to anyone designing a new model. If you are adapting an existing model rather than designing one, the Expressing Parallelism section applies without touching the checkpoint. The general rules hold for any large-matmul architecture (dense LLMs, multimodal, and world models), and the companion AI-Hypercomputer/tpu-codesign repository grows model- and workload-specific sections over time.

Eighth-generation TPUs. These principles carry forward to TPU 8t and TPU 8i and to the newly announced TPU 8tx and TPU 8i Max. The reasoning stays the same and the thresholds move with the hardware. TPU 8t and TPU 8i both add native FP4. For training, TPU 8t also brings more balanced Vector Processing Unit (VPU) scaling. For serving, TPU 8i adds the Boardfly topology, a Collectives Acceleration Engine (CAE), and 3x more on-chip SRAM. We note the effects inline in Principles 5–8, and follow-up posts will cover eighth-generation designs in depth.

Measured evidence. Alongside the published sources, notes marked Measured carry results from our own Ironwood profiling program: open 20–30B MoEs (a Qwen3-30B-derived LatentMoE variant; GPT-OSS-20B) plus dense controls, single slice (except for the 512-chip TPU v7x and 1,024-chip TPU v4 ablations in Principle 4), BF16, training shapes up to 32K sequence, all against tuned-systems baselines. Treat the trends and rankings as transferable, the exact percentages as config-specific.

TPUs are purpose-built custom ML ASICs designed from the ground up to deliver state-of-the-art performance per total cost of ownership (TCO). While their architectural choices—such as wide 256×256 systolic arrays, software-managed VMEM, dedicated SparseCore engines, and direct multi-dimensional ICI fabrics—enable exceptional efficiency, they inherently create specific structural constraints and programming patterns that must be carefully followed to unlock maximal performance. Reaching peak efficiency requires aligning model shapes, routing, and parallelism directly with these underlying hardware characteristics:

Make d_model, FFN and expert intermediate widths (d_ff), latent widths, attention projections, and padded vocabulary sizes (used as contracting or output dimensions) multiples of 256 to match the 256×256 MXU systolic array. Token/batch axes and counts such as heads, layers, and experts are exempt. The most frequently missed dimension is head_dim: at 64 or 128, flash-attention QK products leave the MXU ≥ 50% idle. Reshape to head_dim = 256 with proportionally fewer Q and KV heads, preserving total projection widths and logical KV cache bytes (iso-quality in our ablations).

Measured: Reshaping Qwen3-30B MoE attention from 32 × 128 to 16 × 256 (identical parameters and FLOPs) sped up end-to-end training, with gains widening at longer context:

These rules apply for non-quadratic attention as well, notably GDN ablations from Yang et al. 2025 also noted at par or better quality for head dimension 256 for their Gated DeltaNet Attention.

XLA compiles each program for exact tensor shapes, so every buffer has a fixed address and every loop a known trip count, and a new shape means a recompilation and a host stall. What about dynamic shapes? Dynamic workloads fit this model: the trick is to separate the shape of a buffer from the extent of the data inside it.

Instead of padding to the largest group or the longest sequence, we keep the buffers at fixed shapes (tokens sorted by expert and padded only at the tail, the KV cache as a fixed pool of pages) and describe the ragged structure with a few small integer arrays (group sizes and offsets; KV lengths, the page table, cumulative query lengths). Pallas copies these into the TensorCore's scalar memory before the grid starts (PrefetchScalarGridSpec), and the kernel reads them to choose the next tile and bound its loops, with a mask covering a tile that straddles a group boundary or a partly filled last page. The data dependence becomes an address and a trip count instead of a shape, so the compiled program is the same for every routing and every batch composition and the kernel runs at the unpadded FLOP count.

Standard EP dispatches every routed token at full d_model width, making EP + FSDP slower than plain FSDP in our Ironwood benchmarks. LatentMoE projects routed tokens from d_model down to latent width ℓ (d/ℓ ≈ 2–4×) before dispatch, executes experts in latent space, and projects back up after combine (introduced by Nemotron 3 and adopted by 962B MAI-Base-1 and 2.8T Kimi K3):

Measured: On a 30B / 128-expert MoE, an ℓ = 768 (2.67×) LatentMoE cut on-device permute time 2.8×, lifted end-to-end training throughput +14% tokens/s (322 vs 270 TFLOP/s/chip, or +19%, since the latent projections add ~5% FLOPs per token; tokens stay ℓ-wide past the down-projection, so on-device permute and combine shrink alongside EP all-to-all), and freed ~25 GB/device of activation HBM when remat checkpoints were anchored on the compressed latent. While current MoE recipes in tpu-recipes run FSDP (+ TP), LatentMoE + FP8 dispatch makes EP roofline-viable when per-device batch is too small to hide FSDP weight all-gathers. Under both FSDP and EP, avoid over-fragmenting experts: small tokens × K / N group sizes starve MXU tiles.

When candidate MoE layouts match on quality, pick the one with higher compute intensity at a similar active-parameter budget. A larger shared expert (wider d_shared) shifts work into dense, communication-free GEMMs that overlap EP dispatch/combine in training and amortize weight reads across the decode batch. Fewer, wider routed experts (halving N and K while doubling d_ff at fixed sparsity K/N) cut dispatch traffic in half and double per-expert GEMM fill. Optimal (N, K) grows with model size, and over-coarsening hurts routing diversity. Ablate expert granularity and the shared-to-routed split at your target scale rather than copying GPU co-designed models, since the TPU optimum is more shared-heavy.

Measured: On a 63B LatentMoE (ℓ = 768, K/N = 1/32, 8K context, 512 TPU v7x chips at EP = 128), halving (N, K) from (512, 16) to (256, 8) while doubling d_ff and the shared expert lifted training throughput +57% tok/s and held lower loss across 120.8B tokens:

Low-concurrency decode streams model weights every token (~300 concurrent tokens per weight shard are needed in dense FP8 to reach compute roofline). Combine two architectural levers with explicit concurrency sizing:

Norms, activations, masking, routing math and quantization scale applies run on the Vector Processing Unit (VPU), which is roughly two orders of magnitude below the MXU in throughput and executes in sequence with the matmuls unless XLA fuses it into an adjacent GEMM or we move it off the TensorCore. Therefore we drop reshapes and transposes that carry no quality, standardize on fusable RMSNorm and SwiGLU so the elementwise work folds into the neighbouring GEMM, and offload the MoE sort, gather and scatter to the SparseCore.

Note that fused VPU work is free only while its cycle count fits inside the MXU window it is fused into. A quick illustration of this concept is block-scaled fp8 GEMM.

With a scale per block of B elements along the contraction axis, the MXU emits one partial sum per output element per block and the VPU rescales each one (about three vector ops), so per output element the MXU does 2K flops and the VPU does 3·ceil(K/B) ops. The rescale hides when B ≥ 1.5 × r_mxu / r_vpu, which on v7x (fp8 peak 4,614 TFLOPS against a 19.2 TFLOPS vector peak, a ratio near 240) puts the threshold near 340. In simulation on a DeepSeek-V3 expert shape, block 512 runs at the per-tensor time, block 256 is VPU-bound at 1.2x and block 128 at 2.4x. Achieved MXU efficiency is lower than peak, which lowers the effective ratio and can make the smaller blocks workable; the way to decide is to measure the GEMM at each block instead of using peak ratio. This threshold is specific to Ironwood. TPU 8t is designed with specialized support for scaling so quantization for MXFP4 and MXFP8 formats has no overhead. Re-measure the block-size threshold there instead of reusing 340. The rule itself carries forward: keep vector work hidden inside a matmul window.

For workloads dominated by BF16-sensitive matmuls (most notably long-context quadratic attention where direct FP8 degrades accuracy), native single-pass BF16 leaves MXU capacity unused whenever hardware low-precision MXU throughput exceeds native BF16 throughput by more than the pass count. Use the multi-pass BF16 algorithm to execute BF16-accurate matmuls via multiple passes over the higher-throughput lower-precision MXU pipeline without losing numerical fidelity. This matters more as low-precision throughput pulls ahead of BF16. TPU 8t adds native FP4, which doubles MXU throughput. On Ironwood the FP8 to BF16 ratio is exactly 2:1, so two or more passes cannot beat native BF16.

Assign communication-heavy parallel axes to the fastest physical links and keep logical mesh factors aligned with torus dimensions:

Minimum-degree CP matched to kernel trade-offs: All-gather CP is simplest (though its all-gathers can sit exposed) when paired with zigzag load balancing (context_parallel_load_balance in MaxText) so causal masking does not idle tail ranks; Ring CP overlaps collectives but trades off SplashAttention chunk efficiency; Ulysses A2A CP avoids causal skew but exposes all-to-alls and caps at head count.

Start every project with the quality-safe foundation: 256-aligned dimensions (1), static shapes with dropless routing (2), one parallelism plan mapped to physical links (8), wall-clock ablations on a tuned stack, and published reference targets (both in Measuring what matters below). For MoEs, add LatentMoE (3) after an ablation to cut routing/dispatch traffic and decode weight reads. Then prioritize by bottleneck regime:

Google engineered TPUs from the ground up as custom ML ASICs to deliver top-tier performance per total cost of ownership (TCO). Unlocking that potential requires bridging the gap between hardware architecture and model design. By standardizing on 256-aligned shapes, leveraging LatentMoE, and mapping parallel axes directly to physical links, engineering teams can maximize TPU efficiency across both training and serving workloads.

A reference blueprint is only as good as the loop used to validate it. Always evaluate architectural changes in wall-clock time on a tuned software stack rather than paper FLOPs: in our Ironwood benchmarks, pure systems tuning (SparseCore offload, MXU-aligned tiles, and remat tuning) lifted an open 30B MoE from 12% to 23% MFU before any model change, and published Ironwood baselines show 256-aligned MoEs (DeepSeek-V3, Qwen3-235B at 608–630 BF16 TFLOP/s/chip) running at roughly 2× the throughput of unaligned GPT-OSS-120B (330 TFLOP/s/chip). For the 6-signal XProf (profiler=xplane) triage checklist, roofline and HBM budgeting rules, wall-clock ablation methodology, published Ironwood reference targets, and ongoing updates as new TPU generations and kernels land, explore the living diagnostics guide and companion patterns in the open-source AI-Hypercomputer/tpu-codesign repository.

#10186. The sky was once full of stars, kids; then the FCC let Elon Musk launch a million datacenter satellites

Approval for ‘Starmind’ granted but it’s a long way from the launchpad, or making sense

The United States Federal Communications Commission has approved SpaceX’s plan to launch a million datacenter satellites.

The regulator on Wednesday published an order [PDF] allowing SpaceX to operate a “Non-geostationary satellite orbit (NGSO) constellation of 1,000,000 satellites deployed into orbital shells each spanning 50 km at altitudes from 500 to 1,002 km and in 30-degree inclinations and sun-synchronous inclinations.”

The order requires SpaceX to ensure its satellites’ radios don’t interfere with US government systems which use the 28.6-29.1 GHz band and requires the company to stick to operating within the parameters it files with the International Telecommunications Union.

SpaceX asked the FCC to waive its rule requiring the atmospheric disposal of dead satellites orbiting at 2,000 km or less.

Elon Musk’s company asked for “flexibility to dispose satellites operating above 600 km altitude into Earth disposal orbits or heliocentric orbits.”

The FCC found SpaceX did not provide enough information to let it assess if proposed “alternative disposal methods” are compliant with regulations, so deferred its decision on the waiver request.

“SpaceX must file an application for modification of this authorization and provide sufficient justification for any requested waiver(s) of the Commission’s rules,” the order states. The company must also file twice-yearly reports about “conjunction events” – moments when its satellites get dangerously close to each other, or other orbiters.

Whether orbiting datacenters are feasible, make sense, or will ever pay for themselves, remain open questions.

Analyst firm Gartner has described the idea as “peak insanity.”

Google thinks orbiting datacenters become viable if launch costs fall to $200/kg, which would mean SpaceX needs to launch its massive Starship 1,800 times. SpaceX’s assumptions assume near-daily launches, far better than its current cadence of a launch every three days or so.

Fundamental networking research must also come to fruition to handle the amount of traffic large constellations of orbiting datacenters will create.

Terrestrial manufacturing capacity will also need to increase to build all those satellites. Elon Musk’s companies and Intel have planned a giant new fab to make that possible, without explaining how the supply chains behind chip manufacturing will scale to make it feasible. That's a notable omission given that semiconductor supply chains are already extremely strained.

Approval for SpaceX is therefore nice but in no way a roadmap to a million datacenter satellites.

Google, ironically, is a little further along than SpaceX, having launched a test sat in October and last week announcing it has hired Amazon founder Jeff Bezos’ Blue Origin to launch a larger satellite carrying the equivalent of a full datacenter rack in early 2029.

Down on Earth, meanwhile, scientists trying to observe the universe continue to bemoan the obstructions caused by mega-constellations of broadband-beaming satellites. Proposals from across the USA, China, Europe and India currently call for around 100,000 satellites. SpaceX’s proposed million orbiters could run workloads which help us to understand the mysteries of the universe while also making them hard to see from down here. ®

#10184. Taming Typography: Building a Modern CSS Reset

CSS can now make choices about type that used to sit outside a reset. text-wrap: pretty can prevent a stranded word, container query units can scale a heading to the column it occupies, and text-box can trim the half-leading built into a line box. Some of these features are experimental and browser support is uneven, so they need guardrails rather than blind inclusion.

I’ve put together tidy-typography.css, which gathers the useful parts into a default stylesheet: rendering and fallback rules, fluid sizing, vertical rhythm, wrapping, hyphenation, and OpenType detail.

Use it whole, or take the declaration that solves the particular irritation in front of you. I’ll walk you through all the details in this article and give you the full code at the end.

font-synthesis: none stops a browser from inventing bold or italic when the family lacks that face. The smoothing and text-rendering declarations are rendering hints, mainly for WebKit; results still vary by platform.

text-size-adjust disables mobile font inflation that can make a deliberately sized layout jump on rotation. font-size-adjust: from-font uses font metrics to reduce the apparent size change while a fallback swaps for a webfont.

(As with other snippets in this article, this ⁠:root block is a partial excerpt the final stylesheet combines it with additional custom properties defined later on.)

Viewport-based fluid type does not know how wide its element is. A heading in a narrow sidebar can therefore scale as though it fills the viewport.

container-type: inline-size makes the wrapper a container query, so its children can use cqi: a unit based on the container rather than the viewport. Replacing vw in clamp() lets the same heading scale appropriately in an article column or a 300px sidebar without component-specific media queries.

line-height includes space above the cap-height and below the baseline: half-leading. It becomes obvious when text must align tightly with another element. text-box-trim removes it, while text-box-edge measures from capital-letter caps to the alphabetic baseline. This is a metrics-aware alternative to font-specific negative-margin nudges.

Selectors Level 5 proposes :heading, matching h1 to h6 and [role="heading"]. It has not landed in browsers, so keep it behind an @supports selector() check.

Default heading wrapping can leave one short final line. text-wrap: balance distributes text across its lines and works best for blocks of up to about six lines. For paragraphs, text-wrap: pretty avoids a stranded final word without balancing the whole block. Both replace JavaScript orphan fixers. Editable text needs text-wrap: stable, which prevents each new word from reshuffling the paragraph.

hyphens: auto relies on the browser’s language dictionaries, so the document needs a correct lang attribute (for example ). Without one the browser has no dictionary to use and won’t be able to hyphenate text. Even with the correct lang, the defaults can leave tiny word fragments or several hyphenated lines in a row.

hyphenate-limit-chars controls the smallest breakable word and fragments; hyphenate-limit-lines prevents a ladder effect. hyphenate-limit-zone makes breaks less frequent, while hyphenate-limit-last protects the final word. Safari needs the prefixed equivalents, so declare both sets together.

Set code, variables, and headings to hyphens: manual: a broken code word can change meaning, and a hyphenated heading usually reads as an error.

Punctuation marks don’t carry the same visual weight as letters, so a quotation mark sitting at the start of a line can look slightly indented against the text below it. hanging-punctuation lets specified punctuation sit fractionally outside the text block’s edge.

The first value handles opening punctuation, and last handles closing punctuation at the end of a block, while allow-end lets punctuation hang past the right edge rather than force-wrapping. The result is a text block whose edges read as genuinely flush.

Underlines have a related problem. Browsers apply flat, one-size-fits-all thickness and position regardless of the font’s actual weight.

text-decoration-thickness: from-font reads the thickness the font’s designer intended. text-underline-offset gives descenders room to breathe, and text-decoration-skip-ink: auto interrupts the underline wherever a descender crosses it rather than drawing straight through the letter.

Variable fonts often include an optical size axis, or opsz. It lets a typeface use thinner strokes at large display sizes and thicker, more legible strokes when set small.

font-optical-sizing: auto lets the font make that adjustment based on its actual rendered size. The @supports block covers the older font-variation-settings syntax some browsers still need, while font-variant-ligatures and font-kerning enable the font’s own ligatures and kerning pairs.

Light text on a dark background genuinely reads heavier than the same weight on a light background. Where a font ships a GRAD, or grade, axis, the dark mode block dials that perceived weight down without changing glyph shape or width the way an actual weight change would. Fonts without a GRAD axis safely ignore the setting.

Mixing a monospace font into running prose often exposes a mismatched x-height at the same declared font-size. The size-adjust keyword, set once at the @font-face level, scales that font’s rendered output specifically, leaving the actual font-size value untouched.

Numbers in a table need identical widths so they stack into a clean column on the screen. Conversely, numbers in flowing text don’t need the same treatment and should sit naturally alongside the surrounding words.

The tabular-nums and lining-nums keywords give table digits a fixed width and full cap height. oldstyle-nums and proportional-nums let digits sit alongside lowercase letters without sticking up and disrupting the sentence’s texture.

font-variant-emoji makes the choice between full-colour and monochrome emoji explicit. Without it, the browser or platform gets to choose a fallback, and the result can vary more than you expect. Apply .emoji-color or .emoji-mono to containers where you want to enforce that choice, rather than setting it globally on the body.

Quote style and character order differ by language and region. English nests single quotes inside double quotes, British English reverses that convention, and French uses guillemets with a thin space.

The quotes property declares the order for each language through :lang(). The native q element then inserts the right pair through open-quote and close-quote. The same markup can render correctly in each locale without hardcoding characters into the content.

Wrapping the rules in :where() keeps them at zero specificity, so a project can override the quote style with one plain class.

Default sub and sup styling is synthesized. The browser just shrinks the text and shifts the baseline, rather than using glyphs actually designed for that position.

font-variant-position switches to the font’s own purpose-built sub and superscript glyphs where they exist, resetting vertical-align, font-size, and line-height so they don’t fight against the real glyph’s intended size and position. Most typefaces only include proper numerals rather than letters, so check the specific font before applying this broadly.

:where() always resolves to zero specificity, which is useful for a reset. Every rule here is an opinionated default, and wrapping it in :where() lets a project override a property with a plain class.

:is() solves a different problem: repetition. The heading selector list appears several times in a typographic stylesheet, and grouping it avoids the risk of forgetting a level.

Unlike :where(), :is() carries the specificity of its most specific argument.

Here’s the complete reset, combining the stable layers. Unsupported declarations are ignored by the browser, so the stylesheet can be adopted gradually.

Much of this CSS removes work that used to leak into other parts of a build. text-wrap can take over orphan handling from JavaScript, container query units keep a component’s type scale tied to its own width, and text-box-trim avoids the negative margins once used to compensate for leading. quotes, font-variant-position, and font-optical-sizing hand several smaller details back to the language and the font instead of a collection of one-off fixes.

Browser support will continue to change, especially for text-wrap and text-box-trim. Unsupported declarations are ignored, but that only helps when the unenhanced typography is acceptable in its own right.

Some of these rules are still too new for a baseline reset. A news article, a marketing page, and an editable product interface do not ask the same things of their text; the set that belongs in yours depends on the font, the content, and the browsers you still need to support.

Andy Clarke shows you how to create designs that people recognize and remember in his brand-new e-book, Standout Designs for the Web. Order it today!

"English nests single quotes inside double quotes, British English reverses that convention,"

"English nests double quotes inside single quotes, American English reverses that convention (like it usually does),"

It’s nowhere near that cut-and-dried, and double has historically and geographically been much more popular. For some reason, UK publishing mostly switched to single for the 1900s, but they seem to be returning to double these days. Very few other countries followed them to single—Canada and Australia, for example, stuck with double.

A couple of links to start with (and you can find plenty more elsewhere): https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quotation_mark#In_English, https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quotation_marks_in_English#Quotations_and_speech

Overall, :lang(en) double-first with :lang(en-GB) single-first is a decent balance. If you had :lang(en) single-first, you’d want :lang(en-US, en-CA, en-IN, en-AU, en-NZ, …) for double-first.

A more accurate wording of the first sentence would be:

Quote style and character order differ by locale and house style.

I don’t understand the reason to use font-synthesis: none;. Isn’t synthesis just a fallback for when the author makes a mistake with the font faces or when the font file doesn’t load? Users’ fallback font-families likely have weight and style variants but probably not small-caps, maybe it at least should be allowed to be synthesized.

Instead of max-width: 65ch; being applied to p, li, dd, I’d consider applying it to p, dl, ol, ul. Limiting the width of the lists, rather than the list items, prevents longer items from “sticking out” relative to the paragraphs above and below, do to the items’ inline-start margins, padding.

The browser should automatically use French-style quotes (« ») within lang=fr; appears the British quote convention is not automatically used when lang=en-GB, at least not on my USian computer.

It might be worth noting more clearly that some of these are experimental/straight up don’t work today.

hanging-punctuation is Safari-only and doesn’t seem to support quotation marks in my testing, so the text is perhaps inaccurate on that point? Many of the hyphenation properties also only work in Safari, and require the prefixes described as “legacy” in the article.

It’s mentioned briefly in the intro that some stuff is experimental, but being more explicit in the sections about what does/doesn’t work currently would be great.

Definitely cool to explore the upcoming features, but feels a bit odd to frame it as a “reset” when it will likely change how text looks in the future in untested/unexpected ways, and includes superfluous code that won’t actually impact rendering today.

Limiting body, p, li and dd width is not the job of a reset. That’s a per-site choice and should be with other per-site css, not part of a reset block. And there’s lot’s of other very opinionated stuff as well. I’ll stick with my old friend Eric Meyer.

-webkit-font-smoothing: antialiased; is contentious (ill-advised); text-rendering: optimizeLegibility; is unwarranted; font-synthesis: none; has implications the typical user may not realise or like (though avoiding the need for synthesis is definitely preferable); font-size-adjust: from-font; has problematic side-effects, trading one problem for another that is in my opinion at least as bad; text-box is not widely supported and has layout implications that mean your layout will be somewhat broken in other browsers; Given the preceding ruleset, [contenteditable="true"] { text-wrap: stable; } doesn’t work if the content is within an inner p, li, dd (which it typically will be); Hyphenation is just all-round dodgy and generally better avoided; Hanging punctuation is contentious and current implementations are really not very pleasing; Turning contextual alternatives on generally is not necessarily a good idea—there’s a reason it’s opt-in and separated from the normal set of ligatures; font-kerning: normal; is ill-advised: let the browser decide, if it decides not to do kerning, it’ll be for a good reason; font-variant-position: [sub|super]; is a terrible idea when combined with font-synthesis: none, because you can’t know what font the text will be rendered in (no, your web font is not guaranteed to work; more people than you might realise block them and/or force the use of their own chosen fonts—I for one, it makes the web so much better), and the fallback font is unlikely to support the feature; and finally, :is(h1, h2, h3, h4, h5, h6), p, blockquote, figcaption, li is a completely useless example of :is().

Thank you for gathering these typography rules into one tidy reset — the section on font-size-adjust stood out to me, as fallback fonts so often change the apparent size of a page while a webfont loads. I also appreciated the honest note that text-box-trim and hanging punctuation are still unevenly supported, so the guardrails matter as much as the declarations themselves. It is a genuinely useful starting point that I will try on a small project first.

A reset that handles fluid type and vertical rhythm together is the part most resets skip. Hyphens in the same file is a nice touch.

I'd test the trimmed text beside an SVG icon with the webfont blocked, especially when the label wraps.

Thank you for pulling all of this together into tidy-typography.css, Stu. The section I keep coming back to is the one on rendering and fallback sanity. I spend a lot of time previewing font pairings, and the fallback swap is exactly where most sites lose their rhythm — a display face loads, the fallback is visibly narrower or taller, and the whole layout jumps. Your point about font-size-adjust: from-font reducing that apparent size change while a webfont swaps in is the clearest explanation of that fix I have read, and pairing it with font-synthesis: none so the browser does not invent a bold or italic face the family does not actually have makes the reset feel considered rather than just defensive.

The fluid typography section was the most useful part for me in practice. Using container query units so a heading scales to the column it occupies, instead of clamp() with vw scaling it as though it were always in a full-width article, solves a real problem I hit constantly: the same heading in a 300px sidebar should not behave like the same heading in the main column. The same goes for text-wrap: pretty and text-wrap: balance replacing the JavaScript orphan fixers I used to reach for, and the hyphenation tuning with hyphenate-limit-chars and hyphenate-limit-lines — I had never thought carefully about the ladder effect of several hyphenated lines in a row until this article. I also appreciated the honest framing that text-box-trim and hanging-punctuation are still unevenly supported, so they need @supports guardrails rather than blind inclusion.

One practical habit from my own work that fits well with this reset: when I test a new pairing, I preview it at small sizes with font-optical-sizing: auto on, because a face that looks crisp at display size can turn thin and hard to read at body size, and I check the same text with the webfont blocked so I can see the fallback in isolation. For anyone who wants a quick place to try out different styles side by side while testing a pairing, I keep a handy reference at https://fontsgenerators.net/ — it is useful for seeing how a style holds up before committing it to a stylesheet like this one. I will be trying tidy-typography.css as a base on my next project and keeping the experimental declarations behind @supports, exactly as you suggest.

#10178. CSS-Tricks, Version 20

I’ve been bashing on updating this site’s design for the last couple of weeks. It’s high time things get a fresh coat of paint, right? The last version of CSS-Tricks was pushed five years ago. That’s ancient in web (and dog) years.

It’s not like the site has simply sat all that time. We pushed lots of changes and shipped new features, particularly since 2024, but we never really got the paint brushes out of storage.

Now, before there’s a chorus of “things don’t look thaaaat different” I want to say I’m rarely a fan of complete re-builds and tend to lean into iterative releases. And the new v20 theme is what I’d call an iterative rollup that’s worthy of a version bump.

Let’s go over the most notable updates to see why.

First and foremost, some of the biggest changes are what you don’t see. As you might imagine, this site has carried over plenty of pieces across 19 years. I decided to excavate the theme and definitely found some ghosts to bust.

For example, the global compiled JavaScript file retained some scripts that just weren’t being used anymore. Things like handling embedded versions of the newsletter from before we began self-hosting the posts. There were relics that catered specifically to the Flexbox Guide for handling toggles back before we had proper HTML

. There was a jQuery lazy-loader thing that is well-covered by WordPress core now. We had some scripts for serving third-party ads that are no longer needed now that we’re self-hosting ads. Things like that. The overall JavaScript was nicely trimmed from 151.6 KB (55.3 KB gzipped) to 133.8 KB (48.1 KB).

I also decided to formally retire the CSS-Tricks Forum. We put it in “archive mode” back in, like, 2018. None of those discussions are particularly valuable for today as they were over 10 years ago, so I deactivated the bbPress plugin that powered it and cleared out the database… and wow, that carved a whopping 400 MB from the ol’ database, not to mention it allowed me to drop several custom templates from the theme.

There were more ghosts busted along the way and, to be sure, there are still some lurking around. But it’s way less scary poking around the theme directory now, and further cleanup should be a lot easier from here on out.

Yeah, yeah, raise your hand if you’re still running Gulp. We are 🙋‍♂️. That’s not a bad thing in and of itself, but keeping it in a fixed state for all this time is not the greatest. I think we’re sporting a more modern workflow than before, even if there’s still room to grow. A smattering of the related commits:

For years, I endured the same errors every time I fired up a new terminal. Those are gone and things run super cleanly, not to mention quickly. Very happy here.

Oof, this is everyone’s favorite job, right?! Aside from being able to spot and fix a few unescaped custom field calls here and there, I decided to ask Claude to help me with a holistic audit. I prompted it to review recent WordPress security release notes and identify areas where the theme could be hardened to comply with what WordPress is doing.

Whoa, you used AI? Yeah, I sure did. But for things like this where it could be my little gopher and allow me more time to do what I do best: front-end. I’m still able to make most of the patches myself based on the output recommendations, but it’s nice to have a gut-check for things where I’m clearly no expert, like security.

I still use and like Sass very much. I mean, yes, I would like to be straight-up vanilla CSS everywhere, but I can’t tear myself away from partials in particular.

The previous theme had a fairly nice “flat” structure — very few partial directories and most things all in the same top-level source. Totally cool. But an organization nut like me (you should see my house) has to have a place for everything to live. It ain’t perfect, but I’m happy with what I was able to put together without leveling the building in the process.

There are some compromises. Like, /header should really be in /layout. The ordering is really important, though, and some of my ideals were simply not possible. I’m hoping to eventually work in some sort of Cascade Layers structure.

I’m pleased as punch to see the overall size of CSS drop in the process. It went from 180 KB (31.7 KB) to 169 KB (24.6 KB). Not hugely significant, but not not significant either. I had to do a couple tricky things to cheat my way there, like serving all the form styles I’m about to talk about into a separate forms.css file and serving it just on pages where WooCommerce, Restrict Content Pro, and Gravity Forms are enqueued.

Are you starting to notice a trend? We’re still talking about fairly invisible work. CSS-Tricks has so many nooks and crannies that it’s nearly impossible to make things perfectly consistent across the board. But because of those nooks and crannies, so many fixed values, magic numbers, one-off styles, and older utilities were hiding about. I decided to tackle a good chunk of that while reorganizing the Sass.

For example, there are now semantic design tokens that abstract away one-off colors. So many grays, let me tell you. Now there’s a maintainable palette that’s referenced throughout. Same with fonts, font styles, and font sizes. Same with paddings and margins. It feels a lot better to make a change at the “system” level with confidence that the change is taking effect everywhere it needs to.

But this is also where some clearly visible changes happened! Most notably, link styles are consistent all over the place with a small handful of intended and maintained variations. Not only is it more pleasing visually, but it improves accessibility throughout because the inherited styles default to proper visual cues that do not rely solely on color or opacity changes.

Oh, and my favorite piece of this? Forms and tables have both been redone. That’s tough to do in a WordPress theme where these components don’t always come from one source. For example, forms could come from any one of the Gravity Forms, WooCommerce, or Restrict Content Pro plugins, and they all have their own stylesheets. Resetting those allowed me to bring a certain level of harmony in all those instances.

OK, something even more visual. This is the first time CSS-Tricks has had more than one color scheme! It certainly could have been done a long time ago, but I’d also argue that the design hasn’t really called for a light/dark toggle over the last 8 years or so. It’s a nice blend of both light and dark elements that marry the best of both worlds no matter your theme preference.

But I had to take a stab, right? The site has predominantly been dark despite the content being against a white background, so I treated things as though a “dark” mode already existed. The result is a creamy light theme that is largely just a change in background and component styles. And I used some of what I learned from the recent “toggle” debate to handle system, dark, and light themes with a single toggle.

What hasn’t changed is the main content container you see in things like articles. That was already white, even in a “dark” mode, and I’m not confident that simply toggling it between white and black is the best approach. Something on the radar for sure.

Oh, and it’s all wired up to a view transition that slides the new theme over the previous one.

I hope you think the site’s header and footer are cute. Yes, cute. That’s what I was going for when adding a colorful series of dots as the backdrop.

Sorta like a subtle candy texture, right? Or a colorful group of stars? I dunno, but I’m calling it “Jawbreaker” for lack of a better term and because it reminds me of the jawbreaker candy I loved as a kid. Naming still sucks.

It’s all part of the same SVG. I really, really (really) wanted to random()-ly position dots around, but that would have taken a lot of empty individual elements (e.g., ) in the markup. Not my favorite. So, I generate the elements in PHP:

I really, really wanted to random()-ize all the things, but inlining random coordinates on each was the way to go. Same deal with the inlined styles that set a fill, and sizing for each circle in the texture, and variables that set a --delay and --dur on the subtle animation that twinkles them in and out.

I’ll have to look at generating the SVG using or whatever later since that pattern repeats in the footer, too.

That’s carried through to the 404 page as well:

If your browser supports border-shape and random() (only Chrome, I think), then the homepage gives you a blobby-blob shape behind the grid of author avatars. Hover it and it gets, you know, even blobbier.

It takes an existing pattern I’m calling .gradient-card:

…and sets a gradient on the component’s :before pseudo:

That sits underneath the :after pseudo that’s translucent, allowing the :before‘s gradient to show through a smidge:

The differing border-radius values allow :before to bleed outside, giving the impression of a border around the entire deal. That’s the basic shape. The border-shape is where things get blobby:

Those variables? They’re mapped to shape()s. I grabbed the first one from Temani Afif’s blob generator:

It’s called “static” because that’s the default state. Hover or focus on the component and it swaps out those points out with a “hover” version of the shape:

Those variables? They’re mapped to random() values:

So, the hover state is different on each page load. And we animate between the two:

I’m pretty stoked on this one. The Almanac has grown, evolved, and just plain gotten great the past few years. But the primary page for it… not so great. It was definitely overshadowed by work on the pages inside it and never revisited beyond clickable cards to get to each feature subset.

Now the page is a real index of the CSS features that gets you where you need to go quickly. Someone somewhere sometime did some sort of CSS periodic table. I forget where I’d seen that but the general idea sat with me and I thought I could pull it off. There’s nothing “tricky” about it but I love what the layout adds to the overall site.

This is another area that sorely needed love. Our guides have always been a vertical stack of items. The book effect was definitely cool, but I started believing that leaning on it as the main visual hook was holding things back from being more functional. With something like 27 guides, we’re talking about a lot of scrolling with no clear idea of what you’re going to see as you go.

Again, nothing “tricky” but I like the overall usefulness of the new layout. There’s a featured guide, a widget of newly added and updated guides, and a categorized grid of everything below that.

The pages that show all of an author’s published articles got a little spruce-up, too.

It’s a little change, really. The right sidebar was moved to the left and the author image and bio moved in, allowing the articles to creep up to the top. I hope to do more here since the small layout tweak opens up more opportunities.

Also need to do the larger Authors index, but another time.

This one’s just for those of us working in the WordPress editor. I don’t need a complete visual editor to get things done, but it is a nice affordance to have some of the front-end styles sneak into the editing UI for a better idea of what you’re working with.

Using Lighthouse to measure things like performance, accessibility, and best practices is woefully fraught with caveats, but there’s no reason to chuck it completely. I find them more fun than fact and I don’t mind the recommendations for improving things.

So, after a few runs and tweaks, here’s how things shook out theme-to-theme.

I’m happy here. There are some things I just don’t have the control to fix when it comes to performance, so seeing v20 come in consistent with v19 is just fine by me. I’m happier with the boosts to accessibility and best practices. SEO? Meh. Don’t really care unless it’s hurting more important things like accessibility.

Again, very happy. There’s little I can do with time to first byte but everything else seems to clock in at least marginally better. The total page weight is a clear win and that’s probably what I care most about here.

Oh gosh, there’s a lot more but they’re mostly minor things that don’t bubble up to the front end. Sort of the same way browser changelogs have some generic line of “bug squashing” or whatever. In this case, it’s things like better post queries and breadcrumbs and function management and naming and… you get it.

There’s definitely so much more to do. And plenty of things I’m sure I missed or broke along the way. That’s just life and those will have to come in another iterative release less worthy of a version bump. You can let me know what I muffed up in the comments.

I hope you at least like where this is going!

Andy Clarke shows you how to create designs that people recognize and remember in his brand-new e-book, Standout Designs for the Web. Order it today!

#10177. India’s government ponders cradle to grave AI oversight

Machines of loving grace to turn social media grumps into services, finger painting into career advice

Indian politicians have proposed that AI could take a major role in citizens’ lives, partly through pervasive surveillance.

Prime Minister Narendra Modi last week spelled out his vision for an AI-based app that would collect data about children as they grow and analyze it to determine their aptitude for different types of study or careers.

Modi suggested that parents of three-year-old children should enter data about their offspring’s activities and accomplishments into a government-run system that is also accessible to teachers and even a child’s friends. Those records, the PM suggested, will “help identify children's interests and aptitude from an early age and redirect them in the study of their choice in future.”

That’s not an entirely creepy idea, because gaining access to India’s top tertiary institutions requires students to pay a hefty fee to sit for entrance exams. Modi pitched recommendations from the AI-powered app as something parents will appreciate as they contemplate whether to fund their offspring’s studies.

India’s most populous state, Uttar Pradesh (UP), has proposed to build an “AI Stack” to serve its 240 million-plus residents.

A 178-page tender seeks bidders to build a system that uses the state’s Family ID – a unique identifier for each household – to link data held by multiple agencies so it can be put to work in a dozen AI use cases.

One of those use cases is a tool for “AI-Enabled Social Media Monitoring and Urban Grievance Detection” that “analyses publicly available social media content including online news channels and connected grievance channels to detect emerging urban issues, citizen complaints, service interruptions, and recurring service gaps.”

The government wants that system to autonomously route complaints to relevant agencies, to automate actions like sending crews to patch up damaged roads. Critics have pointed to vague wording in the tender as potentially allowing the UP government to link this system to other agencies – a matter of concern given Indian state and national governments have implemented internet shutdowns to stop protestors from communicating on social media services and messaging apps.

Another use case is a knowledge assistant for state police that looks up case histories and other data but can’t use it to assess evidence or provide investigative conclusions. An app for teachers will advise them on how to create custom content to meet students’ needs. Healthcare workers would also get an app to help them make recommendations during patient visits.

Citizens get natural language interfaces to a system that offers advice on how to identify and access government services and schemes.

UP wants to drive these apps with self-hosted open-weight models, and to use only anonymized datasets.

India’s Internet Freedom Foundation worries that the systems described in the tender aren’t explicitly authorized by any legislation, and that the document doesn’t mention important oversight measures for the AI Stack. ®
