#10174. CSS Optimization: Is Using Declarations Just Once (UDJO) Still Effective Today?

CSS optimization is a deceptive topic. On the one hand, it feels like something simple that we should already have all the tooling for — especially when it comes to minification. On the other hand, the idea that we already exploited “everything” there is around CSS optimization may be considered naive. Such a claim looks hard to defend for a language that evolves almost daily.

In this article, I’d like to pick a technique that’s at least 18 years old — UDJO, or “using declarations just once.”

Aware of an older source? Please comment!

The technique is inspired by the principle of DRY, “don’t repeat yourself”, in that it works by identifying and reducing (if not eliminating) declaration repetition.

So, if there’s something like display: flex and it appears two or more times, it gets reconciled in one rule:

The outcome of the technique depends on how intelligently the deduplication is done and how long the selectors are. Intelligence determines if UDJO leads to an actual maintainability advantage — can the change of a declaration really be done to all the selectors attached? And the selector length influences how big file size savings are.

If you’re interested, my article on “DRY CSS” describes the mechanics in more detail. The question here is: Is UDJO still effective and still relevant today?

When we try to answer this question, however, we end up with a problem: When sites are complex and their style sheet declarations have not been de-duplicated before, UDJO can require a lot of work.

To save some time, I built tooling for this work. While it didn’t seem like it would immediately be as comprehensive as manually going through and testing style sheets, it seemed realistic enough that a tool could catch some repetition.

Skipping ahead, I created CSS Dedup not only to identify repetition, but also to deduplicate what could safely be optimized (CSS Dedup has some documentation on this but in essence, deduplication affects selector locations, which is part of how the cascade works). This, then, gives us some data:

For the first batch, I arbitrarily picked 20 websites to analyze. I then used CSS Dedup to check what the file size changes would be if we deduplicated what seemed provably safe, and also what seemed probably safe, but only if it led to file size savings.

I picked those two options to provide the “natural” result of deduplication, but also the one site owners may be most interested in to reduce repetition but also decrease transfer size. After all, UDJO as a CSS optimization technique can improve both maintainability and performance.

Important note: These values cannot be used to tell CSS quality—they depend on selector length and also on verifying if deduplication, in the respective case, actually leads to better maintainability. Also note that this leaves out changes after compression—this was not part of the experiment but would be fair to test, too.

For the question whether UDJO still works today, this should be a yes. It works as well as to even come with file size savings.

Maybe you’re curious, too, about what the situation looks like in our field, among web developers. Let’s take some samples on this, too.

It would be valuable to expand this analysis to take more samples and investigate the differences across samples, but there may be something to web professionals using shorter selectors so as to be able to benefit more from UDJO.

As mentioned at the beginning, UDJO’s effectiveness generally depends on the kind of repetition as well as on the length of selectors.

This can be managed, however: While tools like CSS Dedup make no difference between declaration use cases (and are therefore blunt), it is possible to configure them so as to reap the performance benefits.

As the data taken for this article suggest, file size savings may be achieved in most cases. Considering that this is based on safe changes, this means a low-cost performance optimization vector — especially when applied to minified output. It’s still a good idea to couch that against the use of further compression (e.g. gzip/Brotli), but at face value, deduplication shows how it can reduce the size of CSS.

For anyone generally interested in CSS optimization, maintainability, and performance, UDJO should probably be part of the overall approach to CSS. But since the technique has been niche and since tooling is new, I invite you to review, test, and explore this vector. It seems hard to argue that we have reached the ceiling of how far we can take CSS optimization.

Andy Clarke shows you how to create designs that people recognize and remember in his brand-new e-book, Standout Designs for the Web. Order it today!

and also to add that in the last weeks I’ve been testing some approaches to using CSS Dedup. It has proven quite effective, especially in the following setup: On static sites, reaping only savings via css-dedup CSS_FOLDER -p "(?

I’ll write more about this shortly (on my website)—and am curious about your thoughts and observations!

I’d be curious to see the minuscule performance implications, if any at all.

If up to 11% savings in a small sample are true – even pre-compression – then that’s not minuscule. Especially not with the average size of a stylesheet.

I’d like to see more sites being tested both pre and post compression.

#10172. UK Home Office waved through border system without a working fix for bad data

ICO reprimand follows failings that risked wrongful detention, with a temporary correction process taking more than a year to arrive

The Information Commission's Office (ICO) has reprimanded the UK Home Office over a border watchlist system launched without an effective way to correct inaccurate personal information.

The regulator said the failings risked people being wrongly detained for additional checks at the UK border, potentially causing distress and other harm.

The platform in question, Helios, was slated to replace its predecessor, Warnings Index, in September 2024 and despite an initial delay due to concerns over data quality, went live the following month.

Both Helios and Warnings Index are watch-listing systems, designed to allow law enforcement officials – mainly but not exclusively Border Force – to check people's data against official records at the UK border.

Helios's federated operating model, whereby the data sources are not centrally managed, yielded quicker results than Warnings Index, but contained a critical flaw in that operational staff could not alter incorrect records.

Platform operators, such as Border Force officers, could not amend the data that feeds into it, neither after the fact nor in real time. This was not the case with Warnings Index, which had a dedicated team responsible for correcting data entries in real time.

Under Helios, staff were supposed to report inaccuracies to the organization responsible for the relevant source data, which would make the correction.

One slight problem: there was no established reporting system at the time Helios was launched on October 2.

The platform went live anyway, as officials deemed the data integrity and security risks presented by Warnings Index to be greater than those of Helios.

Just nine days passed before the Home Office's data protection officer had to submit a breach report after Helios operational staff said they didn't know how to request data corrections.

During the time, Helios operators sent 87 data rectification requests to the Warnings Index correction team, despite it having no capability to make the required changes to the data.

The Home Office later said that many other requests likely weren't logged as the proper processes were not in place, and that staff should have logged the issue through a ServiceNow IT ticket, as any other departmental civil servant with any kind of IT issue would have.

But the Home Office also said that including personal data in those helpdesk tickets would itself constitute a data breach.

The ICO said in its reprimand document [PDF] that it is "unclear" how such a reporting arrangement could realistically work in practice, given that a report would require details from a subject's record that required rectification as well as the new, accurate information that should be used to replace it.

The Home Office's proposed workaround was to get operational staff to verbally read out said personal data over a phone call with the IT helpdesk – a procedure it described as possible "but cumbersome."

It took the Home Office more than a year to implement a temporary data-rectification method, which involved a BX Data Query tile on the IT Now platform, allowing Helios operators to securely report data accuracy issues.

The new method went live on October 25, 2025.

By March 18, 2026, the Home Office said the new process had received more than 6,500 correction requests and around 5,000 records had been removed.

The ICO said it "remains concerned" about the efficacy of this method, given that operators cannot include personal data in the tile report, but the regulator is satisfied the problem is at least in part fixed.

The Home Office also said it was aware of an increase in customer complaints following Helios's launch, and around 800 cases in which people were delayed at the UK border pending additional checks.

It was unable to attribute a specific number of these to Helios's technical issues, however.

"The Home Office further stated that it was not aware of any data subjects suffering distress as a result of using its services at the UK border," the ICO's report stated.

A Home Office spokesperson told us: "We take data protection extremely seriously. That is why we have co-operated fully with the Information Commissioner's Office. "We immediately removed all outdated records to minimize delays to passenger journeys and strengthened mechanisms to speed up updates."Our processes will continue to be kept under review, ensuring we meet data protection obligations while protecting border security."

Joanne Stones, investigations manager at the ICO, said: "People must be able to trust that decisions about their immigration status at the UK border are made with accurate, up to date information." She added: "They also have the right to expect that when information is inaccurate, there is a swift and efficient way for it to be corrected.

"We recognize that federated systems like Helios present unique challenges and welcome the steps the Home Office has taken since this issue was identified. However, the Home Office must ensure that its permanent solution provides a robust and effective way to identify and correct inaccurate information without delay."

The ICO's public sector enforcement policy favors guidance, reprimands, and other interventions, reserving fines for the most egregious cases. Its rationale is to avoid diverting money from public services and effectively punishing those affected twice.

Fines have included £350,000 for a 2021 MoD email that exposed details of Afghan evacuation applicants, and £750,000 for the PSNI's 2023 disclosure of workforce information, including surnames, initials, ranks, and roles. The PSNI penalty was reduced from £5.6 million under the public sector approach. ®

#10170. Beyond 12 Columns: Designing Layouts With Personality

When I started designing websites thirty years ago, David Siegel had just published Creating Killer Web Sites, the book that popularised HTML tables and spacer GIFs for laying out web pages. CSS eventually gave us more control over layouts through floats and positioning. Neither was intended for page layout, and inconsistent browser behaviour could make using them frustrating. Still, that didn’t stop me and a generation of other designers from using floats and positioning.

When CSS frameworks began providing ready-made grid systems, things got a little easier. Blueprint CSS) offered a 24-column grid, while Nathan Smith’s 960 Grid System offered 12- and 16-column options. These frameworks gave designers and developers a shared structure, making layout planning and implementation considerably easier.

Twelve-column grids weren’t invented in the 2000s. Graphic designers had used column-based and modular grids for decades, and twelve is a convenient number because you don’t need to be a maths genius to divide it into halves, thirds, and quarters to create the 2-up, 3-up, and 4-up components we see everywhere online.

Frameworks like Bootstrap and Foundation followed, helping establish twelve-column grids as the de facto standard for page layouts. I get why that happened. But somewhere along the way, one layout option became the default starting point for almost everything.

CSS Grid changed what’s possible for page layouts, but I still see designers start by dividing a page into twelve equal columns, then deciding how to fit their content into them. The designs they end up with might be perfectly fine, but they can also look remarkably similar.

Still, I don’t think twelve-column grids themselves are the problem. It’s reaching for a familiar solution before considering what makes the company I’m designing for distinctive and what its website needs to communicate. So what happens when I start with those questions instead?

Before I decide how many columns a layout needs, I want to understand the company I’m designing for. What makes it different from its competitors? What does it want people to remember? Does it have a personality that could influence how its website looks and feels?

These questions matter because a layout does more than arrange content. The size, position, and relationships between elements influence how people experience a website. A restrained, orderly composition can communicate something very different from one that’s full of energy and movement. So to demonstrate what I mean, I’ll use Wheelman. It’s a fictional company I invented for my book, Standout Designs for the Web.

Pre-orders for Standout Designs for the Web are now open in the CSS-Tricks store.

Wheelman provides products and services for getaway drivers, including legal advice, robbery insurance, and an online shop selling break-in essentials. It also publishes a magazine with advice, news, and stories about life behind the wheel.

Wheelman isn’t an ordinary company, so why should its website look like an everyday collection of components? It takes its business seriously, but isn’t afraid to have fun with its design. It’s rebellious, but doesn’t want to look off-putting. Its audience is criminals, but they demand professionalism. Wheelman has plenty of personality to work with, and I want its designs to reflect that.

That doesn’t mean making Wheelman different for difference’s sake. Its customers still need to find what they’re looking for, understand what it provides, and buy its products and services. But I can make those experiences more memorable by choosing layouts that fit the company, rather than settling for familiar arrangements.

Let’s imagine I’m designing a new landing page for Wheelman’s online shop. It includes two products, one with a gigantic headline, product images, and the type of functionality and information you’ll find on most stores.

Before I decide how to arrange these elements, I want to look beyond them and consider how they relate. I ask myself questions like:

Only then do I start thinking about visual design questions: “Would a large photograph set the scene? Should I make the introduction more prominent? Can whitespace lead the eye and create spaces to pause? How can I bring images and text together into a considered composition?”

These questions help me understand what the layout needs to accomplish before I decide which grid might help me achieve it.

I could arrange everything in a single column for a straightforward reading experience. That would make responsive design considerably easier. But I won’t.

Wheelman has a rebellious personality, and I want its shop to reflect that. A more energetic layout seems appropriate, but I still need to establish a clear hierarchy between the products and their information. Two equal-width columns give both products equal prominence.

Changing their proportions creates a hierarchy, even when most elements stay the same size.

I could base those proportions on a twelve-column grid, but I won’t. Columns only divide space vertically, and I want a layout that adds another dimension.

Dividing a layout into columns is a useful starting point, but adding rows creates another dimension and opens up more creative possibilities. Where columns and rows intersect, they form rectangular or square units called modules.

Combining these modules creates larger spatial zones that can establish hierarchy, control whitespace, and add variety to a composition.

Using a modular grid, I can add drama, establish hierarchy by varying scale, and make the composition feel far less predictable. Starting with four columns and four rows gives me sixteen modules to work with, but I don’t need to fill them all.

I can create drama by spreading the large graphic headline and product image across four modules, while the product information and shopping tools occupy one module each.

The modular structure becomes even more apparent in the grid of alternative products. Most occupy just one module, while others fill two, either horizontally or vertically. Varying their sizes gives some products more prominence and makes the overall arrangement feel less predictable.

The result still feels like a product page, with a clear reading order and space for the content to breathe. But it also feels unmistakably like something Wheelman would publish.

I don’t expect this composition to work at every screen size, so I can adjust the size and quantity of modules to improve the reading experience.

And if one grid offers this much flexibility, imagine what combining two or more grids can do.

One thing I want from Wheelman’s website is variety. Its magazine, shop, and services serve different purposes, and I don’t want every page to feel like I’ve poured its content into the same template.

Wheelman needs layouts which feel organised without being predictable. Compound grids give me the structure I need while leaving room for less conventional arrangements.

A compound grid combines two or more grids of any type — column, modular, or hierarchical — on the same page. These grids can overlap, sit side by side, or be stacked one on top of the other. Combining grids creates possibilities which neither grid offers on its own.

I can start by overlaying a two-column grid with one containing three columns. Their combined grid lines create four columns of two different widths, giving me more flexibility without losing the underlying structure.

Those proportions offer more possibilities for arranging content. I might use a wider area for a photograph or headline, then place a pullquote into one of the narrower spaces. Another page could use the same underlying grid but combine its spaces differently to suit another story.

Combining grids with different column counts gets even more interesting as those numbers increase. In Standout Designs for the Web, I explore several combinations, including mixing four- and five-column grids to create different compositions for Wheelman’s pages while maintaining a connected visual structure.

The point isn’t to make every page look different for its own sake. It’s to give each section a layout that suits its content while keeping them all feeling like part of the same company.

Twelve-column grids are useful, and I’m certainly not suggesting you should stop using them entirely. But they’re only one option among many, and starting every design with the same structure limits your possibilities before you’ve even considered the story your design is telling.

There’s also nothing inherently wrong with the 2-up, 3-up, and 4-up patterns a twelve-column grid inspires. They can sometimes be exactly what’s needed. But reaching for them automatically can leave little room for the character and personality that make websites memorable.

Wheelman gives me plenty of reasons to choose different layouts. Its stories, products, and services don’t all need the same arrangements, and its personality gives me opportunities to make those arrangements more distinctive. The grids are simply tools that help me put those ideas into practice.

And this applies to much more than layout. The same thinking should influence the colours I choose, the typography I use, the images I commission, and the way I bring those elements together.

That’s why I wrote Standout Designs for the Web. I want to encourage designers to look beyond familiar solutions and make more deliberate decisions about colour, typography, imagery, and layout. Not to be different for difference’s sake, but to create websites that look and feel like they belong to the companies behind them. And no one else.

Pre-orders for Standout Designs for the Web are open! Everyone who pre-orders will also receive the Hardboiled Client Interview Guide for free — a practical companion to help designers ask better questions and discover what makes a company distinctive.

The book will be published on November 20, priced at $14.99 for the DRM-free EPUB and PDF editions.

Andy Clarke shows you how to create designs that people recognize and remember in his brand-new e-book, Standout Designs for the Web. Order it today!

#10169. Agents are sessions, not requests: size GKE node pools for AI agent sessions

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Your company wants to move its coding agents from developer laptops to a shared GKE cluster, for persistence, scale, and governance. Each of those agent sessions stays open all day but works only part of it, and how you run them decides how much of your node bill pays for waiting.

By the end of this article, you will know why an agent session needs a different runtime than a web request, how Agent Substrate and AX run sessions on GKE, and how to identify cost optimization opportunities in your GKE node pools. This article assumes you know Kubernetes Pods and node pools. If not, check out the GKE overview.

A web request lasts milliseconds. The server answers it, keeps nothing, and serves the next one, so Kubernetes scales a web app by its traffic: more requests, more Pods.

An agent session works another way. A coding agent's workspace can stay open for the developer's whole workday: it keeps its memory and its files between prompts, and it works only after a prompt arrives.

How much of that day does the agent really work? Much less than it waits. In a July 2026 study of agent traces, Banruo Liu and colleagues found that in a typical session with several prompts, the agent waits for the user about 80% of the time. A turn, one prompt and the agent's whole response to it, takes about 6.5 minutes of agent work on average

Put those numbers on one developer's day. With 2 prompts an hour over an 8 hour workday, which is our assumption, the agent works 22% of the day and waits for nearly 78% of it. If each session holds a machine, you pay for that waiting time

Teams usually pick one of two answers, and both work well for web apps.

The first is to give each session its own Pod. The session keeps its state, but its Pod holds 1 vCPU and 1 GiB all day. For example, a c3-standard-4 node fits 3 such Pods after GKE's reservations, so in our cost model 100 coding agents need 34 nodes and about USD 1,288 a month, for agents that work 22% of the time.

The second is to scale the Pod to zero between prompts. The node's capacity is released, and files on a persistent volume survive, but the process and its memory do not, so the next prompt starts a new agent process.

Based on that, you want a third option: keep the session, and free the machine while the session waits.

Agent Substrate and AX are built for that: the developer's session stays open, and the sandbox that serves it gives its machine back while the session waits. Together they cover the parts of an agentic platform that are the same for most teams: the control plane, the runtime, and the sandbox. Agent Substrate answers where and how an agent runs. AX answers what the agent is.

Following the CNCF donation of gVisor, Agent Substrate, and OpenRL in November 2026, you can run Agent Substrate on GKE or on other Kubernetes clusters. Agent Substrate 1.0 is generally available on GKE.

One distinction helps from here on. The session is the multi turn interaction between the developer and the agent, with its conversation history. The compute that serves it is an isolated sandbox: an actor in Agent Substrate, or a Task in AX. Parking acts on the sandbox, and the session stays open.

Agent Substrate answers where and how an agent runs. It separates the stateful agent from the compute it runs on. An actor is the sandbox for one agent, and it holds the agent's running processes, files, and identity. A worker is a reusable sandbox drawn from a warm pool of Kubernetes Pods, and one worker hosts several actors.

An agent runs code that a model wrote, so anything in its environment can leak through a prompt injection or a bad command, API keys included. Gateways control the actor's inbound and outbound traffic and add credentials on the way out, based on the actor's identity, so credentials stay out of the agent's environment.

SuspendActor snapshots an idle actor to object storage and frees its worker, and the next request restores the actor on a warm worker with room for it. Agent Substrate snapshots memory by default and falls back to files when it cannot capture memory. Many parked sessions share a small warm pool, so in our cost model the same 100 agents need 16 nodes instead of 34.

AX answers what the agent is and what it knows. You declare a Task, which says what runs (image, command, and limits), and a Workspace, which says what the agent knows (repositories and files). AX runs each Task as an actor. ax suspend parks the task and keeps its /workspace. Today AX asks for a volume snapshot, so the files come back on resume and the agent's processes start fresh (runner guide).

AX passes suspend, resume, and delete to Agent Substrate. The AX roadmap plans to add budgets, policies, and gateways, which limit what an agent may do, and a record of what each task did.

Two details decide the bill before you size anything.

First, you decide when a sandbox parks. Agent Substrate does not suspend an idle actor by itself (#424), so your agent harness, or the service in front of it, calls the suspend API after an idle timeout. Until then, an idle actor keeps its worker. When idle actors hold every worker, a request for another actor waits up to 5 seconds by default, then fails with HTTP 503 (Agent Substrate docs).

Second, you decide how much CPU each sandbox is promised. An actor is sized by its CPU and memory limits, and each worker books those limits against the capacity you declare for its pool (API guide). Our cost model sets that capacity to what the node really has, so every actor keeps its full limit. You can declare more capacity than the node has, and then you overcommit CPU at the pool instead of in each Pod.

Our cost model prices parking for the same 100 coding agents, at Google Cloud on-demand list prices, as of November 2026, in us-central1.

With a 2 minute idle timeout, each session holds a worker nearly 29% of its open time. A warm pool sized for bursts (the 99th percentile of sessions holding a worker at once, if sessions act independently, plus 15% headroom) needs 15 nodes. Add one node that runs around the clock for the Agent Substrate control plane and its PostgreSQL database, and a job that scales the pool to zero after hours:

Parking saves nearly 40% of the node bill. The pool is full about once in 3,000 turns, so a prompt rarely meets that 503. In an hour at 2.5 prompts instead of 2, the same 15 nodes are full for 3% of turns, so size the pool for your busiest hour.

Keep in mind that parking pays most when your agents wait the most. At 2 prompts an hour, the saving is nearly 40%. At 4 prompts an hour, the agent works 44% of the hour, the pool grows to 27 nodes, and the saving is 4%. Above about 4 prompts an hour, one Pod per session becomes the cheaper option, so measure your prompt rate first.

Also, the pool size is yours to set. The autoscaling option in the GKE install guide follows worker Pods, not suspended actors, so scale the pool to zero after hours with a scheduled job: a pool that runs all 730 hours of the month costs nearly twice the one Pod per session bill.

Finally, the fleet size matters. The Agent Substrate control plane costs the same for 10 sessions or 1,000, so parking pays from about 37 coding sessions, or 13 for an assumed chat assistant that works about a minute an hour.

Overcommit changes the comparison. If your Pods request less CPU than their limit, Kubernetes places them by their requests. A Pod that requests 0.25 vCPU with a 1 vCPU limit packs 12 to a node (its 1 GiB of memory caps it there), so the same 100 coding agents fit on 9 nodes, fewer than our parked pool. Agent Substrate can overcommit too, through the capacity you declare on the worker pool, and our cost model does not price that case.

Overcommit has the same cost on both sides: agents share CPU and slow down when their neighbors are busy, during builds and tests. Compare like with like before you decide to park: Pods and actors with the same CPU guarantee.

Back to the shared cluster. Agents are sessions, not requests: size the warm pool for the sessions that hold a worker, not the sessions that are open.

We would park long lived sessions that wait most of the hour, and keep one Pod per session when developers prompt more than about 4 times an hour. Size the warm pool for working time plus a short idle timeout at your busiest hour, and compare with your current Pods at the same CPU guarantee before you park.

Thanks for reading! If you run agent sessions on Kubernetes today, how many prompts an hour do your users send? The prompt rate is the input we are least sure of. You can reach Ivan on LinkedIn or X with your numbers, questions, and corrections.

Six chapters from Brad Frost, TJ Pitre, and Ian Frost schooling you on using AI in your work without producing slop. #10166. 45°C inlets just killed the chiller. Most people haven't noticed yet

In my mind and in my car, we can't rewind, we've gone too far

SC25 in St. Louis looked like a plumbing convention cross booked the supercomputing hall. Pipes, pumps, manifolds, quick-disconnects, and a lot of people who clearly spend more time thinking about pressure drop than floating-point. I kept expecting to see a booth selling industrial fire hoses. Chicago is a much bigger building – McCormick has roughly four times the exhibit space St. Louis had – so the pre-show question was whether SC26 will still look like that or whether the hall is large enough to dilute the effect.

If anything, cooling was even more dominant in Chicago. The heat problem didn't get smaller just because the building got bigger.

We also saw a lot of new companies displaying their cooling wares at SC26. I follow this stuff closely and see two broad trends. First: consolidation with big industrial suppliers buying up the liquid cooling pioneers. Second: anyone with an angle is heading into the market. Both trends were visible on the floor in Chicago, and everyone had something to say about higher inlet temperatures, whether they have a real story or not.

The real news, though, is a number Nvidia dropped earlier this year: 45°C (113°F) liquid inlet temperature for their parts. That's not a small tweak. Most liquid-cooled systems have been living in the 30-35°C (86-95°F) world because that's what the chips would tolerate. Jump to 45°C (113°F) and a whole bunch of datacenters that currently need chillers suddenly don't.

If AMD, Intel, and the Arm folks can match it – and we’d be shocked if they aren't already working on exactly that – then free cooling stops being a cool climate thing and becomes the default for a lot of places.

Rooftop dry coolers can reject nearly any incoming fluid temperature and bring it below 45°C (113°F). There are few places in the world where ambient temperatures approach 113°F, and for the hours when outside temps spike, adiabatic assist is available. This is where water is trickled through a mesh surrounding the dry coolers, which drops the effective temperature another 10–20 percent.

So, in our new cooling world, there are two closed loops: one taking heat from the server gear and then passing it to a second, different loop that takes the hot water to the roof, cools it, then brings it back again.

What's missing here? It's the chillers. You won't need them anymore. These are essentially huge rooftop refrigerators that cool water even more after the dry cooling. They're expensive, need a lot of maintenance, gulp electricity and are thankfully unnecessary now for new datacenter builds.

The power and water savings are not trivial. Legacy designs that rely on chillers and open evaporative towers commonly put 20 percent to 40 percent of total facility energy into cooling. A large share of that is the chiller plant itself – compressors, pumps, and cooling-tower fans. Move to dry coolers running at 45°C (113°F) inlet and that slice collapses.

Published estimates and vendor modeling for high-temperature liquid loops put the reduction in cooling energy in the 50 percent to 70 percent range versus a conventional chiller-plus-tower plant, which translates into a 10 percent to 20 percent drop in total datacenter power  – depending on the starting PUE (Power Usage Effectiveness) and climate.

Water use falls even harder. Traditional evaporative towers can consume millions of gallons a year at a mid-size site. Dry coolers use essentially no outside water in normal operation; adiabatic assist only wets the mesh during the hottest hours, cutting annual water consumption by 80 percent to 95 percent in most climates. Those are the numbers that make the 45°C (113°F) inlet such a big deal.

The same shift also changes the economics of the mechanical plant itself. A chiller plant is not just an operating cost; it is capital equipment that has to be sized, installed, maintained, and eventually replaced. Dry coolers are simpler, cheaper to install, and have far fewer moving parts. When the chips will accept 45°C (113°F) fluid, the business case for putting a mechanical refrigeration plant on the roof of a new build gets hard to defend in most climates. That is the part that has not yet fully registered outside the cooling specialists.

Designing systems around these higher inlet temperatures still takes real engineering work – sizing the loops, choosing the dry coolers, and making sure the facility can run at the new set points without surprises. Existing buildings add another layer. Many were built with pipes, pumps, and heat exchangers sized air cooling or lower temperature liquid. Raising liquid inlet temperature means checking flow rates, pressure drops, and making sure the existing set-up delivers.

At SC26 I had meetings with a couple of companies that do this work and buttonholed more in their booths. My top-of-mind questions were: Are the 45°C (113°F) inlet temps for real? Yes, they are, and are being used as a design basis to an extent – they need to leave headroom, of course.

Any news on whether the other chipmakers are going to hit the Nvidia number on inlet temperature? Attendees were closed-lipped on this one. Nothing public from the other vendors (AMD/Intel/Arm). But on the cooling vendor side, they are happy to see the increase and will comply.

My instincts are telling me that the other chipsters may have been caught flatfooted by Nvidia's 45°C stake in the ground, but those same instincts also tell me that handfuls of Peanut M&Ms healthy snacks. I’ll nose around more.

There's real money at stake here from a TCO standpoint. One cooling vendor reports that their initial testing shows 3 percent to 4 percent cooling energy cost decrease for every additional 1°C inlet temperature increase. That's a significant number at scale.

We're approaching a point where industry advances are answering many of the objections (the real ones, not the hysterical ones) raised by the anti-data center movement.

Much lower energy use, water consumption down by 85 percent to 90 percent, higher efficiency and lower costs. Not a bad deal, right? ®

#10164. Staggering Scroll-Driven Animations with animation-range-start

The other day on the CSS-Tricks Slack channel, Danny Schwartz shared an interesting recent Master.dev article by Ashutosh Biswas on how to stagger CSS scrolling animations using a clever combo of several emergent CSS features and a lot of math, which Ashutosh admits is “a huge formula and can look intimidating.”

When Danny Slacked the link for the Frontend Masters article, he described it as “CSS on drugs.” Speaking of drugs, I am on heavy prescription painkillers while recovering from a serious injury, so that might not help my current comprehension skills. Nevertheless, after reading Ashutosh’s article a few times, here’s a meme showing how I’d feel using such an elaborate algorithm for something as mundane as staggering the animations of some characters as they scroll into view.

Don’t get me wrong. I know the complexity is because Ashutosh is going for a generic solution in which we can connect a “progress value to an animation, scroll progress, or any other input to drive the staggering.”

In fact, I can dream up a scenario in which this might be what we want: Imagine a CSS karaoke machine that displays a ball bouncing on text that incrementally highlights, based on the user’s progress through the vocals measured via speech recognition. Running that kind of animation “not as a collection of independently timed objects, but as a single predictable, holistic effect driven by one progress value” might be exactly what the doctor ordered. This would be true because the staggered animations could be interrelated, and the overall animation would be driven by a custom input that’s not built into the browser.

But unlike this hypothetical scenario, replacing the passage of time with scroll progress to control animations is already built into modern CSS. I contend that we’d need a better reason to roll our own implementation than the examples Ashutosh gives.

Ashutosh showcases a scroll-driven use case as “one of those ideal scenarios where the stagger formula shines.” But a red flag that we’re in danger of reinventing something we can get for free is that the spec for scroll-driven animations is cognizant that if we opt to base an animation on scroll progress rather than on time, then, as Ashutosh says, “the animation we are going to achieve is different from traditional ones because we don’t include time in our considerations.” This is no surprise to the creators of scroll timeline, and the MDN docs make Ashutosh’s point in different words when introducing scroll timeline.

The docs also point out that animation-delay is only meaningful when we are using the default time-based document timeline. Therefore, animation-delay does not affect scroll-driven animations. But this is why CSS introduced animation-range-start, which allows us to delay a scroll-driven animation relative to scroll progress and not time.

So, as esoteric and trippy as the math-heavy approach appears, that could be because we are doing something that should be simple in an unusual way.

Let’s see if I’m right by rewriting Ashutosh’s demo to remove reliance on custom CSS functions and use animation-range-start instead.

Ashutosh’s rework of Frontend Masters’ animated tally of donations in the site footer is a nifty, subtle use of scrollytelling that makes the user feel invested in revealing that number.

Sidenote: The only qualm I have about this use of scroll-driven animations for the donation tally is that it might be too subtle — if the user scrolls too quickly, the animated entrance of each digit might occur too quickly to notice. Therefore, the donation tally could be a good case study for comparing the merits of scroll-driven versus scroll-triggered animations as design options. However, that story will have to wait for another day, as the focus of this article is only to show that the staggered scrolling CSS animations can be greatly simplified.

In the video below, my eight-year-old narrates, as a reminder that simplicity is key to the web we’re choosing to leave to the next generation. He compares the complex math in the original demo source code with my animation-range-start fork. He shows the two versions working side by side, demonstrating that sometimes the only thing we lose by judiciously deleting complex code is a headache.

And for those using a Chromium-based browser, my fork of the Pen is below. You can peruse the CSS to see how it does away with the grand unified theory of staggered scrolling animations, and instead deals only with the problem at hand. Since I preserved the original functionality, you’ll need to scroll gradually to appreciate the staggered animations.

I once tackled a related problem with a mathematical formula in a previous article, which touched on how we can use sibling-index() to “increment animation-range-start to stagger the delay before characters fade in as the user scrolls.”

The truth is, if we refuse to fall into the coding trap of possibly abstracting the wrong thing, we can use one line of CSS to replace a formula that took 1.3k words in the Frontend Masters article for me to still not fully grasp:

The 2.5% increment for each sibling is because there are eight characters to stagger, and I wanted to choreograph the animations such that they are delayed in evenly-spaced increments till the last character makes its animated entrance when it reaches 30% of the containment range (10% + 20%).

It was inadvertently apt to describe the original post as “CSS on drugs.” Once you reinvent one built-in CSS feature, you can’t stop there. You find yourself having to recreate more stuff that CSS should be able to do by itself. Sure enough, even after we get through the custom formulas that make scroll timelines do what they already do, Ashutosh announces, “Unfortunately, we can’t use easing keywords or cubic-bezier() or linear() functions here. We are left on our own to create easing functions.”

We shouldn’t have to because, despite the word “timing” in the name of the animation-timing-function property, MDN is clear that “animation-timing-function has the same effect when creating CSS scroll-driven animations as it does for regular time-based animations.” I know from experience that scroll-driven animations work with animation-timing-function because I used scroll-driven animations with steps() to animate a sprite climbing a ladder in my CSS scrollytelling game.

The problem in the land of CSS on drugs is that after merging all the staggered animations into one, the easing applies to the entire master timeline rather than to each element. It’s not a flaw in CSS, but a logical consequence of the “holistic effect driven by one progress value.” Be careful what you wish for.

To be fair, over the years I have seen impressive results from developers reinventing things the browsers already do, usually because the reinvention gives us control we couldn’t obtain otherwise.

I mention this to show that reinventing something like scroll timelines isn’t an inherently bad idea if we know why we’re doing it. After all, scroll timelines are only a reinvention of what developers were doing for decades prior in JavaScript. Having said that, I believe my rewrite of the staggered scroll-driven tally demo shows that the original approach was overkill for that example. Do let me know in the comments if you see any advantage to the custom CSS functions version over using animation-range-start.

And yet the thought that stuck with me about the Frontend Masters post is that it opens the door to linking the progress of a CSS animation to any arbitrary input value that could be used in a conceptually similar way to scroll timelines.

This really appeals to me, because even when I first started writing about scroll timelines in 2024, I mentioned that “I would love to see other input controls that can manipulate animation timelines. Imagine text fields progressing animations as you fill them out.” Or how about an animation driven by how closely the new password you are typing matches a password scheme?

Sidenote: Adam Argyle’s Prop For That library, which Geoff recently wrote up, makes it more convenient than ever to expose validation information about a field via CSS custom properties.

I can add my hypothetical karaoke machine scenario to the wishlist of custom progress inputs for controlling animations. How about an animation driven by the user’s progress through a quiz or a shopping cart?

Custom animation timelines would open the door to all sorts of engaging user experiences—but the code Ashutosh has shared may be the closest we are likely to get to driving CSS animations using values other than time or scroll progress.

Let me know in the comments if you can think of other types of progress inputs that could drive CSS animations to create immersive, interactive experiences.

CSS-Tricks editor Geoff Graham sits down with author Lee Meyer to demo Google’s Prompt API and weigh it (good and bad) place in front-end development.

Andy Clarke shows you how to create designs that people recognize and remember in his brand-new e-book, Standout Designs for the Web. Order it today!

Staggering with animation-range-start is neat because each element can reveal on its own scroll offset without JavaScript or IntersectionObserver setup. One thing I’d consider is wrapping it in prefers-reduced-motion so sensitive users aren’t hit with moving content. Did you test fallbacks for browsers lacking support?

You are right, George, in a real production version it should definitely check for both prefers reduced motion and whether view timeline is supported. For what it’s worth this stuff is newly baseline since Firefox finally supports it. The other browsers have supported it for a while

There is a polyfill for older browsers https://github.com/flackr/scroll-timeline which does work quite well from my testing, although it can’t provide the performance improvements of native scroll timeline so it can get janky and it has some other limitations such as timeline scope not working the same

It is still better than nothing and uses the native version where possible

#10161. When Everything Has Changed, What Do We Do Next?

It’s been a… year, huh? I think the most common emoji I’ve seen this year is “Face with Bags Under Eyes” (🫩) and that says a lot. One thing is for sure: the industry we make a living in is in flux, regardless of the outcomes of the AI bubble. Let’s take a look at how we can try and navigate that situation as best we can. But first…

Technology hyperscalers like Microsoft and Meta, along with AI startups like Open AI and Anthropic have transformed everything about tech and the job we do. This is true regardless of if they fail or somehow succeed in their quest to have everyone paying a subscription fee for their Large Language Model (LLM) technology, which is marketed to us as AI. I’ll refer to this technology as AI for the rest of this article for simplicity.

It’s not just tech jobs that have transformed either. Everywhere you go, you will see some slop, somewhere. Be it a flyer, website, a village poster or a food truck menu. Heck, people are even using LLMs to work out what’s special about their own children. The effects of this technology are far-reaching, way beyond our own industry.

The technology has hit us all in tech especially hard though. Mid-last year, I did some investigating because I suspected that so-called AI mandates were becoming increasingly popular and what I learned from conversations then feels like some sort of utopia compared to now. In the space between that piece being published and now, we went from token maxxing to companies realizing that actually, this technology is extremely expensive and not returning the promised outcomes.

The mandates have cooled, sure, but the demands to use AI to produce more volume, regardless of whether that volume is actually needed or not are still very much there. I’m in a fortunate position to have friendships with people all across the industry, from tech hyperscalers, to tiny little studios, and the stories I have heard from their work life are nothing short of horrifying. No one really seems to be happy with their work either.

This is such a long way away from even a handful of years ago where yes, there was a lot of problematic culture and outcomes from the tech industry, but people were generally quite happy with the work they were doing at least.

The sudden urge to sprint towards AI technologies over the last few years has had a devastating effect in so many places. The technology has encouraged suicide, devastated communities with the data center build-out and of course, displaced thousands of people’s careers. It’s worth noting that I’ve mentioned only a tiny sample of the harms there. If I listed them all, this article would be unbearably long and depressing.

Some harms I do want to touch on though are the effects on our communities in tech. The community spirit of open source and open knowledge was the foundation of the industry’s — especially the web industry’s — rapid success. At the root of everything there was people happy to share knowledge and build everyone up with them. Those people still do exist — myself included — but being one of those people is increasingly difficult.

As the numbers go up in the AI world, the numbers very much go down for independent educators, publishers and community conferences. There’s a developing and worrying culture of “I don’t need to skill up now because I can use AI for work”, so allow me to break down why that reductive view of your work and your career is harmful in the long term.

And don’t ever forget that! Let’s have a think for a second about what actually makes you a good developer, because typing characters in a text editor certainly isn’t it. What makes us good is experience, knowledge, nuance and above all else, creativity.

Look, I get it, AI really can feel like it’s a miracle machine, but it’s important to take a wider view. Along with the published studies on cognitive decline and the widening skills gap, it’s more important than ever to invest in your brain to keep your skills sharp because you’re not getting that repetitive reinforcement that writing code every day gives you anymore if AI tools do everything for you. There’s a reason that I often build most of my teaching around a linear build-along because the tangible nature of repeating patterns you’ve learned really helps that knowledge to sink in.

As always, this article is addressed to everyone, but I want to talk directly to founders (like me), decision makers, and managers for a second. A big part of our job, in fact, the most important part, is to ensure that our workers are looked after. I’m not just talking about compensation and perks here. I’m talking about our responsibility to ensure that our workers’ skills are kept sharp, their professional curiosities are satisfied and above all else that they are happy in the work they are doing. Our workers are much more than “number go up” vehicles.

You should be giving your developers more time to learn, rather than chasing endless volume. If AI enthusiasts are to be believed, there’s hours upon hours of time efficiencies created by this technology. Don’t fill that time with more volume. Instead, invest that time in developing your worker’s skills. It’s going to avoid burnout which yep, daily LLM use will likely cause in the long term.

One thing that is increasingly clear is that the health of the AI industry is not good. The bubble has already effectively burst in China and more worryingly for economy enjoyers, the “picks and shovels” — AKA the chips industry in Korea — has seen dramatic downturns. This is not how an “inevitable” outcome is supposed to happen!

The U.S. AI industry is on very shaky ground too. Circular financing, unbelievable levels of debt and a data center build out that simply cannot keep up with the required power levels to turn profits for hyperscalers and startups, mixed with a genuine hatred of the technology by the general public, creates enough risk in itself. Recent events, such as the Democrats dominating the midterms causing AI stocks to drop and as well as Nvidia’s stock because of its CEO being caught up in a spying scandal shine a light on the fragility of the industry. A healthy industry doesn’t work like this.

With all that in mind, what would be a really silly thing to do? Yeh, you guessed it: put AI at the heart of everything in your business. Sure, there’s open source models — which I think is the logical destination of the technology — but how many companies are going to be willing to maintain their infrastructure needs? Not many. The low barrier of entry of commercial AI technology is the selling point, let’s be honest.

Above everything, people need to be invested in, not pushed aside by technology like AI. As I mentioned earlier, it’s experience, knowledge, nuance, and creativity that sets humans far apart from AI. That technology does indeed keep evolving, but even the most ardent of AI supporters will concede that the rate of frontier model improvement is very much levelling out.

There’s never been a more important time to keep human skills sharp, so here’s some ideas on how to do that.

This is not just directed at individuals but also at founders, decision makers and managers. Our industry has been historically excellent at investing in skilling people up and we need to bring that back.

Give staff the time and budget to learn, to develop their skills and to engage with the community at conferences. You already know the positive outcomes that brings, so bring it back to your organization. At the absolute worst case, it’ll make the usage of AI in your organization more efficient and when the token subsidies inevitably end, more cost effective.

Again, token subsidies will inevitably end. So, getting thousands of dollars of worth from a $200 a month subscription will be something you’ll look fondly back on. When hallucinations from AI tools are suddenly costing thousands, does it make sense to force your staff to produce as much volume as possible? Of course not.

My advice is to re-evaluate those prior decisions and invest saved costs back into developing your workers instead, while there’s still a hope of them not burning out completely. Make sure you include your workers in these decisions and discuss the impacts of mandates on them. It’s important for you to understand the impact of these sort of decisions.

This advice is more aimed at developers, but stay paying attention, managers. As I said earlier, repetition (especially typing) reinforces new learning and existing knowledge. The slower pace also allows your brain to think, which from a quality of output perspective, is imperative. Doing none of that atrophies skills over time.

I recently read Jenn Schiffer’s great post. She did the Weird Web October challenge and wrote this:

[…] i encourage anyone who uses ai and/or vibe codes to consider doing an exercise like this without those tools as a way to challenge your brain and you can continue doing your regular work the way you do your regular work

I think that’s a very good idea, even if it’s messing around with a little side project — if you’ve got the energy from Producing Value all day, of course. Guess what bosses: this is another way you can make your staff happier, by giving them time to do this on the clock.

At the end of the day, you’ll miss the soul when it’s gone. It’s not gone yet though. Far from it. Independents like CSS-Tricks, my publication, Piccalilli, and Smashing Magazine are trying hard to keep the soul of our industry alive by sharing high quality educational content with the community.

Most of this content is free at the point of entry, but it certainly isn’t free to produce. We all pay writers fairly to share their knowledge with the community and we are all tiny organizations. We’re certainly not in this game to make huge profits, that’s for sure.

All of us could do more though, but funding is the ultimate barrier. For both companies and individuals, purchase courses, join the membership programs, and purchase ad space. With the right financial support, publications like ours can be a real force in pushing the good stuff back into our industry and bringing everyone up with us. That’s what we do it for.

Some course producers I recommend are Miriam, Sara, Mat, Scott, Brad and Ian, Josh, Ahmad, Christine, Vitaly, Ben and of course, myself! All of these will have generous Black Friday deals, so go ahead and ask your boss to invest in you.

Andy Clarke shows you how to create designs that people recognize and remember in his brand-new e-book, Standout Designs for the Web. Order it today!

It is a tough state of affairs, but if nothing else, at the end of the day we’ve got one of the best and most resilient communities around. Front-end is built on knowledge sharing, and as long as we hold each other up and strive for the future we want to see, we’re in it with a fighting chance. It has been hard, and it’ll continue to be hard, but the challenge is all the more reason to rise to the occasion and persevere.

I seem to be one of those ‘unique’ developers who has managed to avoid using AI entirely so far. I work for a conservation not-for-profit as the only IT guy (there’s only 5 of us in total). We regularly work with legally sensitive information (protected species records) and early on I had a long discussion with my manager about the dangers to information security of using AI, as well as the various ethical and environmental costs of the technology. My viewpoint is that AI is a tool and, like any other, the decision to use it needs to be made after carefully considering the pros and cons. At the moment, the cons seem to massively outweigh the pros and I see no sign of that changing in the near future; though I may well be wrong – I’m not an expert on machine learning after all.

Despite being ‘lucky’ in regards to management supporting my decision to abstain from AI use, I’m still not immune to the depression and burnout that this new technology has brought. I spend 50% more of my time patching websites than I did 2 years ago, and trying to find packages to use in projects is far more time consuming that it used to be due to all of the noise created by slop. Frankly, it’s sucked all the joy out of my work, and I’m just on the periphery.

I think your emphasis on the dangers of not learning anymore is a serious hazard. I started writing a book on Building Websites for Beginners some years ago, but have since neglected it due to health issues and house moves etc. Maybe taking up writing again is a way to alleviate the sense of hopelessness we’re all feeling?

This really resonated — especially the “build stuff by hand” part. I run a small tool site that I built by hand, and doing it the slow way is exactly how I learned how typography and fonts actually work. AI tools are fast, but they never teach you that. The point about supporting independents hit home too — small sites like ours are the ones keeping the web human. Thanks for saying it out loud.

Great article, your viewpoint on AI always has me hooked. I feel like you’re being slowly abandoned by your peers within the community, whether they want to or not. Look up any of the (my own opinion, granted) core community sites and they’re infested with AI content, articles and functionality. A lot of the leading voices and companies in the front-end dev community, who were once idealistic and morally guided, work for some of those companies you mention and are now mouthpieces for the tech. Or a peddling the tech themselves.

Just like the AI industry, I’m not sure what the future looks like for the web community. But it’s encouraging to see sites like this come back and continue to inform and educate.

It’s the reason why I brought my indie newsletter WDRL back to life this week. It’s important that we play with new technology on our own, that we explore the details, the hacks, the pitfalls of web development and share them on blogs. If we solely(!) rely on AI for everything, that’s going to backfire soon. Let’s revive the old community style!

I was SO happy to see your newsletter return!

Amazing and well written article. I agree with all the points you said, specially the burnout one. As a webdesigner I feel the pressure to deliver as fast as a machine and is getting me depressed and tired. Also I don’t find happiness in “validating” or “patching” AI slop code :(. This week I had to see a javascript code from Claude code that was was: let kids (was the name of the variable), that had nothing to do with schools or toys or whatever the heck the AI had in mind. I’m tired of being AI’s code babysitter, I’d rather write the code myself. Thank you for all the hard work you put on this website. Greetings from Portugal (hence my grammar errors :D )

I’ve never really left a comment on CSS-Tricks, so this is my first to say I really appreciated this article and your thoughts on it. It’s been a very tired few years, lay-off laden and hype driven. And it’s nice to see someone caring about the learning, as that’s what drove me into writing code and doing mathematics in the first place. It’s a good feeling to see someone understanding that, in a field that seems to care less and less about understanding itself–hard to think of it as a “science” these days if we do not learn.

If we ignore that AI is one of several threats to kill us all (apart from climate catastrophes, a possibly nuclear world war—famines and generally decreasing living standards in consequence—, the disintegration of our societies with widespread civil unrest, and other increasingly probable threats), then I believe one way of looking at AI is that professionally, we’re in the unfortunate situation that it uproots what we’ve learned and invested in.

It’s probably normal for many (anyone in the field for 3+ years) to feel uncomfortable and unhappy about this effect of AI—especially in a world that says “everyone on their own” and knows out-groups that can be neglected or worse. But no matter how normal, the discomfort and the unhappiness (and perhaps even anger) aren’t going to help anyone, and the sooner we get out of that zone, the better it is for us individually and collectively. That seems especially true because we have important problems to address with AI, problems “just don’t use it” won’t solve (it won’t—AI isn’t going anywhere).

In it with everyone else here, I’m betting on sucking it up, adapting, and doing one’s part to push this in a constructive direction. This is not meaning to give up—it’s pragmatism and redirection of energy, because the ride has only just begun. What’s to come probably needs all our attention and energy.

(What do I know though;—and with the best wishes to this great part of our community!)

#10159. Hackers help themselves to 1Password's source code and customer data

An intruder planted malicious code in backend services, but the biz insists passwords are safe

1Password is reassuring customers that their vaults remain secure after an attacker breached its systems and stole account information and private source code.

The Canadian security biz disclosed this week that an attacker had compromised an employee's corporate GitHub account, made off with private source code, and deployed malicious code to its backend services to siphon off customer information.

1Password, which began investigating the intrusion on November 19, hasn't said how many customers were affected, but confirmed that the stolen data includes names and email addresses used to sign in to the service, account identifiers, timestamps showing when users created and last accessed their accounts, and various pieces of non-secret account metadata.

The company insists the information that customers actually pay it to protect was beyond the intruder's reach. "At no time were customer vault contents accessed by, or available to, the attacker," 1Password said.

Arguably more troubling is the revelation that the intruder gained access to 1Password's continuous integration and continuous delivery (CI/CD) infrastructure, which houses credentials used to keep its products running. The company says it has rotated or revoked potentially exposed credentials and that neither its apps nor its browser extensions were affected.

1Password hasn't explained how access to an employee's GitHub account enabled the attacker to deploy malicious code to its backend services, nor has it detailed the extent of the attacker's access to its software build and distribution infrastructure. The company did not respond to The Register's questions.

The miscreant also got their hands on tokens embedded in emails sent to some customers as part of account invitations, provisioning, and recovery processes. These tokens aren't enough to access an account on their own, according to 1Password, as users must also authenticate via single sign-on or enter a verification code sent to their registered email address.

The company says it specifically notified the small number of users with affected provisioning or recovery processes underway. It found no internal evidence of account takeovers using the exposed provisioning tokens.

Still, the breach has prompted the password management biz to beef up those verification codes, replacing the six-digit numeric variety with ten-character alphanumeric codes to make brute-forcing them considerably more difficult.

1Password has booted the intruder out of its GitHub environment, revoked the stolen token, and rolled back the affected backend services to clean versions of the code. It's since shored up security around its development infrastructure and employees' devices, and brought in outside security specialists to review its handiwork.

The incident recalls the first stage of LastPass's 2022 breach, when an attacker compromised its development environment and stole source code. Information obtained in that intrusion helped enable a second attack that exposed customer vault backups, including encrypted passwords.

The UK Information Commission's Office slapped LastPass with a £1.2 million ($1.6 million) fine last December over the incident, which compromised the personal information of up to 1.6 million UK users. Among the regulator's gripes was that LastPass had fallen short of the higher standard of security expected of a company entrusted with people's passwords.

Customers aren't being asked to change their account passwords or Secret Keys, neither of which 1Password stores, although the company is warning users to be on the lookout for phishing attempts.

A list of email addresses belonging to people known to use a particular password manager is a handy thing for a phisher to have, especially when said password manager has just given them a plausible pretext for getting in touch. ®

#10158. scroll-state()

The CSS scroll-state() query, surprisingly, has nothing to do with whether you’re doom-scrolling in a state of existential dread. It does, however, allow you to change the CSS used based on scrolling behavior. Far from doom, that’s pretty neat-o speedo. (Okay, you’re right, there’s nothing neat-o about a speedo). You can do lots of things that used to require JavaScript, like revealing the navigation when a user scrolls up or animating in a back to top link once the user scrolls down.

While scroll-state(), with those slimming parentheses, looks like a function, it’s not technically one. It’s a container query (@container) like style(). Since scroll-state() is a query, the website is basically asking the browser whether a particular feature of a container has a specific value. If it does, the styles within the brackets will apply. If it doesn’t have that feature at all or has the feature, but the value is different, the @container query is false and the styles in the brackets will not apply.

In the following demo (which we’ll dissect in a bit), a “Back to Top” link slides in from the bottom as you scroll down the page.

scroll-state() has four possible features and each of those has a variety of possible values.

Asks if a container has been scrolled in a given direction:

Asks if a container can be scrolled in a given direction:

Asks if a sticky-positioned element is stuck:

Asks if a snap target has been snapped on a particular axis:

First let’s look at the scrolled feature. This is the demo we saw at the beginning where we animate a back-to-top link when we scroll down:

When the scroll container is scrolled toward the block-end (downward in this case) the translate applies and the .toplink element animates up into the visible area. Because we’re using a transition, when we scroll a different direction (up and down/block-start and block-end are the only options here), it nicely animates out.

While the scrolling direction in the demo above is pretty clear, what if this page was translated into a different language? Or we had a hero image that covered the entire viewport? One way we could indicate the scroll direction would be to have an arrow or other signifier animating and pointing in the correct direction. (I’m more a believer in constructing your page such that scroll direction is pretty obvious; however, I can foresee situations where an indicator was the best user experience or where translations/writing-mode changes could obscure what scrolling direction was appropriate.)

One nice thing about the scrollable feature is that when you scroll to the end of the scrollable area, it shuts off. So when we scroll to the block-end of the page (bottom of the page in this instance) scrollable: block-end is no longer true and our scroll indicator will revert to its default (in this case, display: none).

You could also combine the scrollable and scrolled features to remove the scroll indicator once the container has been scrolled either in the block-start or block-end direction. (We need both, because if we’re down in the middle of the page and we scroll up, scrolled: block-end will no longer be true and the scroll indicator will appear again.)

Another way we could enhance our demo is to have our navigation stick at the top while we’re scrolling down and change the look of the navigation once it’s stuck. The stuck feature must be on the element that gets stuck (as opposed to the scroll container, as was the case above). Also, because the container is set on the element that gets stuck, you cannot select the element itself from within the container query. You’ll need to position: sticky a wrapper element and “demote” all styles you want to change to the child.

The work of setting up the stuck feature happens in that .header-wrapper, but since we can’t apply styles to the container from within the container (not even with :has()). We need most of the styling to be in the

element.

Changing the size of an element in the header that affects the size of the header can lead to conflicting results. i.e. you scroll to a position where the header is stuck, which initiates the size change, but that reduces the size, which pulls it away from the stuck point, which cancels the size change which makes it stuck again, which initiates the size change and round and round we go. Even a small change can make it impossible to even scroll past the stuck point. Adding a transition can help with this and make scrolling work again, but you can still scroll to a place where the resizing happens in a loop. In the following demo, if you scroll down to a pixel or so past where the header touches the top of the viewport, you should see the stutter step.

There is one weird trick to fix this, though. No, really, there is! (shakes fist at the internet for making “one weird trick” an unbelievable phrase.) The one weird trick is to set a height on the element that’s getting stuck (.header-wrapper in the case above). Ideally, set it smaller than the final size of the header if you’re making the header smaller. This prevents the element from resizing because of a change in its content’s size. This does require the header content to overflow. If that’s a no-go for you, you’ll have to change its size with transformations. The transformed element has a sort of placeholder that stays in the DOM where the transformed element originally lived. This placeholder is what’s used to determine the sticky position and does not change based on what happened in the animation.

We can also add some styles when scroll snapping has been applied and that scroll has snappity-snapped (technical term). Similar to the stuck feature, snapped should be defined on the element that is snapped, not on the scroll container. If scroll snapping has not been applied to the element, then the snapped feature returns false and the container query does not apply.

First, we set up our scroll snapping in the scrolled container.

Note: As with most scrolling CSS (scrolling animations, etc.), if you’re using the document’s default scroll, the element is what’s being scrolled, not the .

The slide

s are what we’re going to snap to the size of the viewport:

As said above, the container type and name go on the snapped item. And then we modify descendants of the slide once it snaps:

Again, as with stuck, you cannot apply styles directly to the snapped element/the container, but only to the children of that element/container.
